**Summary.** The authors propose using a single quantum emitter coupled to a waveguide with coherent time-delayed feedback to simulate complex, nonreciprocal many-body spin models. This method effectively creates a scalable, cascaded spin chain whose dynamics can be read out by measuring the emitter and output field. The work successfully demonstrates access to exotic nonreciprocal phenomena, proving the concept's feasibility for experimental quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally realizable quantum optics platform (single emitter + waveguide) to study fundamental many-body physics concepts like nonreciprocity and topological effects (LSE) that are usually studied in solid-state or cold atom arrays.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently simulating nonreciprocal many-body spin models, especially in regimes where excitations propagate unidirectionally.

**Main result.** They demonstrate a scalable scheme using coherent time-delayed feedback on a single quantum emitter to realize and probe nonreciprocal physics, exhibiting features like the Liouvillian skin effect.

**Method.** The simulation maps the physical system onto an effective cascaded spin chain, allowing access to steady-state and quench dynamics via measurement of the emitter and output field.

**Model / system.** The platform is a single quantum emitter coupled to a 1D waveguide mode. Nonreciprocity is engineered using coherent time-delayed feedback ($	au$), creating an effective interaction where the emitter affects itself at later times.

**Key observables.** Steady-state quantities, two-time correlation functions, local spin density profiles ($\langle \sigma^z_i angle_{ss}$), and output photon correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The delay time $	au$, the coupling strength $\kappa$, and the loss/dissipation rates ($\gamma_L, \eta$). The maximum simulable length is limited by the ratio $\kappa/\gamma_L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The scheme assumes the feedback delay $	au$ is large enough for steady-state access, and the reset procedure must be much faster than the interaction time scale.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (QE with feedback), the mapping to cascaded spin chains, and the evolution of local observables showing signatures of nonreciprocity like the LSE.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical setup and the effective Hamiltonian, details the simulation methods for steady-state and quench dynamics, analyzes key nonreciprocal signatures (LSE, correlation decay), and concludes with experimental feasibility and limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a scheme to efficiently simulate nonreciprocal many-body spin models using a non-Markovian open system. Our scheme utilizes a single quantum emitter coupled to a waveguide mode that is fed back to the same emitter after a time delay. With this coherent delayed feedback, an effective nonreciprocal interaction can be engineered, wherein the emitter at earlier times affects itself at later times, creating a scalable spin chain. We show that all the steady-state quantities of such spin models can be accessed through measurement of the emitter and the output field. Furthermore, using classical feedback that resets the emitter, quench dynamics from arbitrary product states can be simulated. We demonstrate striking features of nonreciprocal models, such as the Liouvillian skin effect, anomalous relaxation dynamics, and quasi-long-range order of output photons. Finally, we show that the proposal is amenable to experimental realization and robust to imperfections. Our work establishes a feasible way to scale up a nonreciprocal many-body system, and provides a new route towards generation of exotic states of light.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies the rate operator formalism to analyze the open Bose-Hubbard model, providing a systematic way to model non-Hermitian quantum evolution. By using the Rate Operator Quantum Jump method, the authors successfully engineer effective Hamiltonians that predict steady-state phases and transitions, even in regimes where traditional simulation techniques fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, systematic tool (ROQJ) to analyze open quantum dynamics, which is crucial for understanding realistic physical systems where environmental coupling dictates the final state and dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the engineering of non-Hermitian dynamics and the characterization of phase structure in open quantum systems.

**Main result.** The rate operator formalism successfully constructs effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonians that reveal unique steady states, even when standard methods fail, and allows for the controlled transition between distinct steady-state phases.

**Method.** The core method is the Rate Operator Quantum Jump (ROQJ) formalism, which systematically unravels the master equation into stochastic pure-state trajectories.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Bose-Hubbard model subjected to environmental dephasing, modeled via a master equation (GKSL equation). The analysis compares local and non-local dephasing schemes.

**Key observables.** Populations ($ho_{N00, N00}$), absolute squares of amplitudes ($|\alpha|^2$), the imaginary part of the leading eigenvalue ($	ext{Im}(\lambda_1)$), and the expectation value of the energy ($\langle H/\gamma angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), dephasing rate parameters ($k$ or $\gamma$), and the non-Hermiticity control parameter introduced via the rate operator.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the rate operator $R_\psi$ is positive semi-definite for independent realizations, and it compares results derived from the ROQJ method against standard Monte Carlo wavefunction methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures map key steady-state diagnostics ($	ext{Im}(\lambda_1)$ and $\langle H/\gamma angle$) across parameter planes for both local and non-local dephasing schemes, demonstrating parameter-dependent phase boundaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the rate operator formalism, applies it to the Bose-Hubbard model, details the dynamical evolution using ROQJ, and finally characterizes the resulting steady-state structure and phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Hermitian evolution can be realized through post-selection on stochastic pure-state trajectories arising in continuously monitored open quantum systems. The rate operator formalism provides a versatile and systematic framework for unraveling a master equation into stochastic pure-state evolutions, offering enhanced control over the resulting non-Hermitian dynamics. In the present work, we explore the applicability of the rate operator formalism as a tool for engineering non-Hermitian dynamics. Specifically, we apply this approach to the Bose-Hubbard model subject to environmental dephasing, examining its consequences for controlled state manipulation. Our analysis is framed within the broader contexts of quantum state engineering and measurement-induced phase transitions. We demonstrate that the rate operator formalism enables the construction of effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonians exhibiting a unique steady state-even in regimes where the standard Monte Carlo wavefunction method fails to produce one. Furthermore, we show that this framework facilitates transitions between distinct steady-state phases, governed by tunable parameters such as the interaction strength and a non-Hermiticity control parameter introduced via the rate operator formalism.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops and experimentally tests an information engine using active bristle bots to convert information into mechanical work. The study finds that the work output deviates significantly from predictions based on ideal thermal systems. Crucially, the optimal operating regime switches based on particle density, a feature confirmed even when considering realistic friction and finite-time cycle dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work done here on active matter thermodynamics and non-equilibrium work extraction provides a physical realization of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics far from equilibrium, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and energy transduction.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates information engines that extract work from active matter, comparing their performance to theoretical thermal systems.

**Main result.** The active engine exhibits a qualitatively modified work-information relationship compared to thermal systems, and the optimal operating regime switches depending on particle density.

**Method.** The work is calculated by analyzing the mean work extracted ($ar{W} = p_1 W_{st}$) based on the probability of finding an empty detection region ($p_1$) during a piston-like cycle.

**Model / system.** The system uses self-propelled bristle bots (active matter) confined in a box, where work is extracted by periodically measuring the occupancy of a movable detection region near a piston.

**Key observables.** Mean work per measurement ($ar{W}$), empty-region probability ($p_1$), and maximum power output ($	ilde{P}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle density ($\Phi$), detection region size ($\Delta x$), and the energy scale ($	ilde{\alpha}$ vs. $k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the active particle force scales linearly with density ($\alpha \Phi$), and later models incorporate dry friction and finite-time cycle constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, pressure scaling ($P \propto \Phi$), comparisons of $ar{W}$ vs $p_1$ showing regime switches, and simulations of stick-slip dynamics under friction.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the information engine concept and measuring work in active matter, to comparing results against ideal gas theory, analyzing the density-dependent switching of optimal operation, and finally modeling the cycle under realistic, non-ideal friction constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Information engines convert information into extractable work through measurements and feedback. We realize an engine that employs information to compress a gas of self-propelled bristle bots. Its work per measurement, $\bar{W}$, is controlled by the size of the detection region $Δx$ and the probability $p_1$ that this region is vacant. In thermal systems, $\bar{W}$ follows the universal form $-p_1 \ln p_1$, whereas the active engine shows a qualitatively modified relation. As particle density increases, the maximum of $\bar{W}(Δx)$ switches between two distinct operating regimes. Notably, this transition is also found in the solutions that maximize power output for finite-time cycles with dynamics affected by dry friction. We attribute this nonequilibrium feature to the accumulation of active particles near the boundaries.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces advanced theoretical techniques, pcst++ and deepCST, to calculate effective quasiparticle-conserving Lindbladians for open quantum many-body systems in the thermodynamic limit. By applying these methods to the dissipative transverse-field Ising model, the authors demonstrate how to systematically handle dissipation while preserving the quasiparticle structure. This establishes a versatile tool for studying non-equilibrium steady states in complex quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful, generalizable theoretical tools (pcst++/deepCST) for analyzing non-equilibrium steady states in interacting quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding open quantum dynamics in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a systematic framework to construct effective quasiparticle-conserving Lindbladian operators directly in the thermodynamic limit for open quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed and applied two complementary methods, pcst++ and deepCST, demonstrating they can derive effective Lindbladians that preserve quasiparticle number in the thermodynamic limit.

**Method.** The core methodology involves extending continuous similarity transformations (CSTs) from Hermitian Hamiltonians to non-Hermitian Lindbladians, enabling block diagonalization with respect to the quasiparticle number.

**Model / system.** The study uses the dissipative transverse-field Ising chain (DTFIM) with local dissipation as a paradigmatic model. The system is analyzed by purifying the Lindbladian and mapping it to a quasiparticle basis.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the eigenvalues ($\lambda$) of the Lindbladian, from which the decay rates (inverse lifetimes) of elementary excitations are extracted. The Liouvillian gap is also a key diagnostic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the thermodynamic limit and treats the Ising interaction ($J$) as a small perturbation in the low-Ising regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods assume that the continuous similarity transformations can be systematically applied to the non-Hermitian Lindbladian structure, and the analysis relies on perturbative expansions (linked-cluster/flow equations).

**Figures summary.** Figure 8 compares the calculated inverse lifetime of the longest-living state in the 2-qp sector as a function of the coupling $J$ using both pcst++ (solid lines) and deepCST (scatter plots) expansions, showing convergence up to $J \approx 0.4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the two complementary theoretical frameworks (pcst++ and deepCST), applies these methods to the DTFIM, and analyzes the resulting effective Lindbladian structure and its spectral properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Open quantum many-body systems are commonly described by Lindblad master equations, yet the treatment of Lindbladian operators in the thermodynamic limit remains a major challenge. We develop a framework for constructing effective quasiparticle-conserving Lindbladian operators directly in the thermodynamic limit. Our approach extends continuous similarity transformations to non-Hermitian open quantum systems and enables the systematic block diagonalization of Lindbladians with respect to the quasiparticle (qp) number. We formulate two complementary methods. The first, projective continuous similarity transformations (pcst++), generalizes perturbative continuous unitary transformations to Lindblad operators so that a linked-cluster expansion allows us to obtain high-order series expansions of the infinite system. The second, deepCST, extends directly evaluated enhanced perturbative continuous unitary transformations by combining the same qp-conserving generator with a perturbative truncation scheme that yields non-perturbative effective Lindbladian operators directly in thermodynamic limit. We apply pcst++ and deepCST to the dissipative transverse-field Ising chain with local dissipation. We focus on the low-Ising regime. We purify the Lindbladian by splitting each spin into two sites. A spin flip then corresponds to two qps. We derive and analyze the effective, qp-conserving Lindbladian in the sectors with zero to two qps. We show how the Ising interaction renormalizes the decay rates of elementary spin-flip excitations and provides the microscopic mechanism for a competition between coherent interactions and dissipation. Our work establishes perturbative and non-perturbative continuous similarity transformations as a versatile tool for deriving effective qp pictures of open quantum many-body systems in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QArray+, an advanced simulator for quantum dot arrays designed to overcome limitations in current models that assume steady-state physics. By incorporating gate-dependent tunnel coupling and a full quantum open-system description, it can accurately model non-equilibrium dynamics crucial for quantum device operation. The implementation leverages GPU acceleration to achieve high-throughput simulation of complex charge stability diagrams.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a sophisticated, scalable tool for simulating open quantum systems in solid-state architectures, bridging the gap between idealized equilibrium models and realistic, time-dependent quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing simulators for quantum dot arrays fail in non-equilibrium regimes because they rely on simplified models assuming instantaneous steady-state relaxation.

**Main result.** QArray+ successfully unifies the simulation of coherent interdot hybridization and non-equilibrium latching dynamics, achieving high computational efficiency on GPU hardware.

**Method.** The authors developed QArray+, which extends the QArray framework by incorporating gate-dependent tunnel coupling and a quantum open-system description using a Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is semiconductor quantum-dot arrays, simulated using models ranging from stochastic Markov jump processes to the Lindblad master equation, allowing for the calculation of Charge Stability Diagrams (CSDs).

**Key observables.** Charge Stability Diagrams (CSDs), coherent interdot charge-state hybridization, and non-equilibrium latching dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltages ($\vec{V}_g$), tunnel coupling strengths ($t_{ij}$), and the ratio of measurement rates to tunneling dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stochastic model assumes a single charge transition per time step, and the original models assume relaxation to a steady state much faster than the measurement timescale.

**Figures summary.** Figures show comparisons of CSDs computed using various models (QArray, Lindblad, Hubbard), demonstrating the different physical regimes captured, and Figure 9 shows the scaling of computation time with tunnel-coupling density.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QArray+ to address limitations in existing simulators, details the incorporation of open-system dynamics and gate-dependent coupling, presents the computational implementation in JAX/GPU, and validates the method by comparing results across different physical regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconductor quantum-dot arrays are a compelling platform for scalable quantum technologies, yet their practical operation is hindered by the complexity of tuning large-scale devices. Existing automation tools rely on simplified physical models---such as constant-capacitance approximations and equilibrium Hubbard models---which assume instantaneous relaxation to a steady state. These frameworks fail in experimentally critical regimes where measurement rates exceed tunneling dynamics, necessitating more sophisticated non-equilibrium control strategies. To bridge this gap, we introduce QArray+, an extension of the QArray framework that incorporates gate-dependent tunnel coupling and a quantum open-system description of dissipative processes. This approach enables the unified simulation of coherent interdot charge-state hybridization and the non-equilibrium latching dynamics essential for training robust machine-learning models for automated device operation. Implemented in JAX with GPU acceleration, QArray+ scales across GPUs and multi-node systems. For example, a charge stability diagram for a 100X100 grid of gate voltages over 64 dots can be computed in $\sim0.17\,\mathrm{s}$ on multiple GPUs. Since interdot interactions are short-ranged and the corresponding tuning corrections are local, simulations at these scales capture the physics relevant to even larger devices. These capabilities support high-throughput dataset generation for automated device tuning.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores non-equilibrium physics in systems with memory-dependent interactions, showing how these temporal couplings can induce novel forms of order. Key findings include the stabilization of true long-range order in 1D and the emergence of helical dynamics involving topological defects in 2D. The research establishes engineered temporal interactions as a powerful paradigm for non-equilibrium many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit derivation of unconventional critical exponents ($z=4$) and the demonstration of finite-temperature phase transitions in non-equilibrium settings provide new theoretical tools for understanding complex dynamics beyond equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-equilibrium ordering and topological defects in many-body systems driven by time-delayed self-interactions mediated by a memory-feedback mechanism.

**Main result.** The memory feedback mechanism can stabilize true long-range order at finite temperatures, circumventing theorems like Mermin-Wagner, and generating unique helical vortex dynamics in 2D systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs continuum field theory, spin-wave approximations, and detailed analysis of correlation functions (spatial, temporal, and velocity) across different temperature regimes.

**Model / system.** The core model is an interacting rotor system governed by an equation of motion incorporating short-range and long-range retarded memory forces, applicable in 1D and 2D geometries.

**Key observables.** Correlation functions G(r), D(t), and G_v(r); topological defects (vortices) and domain walls (DWs); dynamical critical exponent z.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The memory kernel decay exponent $\alpha$ (for 1D), the delay time $\tau_c$ (for 2D), and the temperature $T$ relative to interaction strengths $J_1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linearizing the equations of motion for low-temperature regimes and utilizing approximations like the Spin-Wave Approximation (SWA).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decay of correlation functions (algebraic vs. exponential) at varying temperatures, demonstrating the persistence of quasi-long-range order and the helical motion of vortices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by analyzing the 2D model (focusing on vortex dynamics and symmetry breaking) and then analyzing the 1D model (focusing on long-range order and the Mermin-Wagner circumvention), concluding with a comparison of results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate many-body systems with time-delayed self-interactions mediated by a memory-feedback mechanism. We show that such temporal interactions generate non-equilibrium orders and unique topological defects absent in equilibrium-specifically, helical vortices wherein opposite vorticities propagate in reverse directions along domain walls. In one dimension, memory feedback stabilizes true long-range order against weak noise, thereby circumventing the Mermin-Wagner theorem and resulting in an unconventional finite-temperature phase transition with a dynamical exponent $z = 4$. Physical realizations of these memory-driven non-equilibrium systems using active mechatronic metamaterials has also been proposed. These results demonstrate engineered temporal interactions as a powerful paradigm for non-equilibrium many-body physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how non-equilibrium effects perturb the critical behavior of conserved Ising models in active matter. Using Functional Renormalization Group techniques, the authors calculate the scaling dimensions of these perturbations. They find that the two most relevant non-equilibrium modes are nearly degenerate in 3D, indicating extremely slow relaxation dynamics in the system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides rigorous RG techniques to analyze non-equilibrium steady states, which is crucial for understanding driven systems far from thermal equilibrium, a key area of modern condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the nonequilibrium corrections to conserved Ising criticality in scalar active matter, aiming to determine the slowest decaying perturbations away from the passive Wilson-Fisher fixed point.

**Main result.** The two most slowly decaying nonequilibrium modes are found to be nearly degenerate in $d=3$, suggesting unusually large crossover scales, and the analysis confirms the absence of additional contact counterterms at two loops.

**Method.** The study employs Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) calculations, starting from the Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-De Dominicis (MSRJD) action, and utilizes Ward identities to constrain the renormalization flow.

**Model / system.** The system is scalar active matter described by a field $\phi(x, t)$ whose dynamics are out of equilibrium due to field-dependent noise-to-mobility ratios ($\Theta(\phi)$) and gradient activity terms. The passive reference theory is related to conserved Cahn-Hilliard or Model-B dynamics.

**Key observables.** RG eigenvalues characterizing decay rates ($y_{\Theta 1}, y_J, y_{ch}$), and the anomalous dimension $\eta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality $d=3$ (and $d=4-\epsilon$), and the ratio of the two slowest modes separated by the Ising anomalous dimension $\eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a specific functional truncation of the FRG and assumes no further contact anomaly for the relevant operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the perturbation space around the passive WF fixed point in $d=3$. Figure 7 shows numerical convergence checks for the FRG calculation across varying polynomial orders and momentum windows.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the active dynamics via the MSRJD action, applying FRG to find the stability matrix eigenvalues, using Ward identities to enforce conservation laws, and finally performing explicit two-loop calculations to confirm the degeneracy and scaling behavior of the slowest modes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active scalar field theories break detailed balance in two physically distinct ways: through a field-dependent noise-to-mobility ratio $Θ(φ)=D(φ)/M(φ)$, and through the gradient activity of Active Model~B+. We classify the resulting perturbations of the conserved Wilson-Fisher fixed point in three dimensions. Working from the Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-De~Dominicis (MSRJD) action, a functional renormalization-group (FRG) calculation shows that the transport sector has a block-triangular stability matrix whose leading odd mode has $y_{Θ_1}=-Δ_φ$, while quotienting the gradient sector by its detailed-balance-preserving tangent leaves a chemical and a current-like mode. Two smooth regulators give $y_{Θ_1}\simeq-0.52$, $y_J\simeq-0.56$ and $y_{\rm ch}\simeq-0.89$. The translation Ward identity represents the current operator by a stress-tensor divergence modulo chemical gradients; conservation and causality then make the active stability matrix triangular to all loop orders, and an explicit two-loop calculation in $d=4-ε$ finds no additional contact counterterm. The two slowest modes are separated only by the Ising anomalous dimension, $y_J-y_{Θ_1}=-η$. This relation is a dimension count that holds provided neither operator acquires a further contact anomaly, a condition we establish within the present truncation for $\mathcal O_Θ$ and verify through two loops for $\mathcal O_J$; under it the $d=3$ reference Ising value gives $y_{Θ_1}\simeq-0.5181$ and $y_J\simeq-0.5544$. We derive the parity-resolved finite-size scaling of this near degeneracy, identify block observables that separate the two modes, and show that when the current amplitude dominates the crossover scale lies far beyond any accessible system size.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that imposing a topological defect, such as a quantized vortex, can dramatically stabilize a quantum fluid suffering from attractive interactions, preventing rapid collapse. By modeling the system with the GPE and analyzing the Bogoliubov spectrum, the authors show that the vortex's circulation counteracts the collapse, delaying it significantly. The study bridges theoretical predictions of instability with direct experimental measurements in quantum optics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally verifiable platform (quantum fluid of light) to study fundamental many-body physics concepts like collapse dynamics and topological stabilization, which are central to understanding BEC behavior.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-trivial topology, introduced via quantized vortices, dynamically stabilizes a quantum fluid that would otherwise undergo collapse due to attractive inter-particle interactions.

**Main result.** The presence of a vortex significantly delays the collapse time of the Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) by an order of magnitude, stabilizing the fluid into a persistent ring structure.

**Method.** The dynamics are modeled using the Gross-Pitaevskii equation (GPE) and analyzed through Bogoliubov excitation spectrum calculations to predict instability growth rates.

**Model / system.** The system is an analogue of a two-dimensional BEC with large attractive interactions, realized experimentally using a quantum fluid of light propagating in geometry. The dynamics are governed by a GPE-like equation.

**Key observables.** Condensate size ($\sigma_0$), ring radius ($R$), ring width ($w$), collapse time delay ($\hat{	au}_c$), and the growth rate ($\gamma$) of Bogoliubov modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vortex topological charge ($\kappa$), interaction strength ($g$), and the effective time/propagation distance ($\hat{	au}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Neglect of laser absorption (at large detuning) and the assumption that the Bogoliubov spectrum can be treated adiabatically for instability analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric effects of vortices on the condensate ring, the dependence of $R$ and $w$ on $\kappa$, and the comparison between theoretically predicted and numerically extracted Bogoliubov instability growth rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical analogy and the collapse mechanism, then introduces the role of vortices to stabilize the system. It proceeds to analyze the ring dynamics, predict fragmentation via modulation instability using Bogoliubov theory, and finally compares these theoretical predictions with experimental measurements of density and velocity fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The size and shape of a quantum fluid in equilibrium is strongly influenced by the inter-particle interactions. In the attractive interaction regime, atomic quantum fluids ultimately collapse in a violent process that expels most of the particles from the macroscopically occupied state. Here, we use a quantum fluid of light in propagating geometry as an analogue to a two-dimensional Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) with large attractive interactions to show that non-trivial topology significantly alters the dynamical behaviour of the collapse, enhancing the BEC stability and delaying the collapse time by an order of magnitude. We measure direct experimental signatures of topology affecting quantum hydrodynamics, unveiling the inherent competition between attractive nonlinearities, that lead to the collapse, and the preservation of topological charge from a multi-charged vortex. We fully characterise the collapse process in coordinate space and the eventual `solitonification' of the system and connect it to the mode structure of the excitation spectrum.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel technique to stabilize polarization entanglement in long-distance quantum fiber networks by combining wavelength-bracketed probing with optical switching. This approach overcomes the limitations of previous methods by eliminating downtime and changing the acquisition time scaling from the product to the sum of link losses. The method successfully demonstrates continuous, high-fidelity stabilization over extended periods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of scaling quantum communication networks by providing a robust, noise-free, and time-efficient method for polarization stabilization, which is a critical component of quantum networking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Maintaining stable polarization basis is crucial for reliable, long-distance quantum networks distributing polarization entanglement.

**Main result.** The proposed method achieves continuous closed-loop stabilization for over 24 hours without classical reference light, demonstrating a scalable strategy for multi-link quantum feedback.

**Method.** The authors combine wavelength-bracketed probing with switch-enabled path decomposition to decouple the polarization stabilization measurement from the central quantum channel.

**Model / system.** The system is a fibre-based quantum network distributing polarization entanglement in a Dense Wavelength-Division Multiplexing (DWDM) configuration, utilizing entangled-photon sidebands and optical switches.

**Key observables.** Central channel entanglement fidelity (e.g., 95.1%), coincidence-to-accidental ratio (CAR), and the polarization transformation matrix (Stokes vector evolution).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Link lengths (e.g., 133 km), loss rates ($\eta_s, \eta_i$), and the scaling of acquisition time (from product-of-losses to sum-of-losses).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The local reference fibre loss is assumed negligible compared to the network fibre losses, and a first-order approximation is used for the wavelength-bracketing technique.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral allocation of sidebands bracketing the central channel, the switching mechanism using WSS, and the continuous, stable evolution of the Stokes vectors over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing feedback methods (noise from classical light, slow acquisition time scaling). It then introduces the two-stage decoupling strategy: using sidebands for non-interruptive probing, and using switches to decompose the joint measurement into independent single-link measurements, culminating in experimental demonstration and fidelity measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fibre-based quantum networks distributing polarization entanglement require a stable and uninterrupted transmission basis for reliable operation. Bright classical reference light enables rapid polarization feedback but can introduce noise into quantum channels. Entangled-photon-based feedback avoids this noise, but typically interrupts the target entanglement channel during calibration and becomes prohibitively slow over long distances due to the product loss of fibre links. Here, we overcome both limitations by combining wavelength-bracketed probing with switch-enabled path decomposition. Spectrally adjacent entangled-photon sidebands track the polarization response of the central distribution channel without interrupting its transmission, while optical switches and local reference fibres independently determine the signal and idler network transformations. Transferring the resulting compensation settings to the central channel eliminates calibration-induced downtime and changes the acquisition-time scaling from the product of the link losses to the sum of losses. We demonstrate the method on a 133 km fibre testbed and achieve continuous closed-loop stabilization for more than 24 hours without classical reference light. The decomposition of multi-link quantum feedback into single-link measurements provides a scalable stabilization strategy for wavelength-multiplexed quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models disease spread using active matter particles to capture spatial effects missed by traditional epidemic models. It finds a critical protection threshold ($\approx 30\%$) that dictates whether contagion is governed by simple encounter frequency or by the protective mechanisms of the population. The work demonstrates the power of microscopic active-matter simulations for complex non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on epidemiology, the use of active matter models (ABPs) to study non-equilibrium collective dynamics, phase transitions (the crossover), and universal scaling laws in a spatially structured system is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how individual protection levels and population density influence the dynamics of epidemic spreading, moving beyond the limitations of classical well-mixed models.

**Main result.** The contagion dynamics exhibit two distinct regimes separated by a crossover protection level ($\approx 30\%$): below this, scaling is universal ($
u_{max} \propto \phi^{1/2}$), while above it, protection becomes the dominant control mechanism.

**Method.** The research employs an agent-based simulation model using Active Brownian Particles (ABPs) to simulate the spatial spread of infection.

**Model / system.** The system models contagion using ABPs (S, I, P) interacting via a WCA potential in a 2D domain. The dynamics are governed by Langevin equations in the overdamped limit.

**Key observables.** Maximum contagion rate ($
u_{max}$), normalized contagion rate ($I'(	au)/S_0$), and the time to peak contagion ($	au_{max}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Protected-agent fraction ($\chi$), population density (packing fraction $\phi$), and the crossover threshold ($\approx 0.3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the overdamped limit approximation and assumes interactions are governed by the WCA potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of infected agents ($I(	au)$) and the normalized maximum contagion rate ($
u_{max}\phi^{-1/2}$) as a function of the protected fraction ($\chi$) for various densities ($\phi$), clearly showing the crossover.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of classical models, then detailing the ABP framework, and finally analyzing the scaling behavior of the contagion rate across different protection and density regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how individual protection and population density influence epidemic spreading remains a central challenge in epidemiology. While classical compartmental models successfully describe the temporal evolution of epidemics, they do not explicitly account for the microscopic motion and spatial organisation of individuals. Here, we investigate contagion dynamics using an agent-based model of Active Brownian Particles (ABPs), where self-propelled agents interact through local contact and a prescribed fraction of the population is protected. By systematically varying the protected-agent fraction and the population density, we identify two distinct contagion regimes separated by a crossover protection of approximately $30\%$. Below this threshold, the maximum contagion rate follows the universal scaling $ν_{\mathrm{max}}\proptoφ^{1/2}$, indicating that disease transmission is governed primarily by frequency of encounters. Above the threshold, universal scaling is lost and protection becomes the dominant mechanism controlling epidemic spreading, with the strongest suppression occurring in low-density populations. These results demonstrate that microscopic active-matter models provide a powerful framework for investigating epidemic dynamics beyond the assumptions of well-mixed population models and reveal how spatial organisation and individual protection jointly determine contagion dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the minimum work required to control particles immersed in active fluctuating environments. It demonstrates that positional information alone can be used to extract energy from the activity, a finding that surpasses previous requirements for direct measurement of the active noise. The results offer crucial design principles for controlling systems in non-equilibrium active matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides general design principles for information engines and optimal control strategies operating in non-equilibrium active environments, which is relevant to understanding energy transduction in complex biological or synthetic active matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the minimum work required for feedback control of particles subjected to active fluctuations, specifically investigating if work can be extracted using only positional measurements rather than direct measurements of the active degree of freedom.

**Main result.** Positional feedback alone is sufficient to efficiently harvest work from activity due to non-trivial correlations, and this positional feedback remains energetically advantageous even in the limit of infinitely persistent activity.

**Method.** The authors use optimal control theory to derive optimal protocols and calculate the average minimum work input required to move a harmonic trap minimum, comparing results from positional vs. active-fluctuation measurements.

**Model / system.** The model involves an active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particle (AOUP) confined by a moving harmonic trap. The dynamics are governed by overdamped Langevin equations incorporating active fluctuations that break detailed balance.

**Key observables.** Average minimum work input ($\langle W_\alpha[\lambda] angle$), optimal feedback protocols ($\lambda^*(s)$), and the comparison of work extracted using positional vs. active-fluctuation information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Persistence time ($	au$), trap strength ($\kappa$), thermal diffusion ($\sigma$), and active fluctuation strength ($\sigma'$). The analysis examines limits like $	au 	o 0$ and $	au 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the joint stationary distribution is Gaussian. The comparison between different feedback schemes is central to the argument.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the average optimal work ($	ext{Ex}_0[\dots]$) for positional feedback versus active-fluctuation feedback as a function of persistence time ($	au$), showing how the benefit of activity changes with $	au$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and control objective, derives the optimal protocols using variational methods, and then systematically compares the work extracted using positional information versus direct active fluctuation measurements, analyzing the asymptotic limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the minimum-work feedback control of particles subject to active fluctuations. Considering an active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particle confined by a moving harmonic trap, we derive exact optimal protocols following an initial position measurement. Our results show that nonequilibrium correlations between position and active fluctuations allow work extraction from the activity based on positional information only, i.e., without directly measuring the active degree of freedom, which was the focus of earlier literature. We find that depending on the persistence time, activity can either facilitate or impede transport relative to passive systems. Surprisingly, unlike feedback schemes based on direct measurements of the active fluctuations, positional feedback remains energetically advantageous even in the limit of infinitely persistent activity. Our results provide design principles for information engines and optimal control strategies operating in active environments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a stochastic field theory for Branching Stochastic Mechanics incorporating Bohm/Fisher feedback. Using advanced techniques like the MSRJD formalism and 2PI closure, the authors analyze the system's connected fluctuations. They successfully characterize the emergent dynamics by separating it into relative localization and collective propagation modes, defining key saturation velocities and pole structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of advanced field-theoretic techniques (MSRJD, 2PI) to analyze non-equilibrium stochastic dynamics and emergent collective modes is highly relevant to understanding complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a stochastic field theory to analyze the Bohm/Fisher feedback acting on the connected reciprocal sector derived from Branching Stochastic Mechanics (BSM).

**Main result.** The theory successfully separates dynamics into relative localization and collective propagation, yielding two fixed-q pole families: a gapless difference branch and a gapped sum branch.

**Method.** It employs the Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-de Dominicis (MSRJD) formalism and a causal two-loop two-particle-irreducible (2PI) closure to self-consistently determine correlation and response functions.

**Model / system.** The model is based on BSM, a stochastic extension of the Schrödinger–Nagasawa representation, focusing on the connected forward–backward kernel $C_{m FB}$ and the associated BSM density $ho_{m BSM}$.

**Key observables.** The saturated information velocity $c_\star$, the connected correlator matrix $C_{ij}$, and the two fixed-q pole families (gapless difference and gapped sum branches).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative screening length, the saturation velocity $c_\star$, and the gap/pole structure in the frequency domain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the results of Paper I, uses the MSRJD formalism, and involves approximations like neglecting crossed physical correlators in the reduced 2PI system.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention comparisons between BSM-MC curves and $2	ext{PI}$ results, and diagnostic plots like the fixed-$q$ Klein–Gordon diagnostic ($M_{KG}$ vs $Q^2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon Paper I, first establishing the stochastic field theory via MSRJD and 2PI closure. It then analyzes the reduced channels (density/phase) and separates the dynamics into relative (fast) and collective (slow) components, culminating in fixed-point matching conditions for the collective theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Paper I introduced branching stochastic mechanics (BSM) by lifting the Schrödinger-Nagasawa pair to reciprocal forward and backward branching fields. Their centered connected kernel $C_{\rm FB}=\mathbb E_ω[ψ_Fψ_B]$ carries the organized reciprocal sector, where $\mathbb E_ω$ denotes expectation over branching-noise realizations, with $ρ_{\rm BSM}=-C_{\rm FB}(x,x)$ on the anticorrelated branch. Here we develop the stochastic field theory of the Bohm/Fisher feedback that acts on this connected sector. Starting from the multiplicative branching covariance of BSM, a Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-de~Dominicis (MSRJD) formulation and a causal two-loop two-particle-irreducible (2PI) closure are used to determine response and correlation functions self-consistently. The free connected theory exhibits secular growth and ultraviolet accumulation, whereas the dressed theory develops a finite relative screening length. A reduced numerical evolution shows bounded formation of this localized sector, and a self-similar Fisher construction defines the saturated information velocity $c_\star$. A Born-Oppenheimer separation then distinguishes internal relative organization from collective propagation. Restoring the complete frequency structure gives two fixed-$q$ pole families: a gapless difference branch and a gapped sum branch. The infrared velocity of the difference branch approaches $c_\star$ at saturation. The common cone and the projected sum-sector gap are then formulated as additional fixed-point matching conditions for the collective theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally evaluates two passive methods—free-space waveplates and fiber polarization controllers—to compensate for polarization degradation in entangled photons traveling through optical fibers. By optimizing each path independently, the authors successfully restored high-quality entanglement, demonstrating that both free-space and fiber-based control methods are equally effective for short-reach quantum links.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental validation of systematic, passive compensation techniques necessary for building reliable quantum communication links over optical fibers, directly impacting photonic quantum information networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Fiber transmission degrades the polarization correlations of entangled photons, necessitating polarization compensation to restore the desired entangled state for quantum communication.

**Main result.** Both free-space (QHQ) and fiber-based (FPC) passive compensation techniques successfully restore high-quality polarization correlations and entanglement, achieving fidelities above 94.5%.

**Method.** The authors experimentally evaluated two passive compensation methods—QHQ waveplates and FPC—using an independent, path-by-path optimization procedure on the signal and idler paths.

**Model / system.** The system involves distributing polarization-entangled photons through single-mode optical fibers. The polarization transformation is modeled as a unitary operation, and compensation aims to apply the inverse unitary transformation.

**Key observables.** Polarization correlations, Bell–CHSH parameter, Visibility (exceeding 93%), and State Fidelity (above 94.5%).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is between QHQ (free-space) and FPC (fiber-based) compensation methods for short-reach links.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The compensation is implemented using passive elements, and the optimization procedure is performed path-by-path before final state characterization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setups for both QHQ and FPC compensation, and show the measured polarization correlations and reconstructed two-qubit density matrices before and after compensation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of fiber-induced polarization degradation, then details the two compensation methods (QHQ and FPC), describes the systematic path-by-path optimization procedure, and finally presents quantitative comparisons of the recovered entanglement quality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement distribution through optical fibers is essential for quantum communication networks; however, fiber transmission can alter photon polarization and modify the observed correlations of polarization-entangled states, necessitating polarization compensation to recover the desired entangled state in the measurement basis. Here, we experimentally evaluate two passive polarization compensation techniques: a free-space Quarter-Half-Quarter (QHQ) waveplate configuration and an in-fiber three-paddle Fiber Polarization Controller (FPC). The polarization transformation along each downconverted-photon path is independently compensated through a systematic path-by-path optimization procedure. Using both techniques, we recover high-quality polarization correlations and entanglement, with visibilities exceeding $93\%$ in three mutually unbiased bases and fidelities above $94.5\%$. The results demonstrate comparable restoration using free-space waveplate-based and fiber-based control, establishing a systematic framework for laboratory and short-reach quantum communication links.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the successful demonstration of storing single photons in a Pr3+:Y2SiO5 quantum memory using a sophisticated AFC spin-wave protocol combined with spin rephasing. By achieving non-classical correlations for storage times up to 180 us, the research provides a significant step toward realizing scalable quantum repeater links for long-distance quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the hardware requirements for quantum networking by demonstrating robust, long-lived quantum memory storage using solid-state systems, a major focus area for quantum information scientists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is achieving long-lived storage of single photons in an absorptive quantum memory that incorporates spin rephasing mechanisms, which is crucial for building long-distance quantum networks.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated non-classical storage of telecom-heralded single photons in a Pr3+:Y2SiO5 quantum memory, achieving storage times up to 180 us with measurable quantum correlations.

**Method.** The experiment utilizes the Atomic Frequency Comb (AFC) spin-wave protocol combined with advanced spin rephasing sequences (like XY4) to mitigate decoherence in the rare-earth ion doped crystal.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a Pr3+:Y2SiO5 rare-earth ion doped crystal quantum memory. The storage mechanism relies on coupling photonic information to the long-lived spin excitations of the Pr3+ ions, which are manipulated using microwave pulses.

**Key observables.** Non-classical correlations between heralding and stored signal photons (e.g., cross-correlation values up to 4.6(4)), and the decay of AFC photon echoes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Storage times ranging from 3 ms (classical) to 180 us (heralded single photons); the use of telecom-band photons and the AFC spin-wave protocol.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coherence time is limited by spin dephasing, which is mitigated by the applied spin rephasing pulses; the system's scalability is supported by the crystal's ability to support multiplexed storage.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, energy level diagrams, and crucially, show the measured non-classical correlations (e.g., time-correlation histograms) confirming single-photon storage fidelity over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing classical echo measurements, then demonstrating the use of heralded single photons, and finally quantifying the non-classical nature of the stored quantum state using correlation measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Long-lived storage of single photons under the form of atomic excitations is at the foundation of long-distance entanglement distribution in quantum networks. To mitigate decoherence effects induced by the environment, rephasing of the hyperfine coherences using microwave pulses have been implemented in a variety of single-emitter and ensemble-based solid-state systems. However, the demonstration of storage of single photons in an absorptive quantum memory including such spin rephasing mechanism remains elusive. In this work, we show non-classical storage of telecom-heralded single photons in a Pr$^{3+}$:Y$_2$SiO$_5$ rare-earth ion doped crystal quantum memory using the atomic frequency comb (AFC) spin-wave protocol combined with a XY4 spin rephasing sequence. Long-lived AFC photon echoes are first observed in the classical regime for storage times of up to approximately 3 ms. We then demonstrate non-classical correlations between heralding photons and stored signal photons generated by a cavity-enhanced parametric photon-pair source for storage times of up to 180 $μ$s and with measured cross-correlation values as high as 4.6(4). Together with the capacity of Pr$^{3+}$:Y$_2$SiO$_5$ QMs to support highly efficient and multiplexed storage, this result represents a significant step towards scalable long-distance quantum repeater links.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a powerful diagnostic tool—the frequency-dependent impedance—to characterize periodically driven stochastic systems. By mapping the system dynamics onto an equivalent electrical circuit, the authors show that measuring this impedance can reveal subtle underlying structural details, such as broken symmetries or the presence of specific coupling mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong connection between non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (stochastic processes) and electrical circuit theory provides powerful, measurable diagnostics for understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the time-dependent currents and associated impedance in periodically driven stochastic systems.

**Main result.** It derives a closed-form expression for the impedance, showing it can be interpreted as the parallel combination of $N$ components, and demonstrates its use to detect broken symmetries in the system dynamics.

**Method.** The analysis employs linear response theory, utilizing the structure of the master equation and performing complex frequency-domain analysis (impedance/admittance).

**Model / system.** The model is a general stochastic system with $N$ discrete states governed by a time-dependent master equation. Specific applications include linear hopping chains with reservoirs.

**Key observables.** Conductivity $\sigma_{mn}(\omega)$ and impedance $Z_{mn}(\omega)$ as complex functions of frequency $\omega$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency $\omega$, system size $N$, and the structure of the transition rates $W(t)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the linear response regime (small driving).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the equivalent electrical circuit analogy, the frequency dependence of conductivity ($\sigma_{mn}$ vs. $\omega$), and how conductivity measurements can reveal structural differences, such as the presence of a shortcut.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the general impedance formula in the linear regime, interpreting it via an electrical circuit analogy, analyzing low and high-frequency limits, and finally applying the diagnostic tool to distinguish between different microscopic system structures (e.g., with vs. without a shortcut).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the time-dependent currents arising in periodically driven stochastic systems. In the linear regime of small driving, a closed expression is obtained for the impedance/admittance associated with these currents. This expression leads directly to an interpretation in terms of an equivalent electrical circuit. For a stochastic system with $N$ states, the electrical circuit consists of precisely $N$ parallel branches, with each branch comprising a resistor and a capacitor in series. The low- and high-frequency limits of these currents are calculated, and the results are generalized to more general current expressions. We demonstrate our findings across several archetypal settings, illustrating the potential to detect specific broken symmetries or the graph topology associated with the stochastic process.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the complex topological structure of non-Hermitian systems using a generalized Hatano-Nelson model. By introducing multiple competing non-Hermiticity sources, the authors demonstrate the existence of high-order Hopf-link braids and EP-mediated topological phase transitions. These findings suggest potential applications for building robust quantum communication channels and quantum computing platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study connects non-Hermitian physics, topological invariants (Hopf-link braids), and engineered gain/loss mechanisms, providing a framework for designing robust quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the exceptional topological signatures of Hopf-link braids in non-Hermitian systems, focusing on how competing sources of non-Hermiticity influence the topology.

**Main result.** The system exhibits higher-order Hopf-link braiding ($
u = \pm 6$) and undergoes EP-mediated topological phase transitions, which can be controlled by gain-loss non-Hermiticity.

**Method.** The analysis employs biorthogonal eigenvector tracking and uses the maximum Petermann factor divergence as a diagnostic tool to map the structural evolution of exceptional phase boundaries.

**Model / system.** The model is a generalized Hatano-Nelson type tight-binding lattice Hamiltonian incorporating higher-order nonreciprocal hoppings, non-Abelian gauge fields, and staggered gain-loss processes.

**Key observables.** Exceptional Points (EPs), topological winding/braiding degree ($
u$), and the Petermann factor ($K_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gain/loss parameter ($\gamma$), non-Abelian gauge phases ($	heta$), and hopping strengths ($J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The general case is analytically intractable, necessitating simplifications to access EPs; the analysis assumes periodic boundary conditions (PBC).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the braiding indices ($
u$) for NNNN interactions, showing $
u=0, \pm 6$ regimes, and map the spectral evolution in the complex plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian, analyzes the topological invariants ($
u$), tracks the phase boundaries using the Petermann factor, and discusses the physical implications of the EP-mediated transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Hermitian physics endows the non-Abelian systems with exceptional topology characterized by non-commutative braid patterns. Interplay of distinct competing sources of non-Hermiticity may induce novel topological effects. Here, we provide a generalized Hatano-Nelson model with higher-order nonreciprocal hoppings, non-Abelian gauge fields, and staggered gain-loss processes showing the exceptional topological structure of the Hopf-link braids that undergoes a EP-mediated topological phase transition. We demonstrate that mixing multiple nonreciprocal channels drives the system into highly intricate, nested complex energy Hopf-link braids and expands the topological landscape up to higher-order braiding sectors. Furthermore, utilizing biorthogonal eigenvector tracking, we map the structural evolution of the exceptional phase boundaries via the maximum Petermann factor in the phase angle parameter planes. We show that the gain-loss non-Hermiticity drives a topological crossover where the extended exceptional contours constrict into isolated regimes. The demonstration of EP-mediated topological phase transition of Hopf-link braids and the associated rich exceptional phase portraits may offer new physical insights with promising applications in robust, fault-tolerant communication channels and quantum computing platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes extended nonreciprocal quantum batteries to optimize energy storage and transfer. It demonstrates that nonreciprocal charging protocols significantly outperform reciprocal methods by achieving higher energy storage and transfer efficiency. The findings provide a theoretical framework for implementing superior, robust charging mechanisms in quantum energy systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics, specifically focusing on engineered nonreciprocal couplings to enhance energy storage and transfer, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum energy harvesting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the performance and inherent advantages of extended nonreciprocal quantum batteries (QBs) for energy storage and energy transfer compared to standard reciprocal and original nonreciprocal charging protocols.

**Main result.** Extended nonreciprocal charging protocols demonstrate superior energy transfer capabilities and achieve high steady-state energy storage, particularly in multi-threaded configurations, which are unmatched by reciprocal charging.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Lindblad quantum master equation to model system dynamics and derives maximum steady-state energy and energy transfer ratios by comparing different charging topologies.

**Model / system.** The model involves an extended nonreciprocal QB system comprising chargers and a battery, described by Hamiltonians for single-threaded and multi-threaded couplings. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Stored energy ($E_{s,c}, E_{m,c}$), charging power ($P_{s,c}, P_{m,c}$), energy ratio ($\eta$), and gain function ($G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\Delta_i$) between the charging system and the external pump, and the dissipation ratio ($\kappa/\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares the extended nonreciprocal scheme against reciprocal and original nonreciprocal protocols, assuming the system can reach steady-state energy limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic structure of the QB, show time evolution of stored energy and power for different charging flows, and plot energy ratios and gains comparing various charging protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model and dynamics, then analyzing the impact of detuning on single- and multi-threaded charging, and finally quantifying the energy transfer advantages using energy ratios and gain functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This study investigates the performance of extended nonreciprocal quantum batteries (QBs), as well as its advantages in energy storage and energy transfer compared to reciprocal charging and the original nonreciprocal batteries. After analyzing the detuning between the charging system and the external pump, we discover that resonance is a key factor in maintaining high-energy batteries and high charging power; furthermore, the detuning of the charger or battery determines the stability of the charging process for different structures. Research on steady-state energy storage in batteries revealed that single-threaded or multi-threaded charging can achieve nearly infinite energy storage in weakly localized environments, thereby demonstrating the significant energy advantages of extended nonreciprocal quantum batteries. Finally, by considering the energy distribution within the charging system, we observe that nonreciprocal charging offers energy transfer advantages unmatched by reciprocal charging; the former achieves a comprehensive balance between charging cost and energy storage capacity that the latter cannot match. As a novel and superior charging protocol, our findings are expected to provide a potent reference for the promotion and practical implementation of nonreciprocal charging.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a detailed numerical study on implementing high-fidelity quantum gates, specifically ECD gates, in a resonator coupled to a fluxonium qubit. By utilizing the fluxonium's inherent protection against bit-flips and developing advanced simulation techniques, the authors achieve fidelities over 99.9%. The work is significant for advancing practical quantum control in superconducting circuit platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles practical limitations (dissipation, nonlinearities) in implementing fundamental quantum gates for bosonic modes, which is a core area of quantum optics and quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses the challenge of achieving high-fidelity quantum control for bosonic modes, specifically by implementing Echoed Conditional Displacement (ECD) gates while mitigating dominant error sources like qubit bit-flips and resonator nonlinearities.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that fidelities exceeding 99.9% are achievable for ECD gates using a fluxonium qubit protected at the half-flux sweet spot, and they propose an improved sequence to counteract photon loss and nonlinearities.

**Method.** The study combines numerical case studies, semiclassical trajectory simulations, and master equation approaches to model the coupled resonator-fluxonium system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-mode resonator coupled to a fluxonium qubit. The Hamiltonian is analyzed in various frames (dispersive, displaced) and includes terms up to sixth order, requiring advanced simulation techniques to handle strong driving and dissipation.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelities (exceeding 99.9%), resonator dynamics (dispersive shift saturation), and vacuum overlap for the ECD gate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating the fluxonium at the half-flux sweet spot ($\Phi_e = \Phi_0/2$), and the strong driving regime ($\langle\hat{a}^\dagger\hat{a}angle\gg 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard ECD sequence is limited by qubit bit-flips, which is mitigated by using the fluxonium; the analysis relies on semiclassical approximations for strong driving.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the out-and-back protocol for parameter extraction, the vacuum overlap of the ECD return state, and comparisons showing fidelity improvements using the modified ECD sequence and repumping protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for high-fidelity bosonic control (ECD gates) to developing advanced simulation techniques for the strongly driven regime, deriving an improved gate sequence to handle losses, and finally demonstrating high-fidelity operation using repumping protocols.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Echoed Conditional Displacement (ECD) gates constitute a fundamental building block for quantum control of harmonic oscillator modes. However, bit-flips of the auxiliary qubit remain a dominant error mechanism for this kind of bosonic control. In this work, we present a numerical case study of a bit-flip protected fluxonium operating as the control qubit and numerically implement ECD gates in a single-mode resonator-fluxonium device, demonstrating that fidelities exceeding 99.9% are possible. We systematically investigate the resonator dynamics using a combination of semiclassical trajectories and master equation simulations, numerically revealing asymptotic saturation of the dispersive shift in the strongly driven regime of the resonator. We develop an efficient technique to numerically simulate the strongly driven regime of the resonator using a semiclassical formulation that maps the full perturbation series in the dispersive expansion as order-by-order frequency shifts. This provides a compact polynomial description of the resonator which is intuitive and remains valid throughout the dispersive regime. Furthermore, we propose an improved ECD sequence that accounts for the effects of photon loss and spurious nonlinear terms on resonator trajectories.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a non-Markovian input-output theory by introducing a 'pseudomode' to model the interaction of quantum light pulses with structured environments. This method allows for the exact simulation of memory effects, demonstrating that such non-Markovian dynamics cause the output field to become multimode rather than remaining single-mode. It is a significant theoretical advance for quantum optics modeling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, solvable theoretical framework (pseudomode approach) to incorporate complex memory effects into quantum optics problems, moving beyond the limitations of the standard Markov approximation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately describing the interaction of a traveling quantum light pulse with a localized quantum system when the surrounding environment introduces non-Markovian memory effects.

**Main result.** The non-Markovian coherence revival in the scatterer distorts the incoming pulse's single-mode nature, leading to a measurable multimode output field.

**Method.** A novel non-Markovian input-output theory is devised by coherently coupling the scatterer to an auxiliary 'pseudomode,' which subsequently decays into a standard Markovian reservoir.

**Model / system.** The model involves a quantum light pulse scattering off a quantum system (e.g., a two-level atom or cavity) embedded in a structured, non-Markovian environment. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating the pseudomode coupling.

**Key observables.** Mean photon numbers ($\langle\hat{n}_kangle$) showing non-Markovian revival, and the resulting multimode nature of the output field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The decay rate of the pseudomode ($\gamma_p$) and the comparison between non-Markovian and Markovian limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes the ability to model the structured environment via a pseudomode, allowing the use of a Lindblad master equation structure while capturing memory effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (pulse scattering via a pseudomode) and compare mean photon numbers for stimulated emission and cavity transmission, contrasting non-Markovian (solid) versus Markovian (dashed) dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the non-Markovian input-output theory using a pseudomode coupling, applies it to specific physical examples (two-level atom, cavity), and establishes the mathematical equivalence between the resulting Lindblad master equation and the corresponding Heisenberg equations for the system operators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interaction of a traveling pulse of quantum light with a localized quantum system shows non-Markovian features when scattering takes place in a structured environment. Here, we devise a non-Markovian input-output theory by coherently coupling the scatterer to a pseudomode, which decays into a Markovian reservoir. This incorporates memory effects of a structured environment without altering the cascaded nature of the Lindblad master equation, whose solution provides the quantum state of the output field of any desired mode. We apply our theory to the stimulated emission by a two-level atom, and to transmission of a Gaussian pulse through a cavity. We observe that the non-Markovian revival of coherence in the scatterer distorts the single-mode nature of the incoming pulse, thus resulting in a multimode output field.

</details>
