**Summary.** This paper demonstrates coherent microwave-to-optical quantum transduction using $	ext{Yb}:	ext{YSO}$ spins coupled to a 3D microwave resonator. By leveraging the zero-field properties of $	ext{Yb}^{3+}$, the authors achieve a measurable transduction efficiency, which they predict can be significantly enhanced by increasing the dopant concentration. This work advances the integration of quantum memory and quantum transduction in a single solid-state platform.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines cavity QED principles (strong coupling to a resonator) with solid-state quantum memory platforms ($	ext{Yb}:	ext{YSO}$) to solve a major engineering bottleneck in quantum networking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the critical need for efficient microwave-to-optical (M2O) quantum transducers to enable entanglement between remote superconducting qubits.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated M2O transduction in Yb:YSO with an initial internal efficiency of $2 	imes 10^{-8}$, while theoretically predicting an improvement up to $10^{-4}$ using higher doping concentrations.

**Method.** The experiment uses continuous-wave (CW) measurements involving a 3D loop-gap microwave resonator (LGR) and single-pass optical coupling to measure the transduction efficiency via the microwave $S_{21}$ parameter.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes $	ext{Yb}^{3+}$ ions doped into a $	ext{YSO}$ crystal. $	ext{Yb}^{3+}$ is advantageous because it exhibits zero-first-order Zeeman (ZEFOZ) transitions, allowing operation without external magnetic fields that cause decoherence in superconducting qubits.

**Key observables.** Internal transduction efficiency ($\eta_{M2O}$), spin-microwave coupling strength ($g_{m,ens}$), and the LGR reflection spectrum ($S_{21}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration (5 ppm vs. predicted 50 ppm), bandwidth (200 kHz), and the strong cooperativity regime ($	ext{C}_{	ext{m}} > 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes negligible residual environmental magnetic fields and relies on complex signal processing (noise de-embedding) to isolate the weak transduction signal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy level structure, the experimental setup using the LGR, the measured transduction efficiency spectrum, and the avoided crossing measurements showing spin-microwave coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical necessity of M2O transducers to detailing the experimental setup, presenting initial measurements of efficiency and coupling strength, and concluding with theoretical scaling predictions for improved performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microwave-to-optical quantum transducers are essential for entangling remote superconducting qubits. Among the available transduction platforms, ensembles of Er$^{3+}$ and Yb$^{3+}$ ions doped into solids have emerged as leading candidates. While external magnetic fields are needed to split the Zeeman levels of erbium ions and enable a microwave--qubit interface, superconducting qubits suffer decoherence in such fields. In contrast, ytterbium ions exhibit zero-first-order Zeeman transitions and large hyperfine splittings at zero magnetic field (when doped into inorganic crystals). Owing to its long optical and spin coherence times, Yb:YSO has been widely used as a quantum memory, yet its potential for quantum transduction remains largely unexplored. Investigating this material could enable the integration of quantum memory and transduction in a single platform. Here, we demonstrate microwave-to-optical transduction in the continuous-wave regime using a 5\,ppm doped Yb:YSO crystal. The internal transduction efficiency is $2\times10^{-8}$ with a bandwidth of 200\,kHz, achieved using a 3D loop-gap microwave resonator (LGR) and a single-pass optical configuration. We explore all the ground states that form a V-type three-level system with the first and second optical excited states and assert the use of the ground state, which provides the highest efficiency and isolated optical transition. We further establish strong spin-microwave coupling from avoided crossing measurements. With a strong microwave drive to saturate the spin transition, we estimate the spin population pumped into the excited state, close to the simulated value. Finally, we calculate target parameter values for maximum efficiency with our system and suggest using 50\,ppm doped Yb:YSO crystal. With the calculated target parameters, the internal transduction efficiency is predicted to reach up to $10^{-4}$ in the current LGR.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a systematic method to program anharmonic potentials in a superconducting harmonic oscillator using a transmon qubit. By employing bosonic quantum signal processing, the authors successfully implemented and characterized various potentials, including cubic, double-well, and Morse forms. This establishes a practical, reconfigurable route for advanced continuous-variable quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, hardware-validated pathway to engineer complex, non-Gaussian potentials in a superconducting circuit, which is crucial for simulating molecular dynamics and other non-linear quantum processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing programmable non-Gaussian phase gates, which correspond to implementing anharmonic potentials, for continuous-variable quantum simulation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a systematic framework to engineer and characterize anharmonic potentials, including cubic, double-well, and approximate Morse potentials, on a superconducting circuit.

**Method.** They use bosonic quantum signal processing (QSP) to compile the target phase gate into a circuit of qubit rotations and oscillator displacements, followed by pointwise force reconstruction from coherent-state probes.

**Model / system.** The platform is a superconducting harmonic oscillator coupled dispersively to a transmon qubit (bosonic cQED). The system is used to simulate potentials $V(\hat{X})$ via the gate $e^{-iV(\hat{X})}$.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity, Wigner negativity, and the reconstructed force profile $V'(\hat{X})$ are key diagnostics used to validate the engineered potentials.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fidelity is high ($\ge 0.84$), and the reconstruction is validated by comparing the measured force profile against the target potential, especially for the cubic and double-well cases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction fidelity is limited by the finite bandwidth of the Gaussian deconvolution process, and the process relies on the dispersive coupling interaction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the modular circuit diagram for implementing various non-Gaussian gates (cubic, double-well, Morse) and compare the reconstructed force profiles against the target potentials.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically demonstrates the framework by first implementing a cubic phase gate, then engineering and validating double-well potentials relevant for tunneling, and finally achieving an approximate Morse gate, while also testing parity symmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-variable quantum systems offer a resource-efficient route to universal quantum information processing and analogue quantum simulation of real-world processes, such as molecular physics and chemical reactions. Realising these applications, however, requires non-Gaussian operations that implement anharmonic potentials, which are challenging to engineer on demand. Here, we demonstrate a systematic framework to implement programmable non-Gaussian phase gates $e^{-iV(\hat{X})}$, corresponding to the impulsive action of a potential $V(\hat{X})$, in a superconducting harmonic oscillator coupled to a transmon qubit. Using modular circuits derived from bosonic quantum signal processing, we realise a range of target anharmonic potentials on a single piece of hardware by varying a set of qubit rotations interleaved with a fixed calibrated control unitary. We first demonstrate a cubic phase gate, a key ingredient for universal quantum information processing. The resulting high-fidelity non-Gaussian states and the potential reconstructed using our pointwise force reconstruction method jointly confirm the cubic nature of the target gate. We then engineer a family of double-well potentials, relevant models of tunnelling and biased transfer processes, and experimentally validate the double-well topology and the tunable asymmetry. Finally, we engineer an approximate Morse gate, a step towards realistic potentials of molecular vibrational systems, and provide a concrete path towards high-quality engineering and reconstruction of the exponential form. Together, these results establish a practical and reconfigurable route towards continuous-variable quantum information processing and anharmonic quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This experiment reports the first violation of a Bell inequality using the momentum states of massive particles, specifically metastable helium atoms. By employing a dual-resonant Bragg interferometer, the researchers achieved independent control over local measurement bases for momentum-entangled pairs. The measured Bell parameter $S=2.52$ confirms quantum nonlocality in the external degrees of freedom of atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly extends quantum nonlocality tests from internal quantum variables (like spin) to the external, motional degrees of freedom of massive particles, providing a crucial testbed for quantum mechanics in the context of matter waves and potentially gravity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to demonstrate the violation of a Bell inequality using the motional (external) degrees of freedom of massive particles, an experimental frontier in quantum nonlocality testing.

**Main result.** The authors successfully measured a CHSH Bell parameter of $S = 2.52 \pm 0.17$, significantly violating the local realistic bound ($S \le 2$), confirming quantum nonlocality in atomic momentum states.

**Method.** The experiment utilized a matter-wave interferometer sequence (Rarity-Tapster type) applied to momentum-entangled pairs of metastable helium atoms.

**Model / system.** The platform involves ultracold metastable helium atoms ($	ext{He}^*$) prepared in a Bose-Einstein condensate. The key technical advance is the use of a dual-resonant Bragg lattice, which allows for the independent selection of local measurement bases for the two spatially separated momentum modes.

**Key observables.** The Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) Bell parameter ($S$), the Bell correlation function $E(\phi_A, \phi_B)$, and joint atom detection probabilities $P_{k,k'}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measured value $S = 2.52 \pm 0.17$, the local realistic bound $S \le 2$, and the visibility $V = 0.93(2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the two visibility reduction effects (Higher-Order Fock states and finite detection window) are independent, and the scattering process is in the low-gain regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental schematic, the Rarity-Tapster atom interferometer sequence, and measurements of the Bell correlation function $E(\phi_A, \phi_B)$ showing clear phase dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, focusing on the dual-resonant Bragg lattice to enable independent phase control. It then describes the measurement of joint detection probabilities, constructs the Bell correlation function, and finally calculates the CHSH parameter to demonstrate the violation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bell's theorem revealed the fundamental incompatibility between the predictions of quantum mechanics and local realism. Bell inequality violations have since demonstrated quantum nonlocality using photons and internal states of massive particles, but never using their motional states. Here we report the first Bell inequality violation in the motional states of massive particles. Using momentum-entangled pairs of metastable helium atoms manipulated by matter-wave interferometry, we measure a Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) Bell parameter of $S = 2.52 \pm 0.17$, violating the CHSH-Bell inequality ($S \le 2$). Our work completes a long-standing objective in quantum atom optics by extending Bell tests from internal quantum variables to the external degrees of freedom of massive particles, opening a new regime for exploring quantum nonlocality in matter waves and for investigating the interplay between quantum mechanics and gravity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper revolutionizes the study of Brownian heat engines by formulating an inverse-design problem: instead of calculating performance for a given temperature profile, it determines the optimal profile. It rigorously shows that the optimal design differs significantly between the ideal quasistatic limit and the realistic finite-current regime. This provides powerful tools for engineering non-equilibrium energy conversion devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous framework for optimizing non-equilibrium steady states by treating the thermal landscape itself as a control variable, which is a key concept in open quantum systems and energy harvesting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the inverse-design problem in Brownian heat engines: determining the optimal temperature profile and energy barrier to maximize a chosen thermodynamic objective.

**Main result.** While the quasistatic limit yields an efficiency optimum using a hot-uphill/cold-downhill profile, finite current optimization requires a different profile, governed by a balance between thermal rectification gain and transport resistance.

**Method.** The authors derive exact functional gradients and box-constrained optimality conditions by analyzing the stationary current and probability density derived from the Smoluchowski equation.

**Model / system.** The system is an overdamped Brownian particle moving in a symmetric triangular periodic potential under a constant opposing load, subjected to a spatially varying temperature field T(x).

**Key observables.** Efficiency ($\eta$), stationary current ($J$), power, and configurational Shannon Entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature bounds ($T_c, T_h$), opposing load, and the ratio of cycle affinity to transport resistance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be controlled by setting the temperature profile T(x) and the barrier height $U_0$ over the admissible class.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the symmetric triangular potential and compares three canonical thermal landscapes (piecewise constant, etc.).

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from deriving the exact stationary current and density for arbitrary T(x) to analyzing the quasistatic limit, and finally to deriving the functional gradient and optimality conditions for finite current optimization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spatial temperature fields in Brownian heat engines are commonly prescribed \emph{a priori}, and the resulting transport and thermodynamic properties are then calculated. Here we formulate the complementary inverse-design problem: determining the temperature profile and barrier height that optimize a chosen thermodynamic objective. We consider an overdamped Brownian particle in a symmetric triangular periodic potential under a constant opposing load and derive the exact stationary current and probability density for an arbitrary bounded temperature field, $\Tc\le T(x)\le\Th$. In the quasistatic limit, the efficiency becomes an exact functional of two inverse-temperature integrals over the uphill and downhill branches. Its rigorous global maximum under the pointwise temperature bounds is $η_{\max}=1-\Tc/\Th$, attained uniquely, up to sets of measure zero, by the hot-uphill/cold-downhill piecewise-constant profile. At finite current, however, the optimization changes qualitatively because the current is determined jointly by the cycle affinity and a nonlocal transport resistance. We derive the exact functional gradient and the corresponding box-constrained optimality conditions, showing that the current- or power-maximizing profile generally differs from the quasistatic efficiency optimum. For any prescribed temperature field, the current-maximizing barrier satisfies an exact balance between the marginal gain in thermal rectification and the marginal increase in transport resistance, with the characteristic estimate $U_0^*\simeq T_{\rm act}$, where $T_{\rm act}^{-1}=(2/L)\int_0^{L/2}\dd x/T(x)$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper generalizes a classic random parking problem to model the random deposition of isosceles trapeziums on a strip. It derives an exact formula for the jamming limit and proves that the variance of the process converges to the same leading asymptotic density regardless of the specific geometry of the remaining substrate. This provides deep insights into the geometry-dependent nature of random packing processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is classical statistical mechanics (packing/jamming), the rigorous mathematical techniques involving recursive relations, asymptotic analysis, and the study of limiting densities are highly relevant to understanding critical phenomena and non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes Rényi's car-parking problem to study the random sequential deposition of isosceles trapeziums on a strip.

**Main result.** An explicit formula for the jamming constant is derived, showing that the least efficient shape is a genuine trapezium, and the asymptotic variance for related processes is shown to be identical for two different substrate geometries.

**Method.** The analysis uses recursive constructions and moment calculations to derive finite-size asymptotics for the expected number and variance of deposited particles.

**Model / system.** The model involves depositing isosceles trapeziums randomly on a strip substrate, leading to a recursive process that generates two distinct, but asymptotically equivalent, substrate geometries: a parallelogram and a trapezium.

**Key observables.** Parking Constant ($	heta$), Expected Number ($\mu(x)$), and Variance ($\sigma^2(x)$) of deposited trapeziums.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lengths of the two bases of the trapezium ($a$ and $b$), and the substrate size ($x$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the substrate is a strip and relies on the asymptotic equivalence of the two resulting substrate types ($P_x$ and $T_x$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the strip substrate, different deposited shapes (triangles, rectangles, trapeziums), and the two relevant recursive substrate geometries (parallelogram and trapezium).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general problem, derives the explicit formula for the parking constant, calculates finite-size asymptotics for moments, and proves the asymptotic equality of the variance for the two underlying substrate types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article, we study a generalisation of Rényi's car-parking problem in which isosceles trapeziums are sequentially deposited on a strip. We derive an explicit formula for the parking constant in terms of the lengths of the two bases, recovering the classical rectangular and triangular models as special cases. We further obtain quantitative finite-size asymptotics for both the expected number and the variance of deposited trapeziums, with convergence rates that depend explicitly on the geometry of the deposited particle. In particular, although the recursive construction involves two different substrate geometries, their variances have the same leading asymptotic density. Finally, we show that the parking constant depends non-monotonically on the ratio of the two base lengths and possesses a unique minimiser, so that the least efficient shape is a genuine trapezium rather than a triangle.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a sophisticated numerical simulation of quantum annealing dynamics using time-dependent variational Monte Carlo (t-VMC). By employing advanced sampling techniques like Parallel Tempering and Blurred Sampling, the authors successfully simulate spin-glass models on complex graphs. The results establish a rigorous classical benchmark for assessing the computational feasibility and accuracy of simulating quantum hardware performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a highly detailed, state-of-the-art classical benchmark for simulating quantum annealing dynamics, offering crucial insights into the numerical limitations and required resources for simulating quantum advantage experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of accurately simulating the non-equilibrium dynamics of frustrated quantum spin systems, specifically those relevant to quantum annealing experiments.

**Main result.** The authors successfully simulate the D-Wave annealing protocol on complex topologies, achieving correlation errors comparable to the quantum annealer results, even for challenging instances where other methods fail.

**Method.** They employ Time-Dependent Variational Monte Carlo (t-VMC) using a structured correlator state ansatz, stabilized by advanced sampling techniques like Parallel Tempering and Blurred Sampling.

**Model / system.** The system is a transverse-field Ising model defined on various spin-glass topologies (e.g., 2D cylinder, biclique). The dynamics are simulated by evolving the variational state according to the time-dependent variational principle.

**Key observables.** Two-spin Ising correlations ($C_{ij}$), relative correlation error ($\epsilon_c$), and numerical diagnostics like MCMC acceptance rates and TDVP residuals ($R_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Annealing times ($t_a = 7, 20$ ns), variational ansatz rank ($R$), and regularization parameters ($	ext{rcond}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is projecting the full quantum dynamics onto a variational manifold defined by the correlator product state ansatz. Numerical stability requires advanced sampling and ODE solvers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the simulation setup, the annealing schedule, the ansatz structure, and diagnostic plots comparing correlation errors ($\epsilon_c$) across different topologies and the impact of advanced sampling techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework (t-VMC, ansatz construction), presents the numerical implementation challenges (sampling difficulties, stiffness), and validates the method by simulating dynamics on increasingly complex and challenging spin-glass graph structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable quantum annealers can realize real-time dynamics of frustrated transverse-field Ising models on large, nontrivial graphs. Recent work by King et al. argued that the classical simulation of such experiments would require exponential computational resources for classical methods such as tensor networks and neural quantum states. Here, we numerically simulate the D-Wave spin-glass annealing protocol with time-dependent variational Monte Carlo (t-VMC) using a correlator state tailored to spin-glass dynamics. For the two-dimensional cylinder, three-dimensional dimer, diamond, and biclique instances considered, at annealing times of 7 and 20 ns, we show that systematically increasing the variational ansatz size enables t-VMC to approximate the final two-spin correlation errors of the quantum processing unit (QPU). We also perform an accurate large-scale simulation of a challenging biclique instance for which no other variational method is known to work, and we find close agreement with the quantum annealer. Through ablation studies, we identify poor Markov-chain mixing, high-variance local-energy estimators, and stochastic Runge-Kutta error estimates as the principal numerical failure modes. We address these numerical issues by using parallel tempering, blurred sampling and an importance-weighted differential equation solver, thereby clarifying the numerical requirements for stable, large-scale t-VMC simulations. Our results extend the frontier of classical simulation while providing a realistic assessment of the computational costs of simulating quantum dynamics at this scale.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to improve the sensitivity of axion dark matter searches in microwave cavities by exploiting the non-monochromatic nature of the axion signal. By carefully tuning the amplifier to operate symmetrically around the cavity resonance, the measured signal power is effectively doubled while the vacuum noise remains constant. This results in a significant reduction of the effective quantum noise limit, enhancing the search capability beyond the standard quantum limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it provides a concrete, quantum-limited technique to surpass standard noise constraints in a real-world detection scenario.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of axion dark matter searches conducted using microwave cavity haloscopes, which are currently limited by the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL).

**Main result.** By exploiting the non-monochromatic nature of the axion signal and operating the amplifier at the half-pump frequency, the effective noise limit can be reduced to $1/\sqrt{2}$ quanta after optimal matched filtering, improving the SNR by a factor of two.

**Method.** The authors use quadrature analysis (homodyne readout) on the output of a Josephson Parametric Amplifier (JPA) to combine signal and idler spectral components.

**Model / system.** The system involves axion-photon conversion measured in high-Q microwave cavities, amplified by a JPA. The analysis models the output Power Spectral Density (PSD) by considering the symmetric population of signal and idler bands.

**Key observables.** Output Power Spectral Density (PSD), Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), and the effective quantum noise limit (quanta per frequency bin).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High cavity quality factor ($Q_c \gg Q_a$), operating at the half-pump frequency, and the resulting noise reduction factor of $1/\sqrt{2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the cavity quality factor is much larger than the axion's bandwidth ($Q_c \gg Q_a$) and relies on the signal symmetrically populating both signal and idler bands.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the analysis compares the PSD in non-degenerate ($
u_c 
eq 0$) versus quasi-degenerate ($
u_c = 0$) resonance regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the SQL constraint, develops the theoretical framework using JPA output PSD calculations, compares the non-degenerate and quasi-degenerate regimes, and derives the improved noise limit for the latter.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The search for wave-like dark matter using microwave cavity haloscopes is constrained by the Standard Quantum Limit, which dictates that phase-preserving linear amplification results in a minimum of one quantum of total system noise for a narrow-band signal. We demonstrate that this limit is effectively halved for a non-monochromatic axion signal coupled to a high-$Q$ cavity. By operating a Josephson Parametric Amplifier such that the cavity resonance is centered exactly at the half-pump frequency, the axion signal symmetrically populates both the signal and idler bands. Through quadrature analysis of the homodyne readout, we show that the incoherent sum of these mirrored spectral components doubles the measured signal power while the vacuum noise remains constant. This operation yields an effective noise limit of 0.5 quanta per frequency bin, translating to an overall effective limit of $1/\sqrt{2}$ quanta after optimal matched filtering.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models a complex dissipative optomechanical system to demonstrate that a synthetic-flux phase can precisely control the degree of hyperchaos. While the control of chaos is robustly shown, the study concludes that this flux control does not translate into a measurable enhancement for force sensing on the chaotic attractor, suggesting limitations in the sensing protocol.

**Why it may be interesting.** It combines advanced nonlinear dynamics (hyperchaos control) with quantum measurement theory (Fisher information) within the context of cavity optomechanics, providing a rigorous, albeit model-level, testbed for quantum control.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the control of hyperchaos order and the performance of matched-resource sensing in a dissipative optomechanical system using a synthetic-flux phase.

**Main result.** The synthetic-flux phase successfully controls the hyperchaos order up to four unstable Lyapunov directions, but the matched-resource sensing protocol yields no flux-induced enhancement on the chaotic attractor.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced dynamical systems diagnostics, including calculating the full Lyapunov spectrum, analyzing the Poincaré map via monodromy multipliers, and evaluating the matched Fisher information.

**Model / system.** The system is a dissipative optomechanical model comprising one cavity and two mechanical resonators, analyzed using semiclassical equations of motion derived from a Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Hyperchaos order (number of positive Lyapunov exponents), Matched Fisher gain, Neimark-Sacker bifurcation points, and Lyapunov exponents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Synthetic-flux phase ($\Phi_{syn}$), Drive amplitude ($E$), Coupling strengths ($g_1, g_2$), and damping rates ($\kappa, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The strong-coupling regime explored is highly idealized and far beyond current experimental anchors; the analysis relies on consistency checks across multiple diagnostic tools.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the validation of consistency metrics (QR exponents), the dependence of Lyapunov exponents on drive amplitude and flux phase, and the correlation dimension of the attractor.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model, detailing the consistency checks (Lyapunov-Floquet), demonstrating flux control over hyperchaos order, and finally analyzing the sensing gain on the resulting chaotic attractor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Within a normalised six-dimensional model of dissipative optomechanics (one cavity + two mechanical resonators), a synthetic-flux phase $Φ_{\rm syn}$ selects the \emph{order} of a drive- and coupling-gated hyperchaos transition---up to four simultaneously unstable Lyapunov directions, beyond any reported single-mode benchmark---while the same matched-resource force-sensing protocol yields no flux-induced enhancement on the chaotic attractor. Building on the topology of Muthukumar \emph{et al.}~[PR Applied \textbf{24}, 014053 (2025)], a phase-consistent Floquet--Lyapunov protocol (cross-checked by monodromy multipliers, dissipative volume balance and a 180-run three-seed audit) localises a Neimark--Sacker bifurcation at $E^{*}=\num{1.060}$ ($θ=0$). At a weakly coupled reference the matched Fisher gain reaches at most $\num{1.32}\times$ (flux-off) and $\num{1.16}\times$ (single-mode), with Monte-Carlo median $\num{1.039}\times$ (90\,\% CI $[\num{1.025},\num{1.053}]$); on the chaotic attractor the identical protocol returns a null result ($\mathcal{G}_{A/B}=\num{1.039}\pm\num{0.014}$). Truncated-Fock and truncated-Wigner checks support the mean-field description at selected points. Both the hyperchaos classification and the sensing result remain strictly model-level: the strong-coupling sector explored here lies $\num{2542}\times$ beyond anchored silicon optomechanical couplings. Closing that gap requires a measured inter-resonator hopping $J_m$ and fixed bath temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel single-shot fidelity metric to rigorously evaluate Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detectors. It overcomes the limitations of standard ensemble averaging by explicitly separating hardware efficiency loss from errors caused by finite energy resolution. The resulting metric allows for the first direct, quantitative comparison of diverse detector technologies for use in photonic quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, quantitative benchmark for quantum hardware, directly linking fundamental detector physics (resolution) to the operational requirements of quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional fidelity metrics for Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detectors are insufficient because they fail to quantify single-shot misidentification errors arising from finite energy resolution.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a single-shot fidelity metric that separates hardware efficiency loss from resolution-driven misidentification, allowing for a universal, direct comparison across different detector types.

**Method.** They combine an efficiency-based POVM with a resolution-driven confusion matrix to construct a single-shot measurement probability, $P_{ss}(n|k)$.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on PNR detectors essential for photonic quantum computing, comparing architectures like Transition-Edge Sensors (TES) and SNSPDs.

**Key observables.** Single-shot fidelity ($F_k$), detection efficiency ($\eta$), and the resolution confusion matrix ($\mathbf{R}_{ab}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon number ($k$), detection efficiency ($\eta$), and the acceptance region ($	au$) used to define measurement boundaries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the detector output retains a gradient of confidence about the true photon number, and Gaussian approximations are used for modeling detector responses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of the single-shot POVM, showing how the composition of efficiency and resolution matrices yields the final fidelity, and compare $F_k$ vs. $k$ for different detector types and operating regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the inadequacy of ensemble-averaged metrics, then develops the single-shot fidelity framework by composing the POVM and confusion matrix, and finally applies this framework to compare multiple real-world detector architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon-number-resolving (PNR) detectors are essential for photonic quantum computing, where a single measurement outcome must reliably herald a specific quantum state. However, detector fidelity is conventionally evaluated using ensemble-averaged statistics obtained from many measurements, which can remain high even when individual photon-number assignments are frequently misidentified. Here we introduce a universal single-shot fidelity that directly quantifies the probability of correctly identifying a photon number in a single measurement. The framework combines an efficiency-based POVM with a resolution-driven confusion matrix derived from the detector response, allowing photon loss and photon-number misidentification to be treated separately and then recombined into a single operational metric. This distinction reveals two fundamentally different limitations. Detection-efficiency loss represents an unrecoverable hardware constraint, whereas resolution-driven misidentification can be reduced by introducing a rejection region, trading generation rate for confidence. Because the metric is defined independently of detector architecture, it enables direct comparison between energy-resolving detectors such as transition-edge sensors and multiplexed click-based detectors on the same footing. Applying the framework to calibration data from three distinct detector architectures, we demonstrate quantitative comparison across photon-number regimes relevant to both discrete-variable and continuous-variable photonic quantum computing. The resulting benchmark provides a common operational metric for evaluating photon-number-resolving detectors and connecting detector performance to photonic quantum-computing requirements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies the concept of Thouless pumping to a Fock-state lattice, a system characterized by bosonic excitations. By analyzing the system's topology, the authors demonstrate quantized transport and extend the method to generate superpositions of squeezed Fock states. This establishes the pumping mechanism as a powerful tool for non-classical quantum state preparation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum state engineering using topological principles in a bosonic setting, providing a robust method for generating non-classical states like squeezed Fock state superpositions, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Thouless pumping in a one-dimensional semi-infinite Fock-state lattice and extends this framework to prepare superpositions of squeezed Fock states.

**Main result.** Topologically protected quantized transport is demonstrated, and the method successfully generates superpositions of squeezed Fock states, evidenced by interference fringes in the Wigner function.

**Method.** The analysis uses topological invariants (Chern number) derived from the overlap matrix in the real-space approach, extending the model via a squeezing transformation.

**Model / system.** The system is a Fock-state lattice (FSL) modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating Jaynes-Cummings and anti-Jaynes-Cummings couplings. The bosonic nature introduces an intrinsic sqrt(n)-dependent coupling, leading to non-uniform hopping.

**Key observables.** Quantized directional transport (Chern number $C=\pm 1$), mean position $\langle \hat{n} angle$, and the presence of interference fringes in the Wigner function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is initially restricted to the dimer limit, and the topological robustness depends on adiabatic parameter tuning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis relies on the dimer limit due to the complexity introduced by the sqrt(n) dependence under generic modulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the model, the topological phase boundary separating $C=\pm 1$ and $C=0$ regimes, and the pumping dynamics showing quantized plateaus in mean position and Wigner function interference.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes Thouless pumping in the dimer limit of the FSL using topological invariants. It then extends the framework by introducing an inter-cell coupling and applying a squeezing transformation to the squeezed Fock-state basis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, Thouless pumping in a one-dimensional semi-infinite Fock-state lattice is investigated. A distinctive feature of such lattices is the intrinsic $\sqrt{n}$-dependent coupling arising from the bosonic mode, which leads to spatially nonuniform hopping amplitudes. In the dimer limit, the topological invariants and the quantized transport dynamics in the Fock-state basis are numerically evaluated and analyzed. By introducing an additional inter-cell coupling and applying a squeezing transformation, the framework is then extended to Thouless pumping in the squeezed Fock-state basis, where a topologically protected scheme for preparing superpositions of squeezed Fock states is proposed. This study establishes Thouless pumping in Fock-state lattices as a useful tool for quantum state engineering, shifting the focus from observing topological transport to harnessing it for the preparation of non-classical states of the bosonic mode.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to study active glasses, showing that self-propulsion systematically reduces the material's kinetic fragility. This activity-induced change fundamentally alters the mechanical response, making the material more ductile and establishing fragility as a universal descriptor for yielding in both passive and active amorphous solids.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical soft matter, the concept of tuning material response (brittle to ductile) via an active driving force is relevant to non-equilibrium dynamics and energy dissipation in complex quantum or mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the introduction of activity (self-propulsion) modifies the fundamental physical parameter, kinetic fragility, that governs the yielding transition in amorphous solids.

**Main result.** Activity systematically reduces kinetic fragility, shifts the mechanical response from brittle to ductile, and establishes kinetic fragility as a unifying parameter for yielding in both passive and active glasses.

**Method.** Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations are used to model the system's response to oscillatory shear, analyzing structural relaxation and mechanical failure.

**Model / system.** The model uses a Kob-Andersen glass former doped with Run-and-Tumble (RTP) active particles, simulating the system's dynamics under oscillatory shear stress.

**Key observables.** Kinetic fragility ($K_{	ext{VFT}}$), common yield point ($\gamma_c$), yield strain ($\gamma_y$), stress relaxation profiles, and shear band morphology.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Activity parameters ($ho_a, f_0, 	au_p$), temperature ($T$), and shear strain amplitude ($\gamma_{max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending the concept of kinetic fragility, previously established for passive glasses, to the active regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of structural relaxation time ($	au_\alpha$) with activity, the shift from brittle to ductile yielding behavior, and the dependence of the yield point on thermal history.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the role of fragility in passive glasses, then introduces active particles via MD simulations, and finally quantifies how activity systematically modifies the yield point, mechanical response, and underlying critical dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The yielding transition marks the onset of irreversible plastic deformation in amorphous solids and plays a central role in determining the mechanical stability and failure of metallic glasses, colloidal suspensions, and biological assemblies. Despite extensive research, the microscopic factors governing the nature of yielding, particularly the transition between brittle and ductile mechanical responses, remain poorly understood. Recent studies have identified kinetic fragility as a key parameter governing yielding in passive glasses; whether this connection persists in active glasses remains open. Here, using molecular dynamics simulations of a Kob-Andersen glass former doped with Run-and-Tumble (RTP) active particles under oscillatory shear, we show that activity systematically reduces kinetic fragility and consequently alters the mechanical response. The common yield point $γ_c$ decreases monotonically with activity and exhibits a power-law dependence on the Arrhenius activation barrier. Increasing activity suppresses the dependence of the yield strain on thermal history and transforms the response from brittle-like to increasingly ductile, with smoother stress relaxation and reduced stress discontinuities. The timescale to reach steady state near yielding retains a critical power-law divergence, indicating that activity does not alter the underlying critical character of the transition. Active glasses also develop broader, more diffuse shear bands. Our results establish kinetic fragility as a unifying parameter governing yielding in both passive and active glasses and demonstrate that activity offers a powerful route to tune the mechanical response of amorphous materials.

</details>

**Authors:** Zi-Qiang Zhao, Nayun Jia, Zhang-Yu Nie, Hua-Bi Zeng, Jing-Fei Zhang, Xin Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02521v1>

**Summary.** This work establishes a universal scaling law, $S_f \propto k^{-(d+p+2)}$, that characterizes the spatial fingerprint of topological defect cores. By deriving this law analytically and verifying it numerically across diverse systems—from superconductors to cosmological backgrounds—the authors provide a powerful new diagnostic tool for studying defect physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The universality of the scaling exponent, independent of the specific microscopic dynamics or underlying system, provides a powerful, model-agnostic diagnostic tool for characterizing topological structures in complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to discover and analytically prove a universal scaling law for the 'fingerprint' of topological defect cores, an aspect previously unexplored despite existing scaling laws for defect correlations.

**Main result.** A universal scaling law, $S_f \propto k^{-(d+p+2)}$, is discovered for the form factor in the region $k>1/\xi$, which is analytically proven to originate from a universal V-shaped cusp at the defect core.

**Method.** The authors used theoretical derivation involving the form factor decomposition and confirmed the scaling law through extensive numerical simulations across multiple physical models.

**Model / system.** The study examines topological defects (kinks, vortices, domain walls) arising from symmetry breaking in various contexts, including condensed matter (Ginzburg-Landau, Gross-Pitaevskii), cosmology (Klein-Gordon in FRW background), and holographic models (gauge/gravity duality).

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the form factor, $S_f(\mathbf{k})$, and the diagnostic is the scaling exponent $-(d+p+2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Healing length ($\xi$), spatial dimension ($d$), and defect codimension ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on approximating the core shape using a $	anh$ function, and the numerical simulations test various regimes (weak vs. strong coupling).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spatial configurations of various defects (Kink, Vortex) and show the scaling behavior of the form factor $S_f$ for different defect types using the TDGL model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the gap in knowledge regarding defect core fingerprints, deriving the universal scaling law analytically, and then rigorously verifying this scaling law using numerical simulations across four distinct physical frameworks (TDGL, GP, Holographic, FRW).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological defects are ubiquitous in physics, arising from condensed matter physics to the early universe. Although there exist many universal scaling laws for correlations between topological defects, such as Porod scaling, the fingerprint of topological defect cores has remained largely unexplored. Here, we discover a universal scaling law in the region $k>1/ξ$, where $ξ$ is the healing length of the topological defects, taking the scaling of the form factor $S_f \propto k^{-(d+p+2)}$, where $d$ is the spatial dimension and $p$ is the defect codimension. We analytically prove that this exponent originates from a universal V-shaped cusp at the defect core and is independent of the underlying system and dynamics. Numerical simulations verify this scaling law in four typical frameworks: the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau and Gross-Pitaevskii equations in the weak-coupling regime, the gauge/gravity duality model in the strong-coupling regime, and the Klein-Gordon equation in the Friedmann-Robertson-Walker background in cosmology. Our work provides a new probe for studying topological defects in systems ranging from superconductors to cosmological phase transitions.

</details>

**Authors:** Dihang Sun, Nannan Ma, Ching Hua Lee, Tianqi Chen, Jiangbin Gong  
**Type:** both · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02394v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

Diagonalization of the effective Hamiltonian H(2n) eff and retention of the low- est k states define the renormalized 2n-site block. **Summary.** This paper introduces a recursive module-coupling algorithm to tackle the exponential complexity of finding many-body eigenstates. It works by iteratively building the low-energy subspace from smaller, coupled modules, offering a speedup over standard methods like DMRG. The approach is validated both through classical error analysis and demonstrated feasibility on current quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a systematic, hardware-compatible framework for approximating ground and low-lying excited states, which is crucial for simulating complex quantum materials or quantum dynamics on near-term quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is efficiently finding the low-lying eigenstates of large, many-body Hamiltonians, which suffers from exponential computational scaling.

**Main result.** The proposed recursive module-coupling algorithm offers a computationally advantageous alternative to methods like DMRG, achieving state preparation in a modular, scalable manner.

**Method.** The method recursively decomposes the system into smaller, locally-coupled modules, constructing the low-energy variational basis from the module eigenstates at each merge step.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general many-body systems modeled by local lattice Hamiltonians, often analyzed using a Hamiltonian partitioned into coupled modules (H = H_A + H_B + V).

**Key observables.** Low-lying eigenstates, energy gap, and state preparation fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (L), truncation level (k), and the size of the elementary module (L_0).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The low-energy eigenstates are assumed to have dominant support within the product space formed by the low-energy subspaces of the subsystems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic recursive projection process, the required minimum subspace dimension (k_min) for target fidelity, and the reduction of error density when increasing the initial block size (L_0).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the modular projected eigensolver and its recursive construction, followed by classical numerical demonstrations, and concludes with detailed error scaling analysis and hardware-compatible quantum circuit formulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finding the eigenstates of a many-body Hamiltonian is a fundamental challenge in physics and computational science. Since the search space grows exponentially with system size, numerous classical and quantum algorithms have been developed to address this problem. A practical strategy is to identify a physics-informed low-dimensional subspace that effectively accommodates the low-lying eigenstates, thereby reducing the computational complexity. In this paper, we propose a recursive module-coupling algorithm, which iteratively treats a system as a composition of locally-coupled smaller modules, with low-energy subspace estimated successively according to the same recursive structure. Unlike the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) approach that optimizes a global matrix product state through repeated local sweeps and obtains excited states sequentially, our algorithm constructs a physically tailored variational basis from module eigenstates and obtains several low-energy states on an equal footing, leading to substantial speedups if targeting moderate accuracy. Our proposed method further leads naturally to a recursive quantum variational algorithm, providing a systematic and modular circuit-construction framework compatible with contemporary gate-based quantum architectures. At each recursive level, block encoders are trained to map logical basis states onto the retained physical subspace, within which a variational circuit is subsequently optimized.Such a quantum-circuit implementation provides not only a quantum multistate eigensolver, but also a systematic prescription for hierarchically constructing quantum state-preparation circuits. Classical simulations demonstrate the accuracy and efficiency of the proposed method, whereas experiments on IBM quantum processors show that eigenstate preparation with reasonable fidelities is achievable even in the current NISQ era.

</details>

<a id="paper-2609.01904"></a>
### [Quantum Circuit and Tensor Network Implementation of the 2D Acoustic Wave Equation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01904v1)

**Authors:** Tamas Nemeth, Gabor Vattay  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01904v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 2: Wavefields originating from the center of the model at location of (1280 m, 1280 m). Upper wavefield is calculated by an FDTD solver and the lower wavefield is by an MPS solver.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work develops a framework to simulate seismic wave propagation by translating the acoustic wave equation into a quantum circuit model. Crucially, they show how this quantum evolution can be deterministically emulated on classical hardware using Matrix Product States (MPS) via Tensor Network techniques. This establishes MPS as a scalable and viable computational tool for large-scale wavefield dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is seismology, the methodology—translating a continuous PDE evolution into a structured tensor network update—is highly relevant for simulating complex dynamics in quantum many-body systems where exact diagonalization is impossible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating seismic wave propagation governed by the 2D acoustic wave equation in complex media.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that mapping the wavefield dynamics onto a Tensor Train/Matrix Product State (MPS) representation provides a viable, deterministic classical emulation bridge for simulating large-scale wavefield propagation.

**Method.** The simulation uses an explicit Finite-Difference Time-Domain (FDTD) formulation, which is then mapped onto a quantum circuit model and subsequently emulated classically using Matrix Product Operators (MPOs) within the Tensor Network framework.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the 2D acoustic wavefield u(x, z, t) governed by the wave equation, simulated using the Marmousi model as a test case.

**Key observables.** The resulting 2D seismic wavefields generated by the simulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bond dimension $\chi$ in the MPS representation, the time step $\Delta t$, and the CFL stability criterion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the MPS representation can accurately emulate the quantum evolution, requiring periodic truncation via SVD to maintain computational tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the 2D seismic wavefields generated by both the standard FDTD solver and the MPS solver for the Marmousi model.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the quantum circuit formulation for the FDTD update, maps this to a Tensor Train/MPS representation using MPOs, and validates the MPS solver by comparing its results against the established FDTD solver.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a cohesive framework for simulating seismic wave propagation utilizing quantum computing paradigms and their classical tensor network equivalents.   We detail a quantum circuit-based formulation for the explicit finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) solution of the two-dimensional acoustic wave equation and   map this quantum architecture onto a tensor train representation, namely for Matrix Product State (MPS).   The MPS solver enables deterministic simulation of large-scale wavefield dynamics on classical high-performance computing systems.   We demonstrate the MPS representation by computing 2D seismic wavefields on the Marmousi model.   Our results indicate that the MPS representation is a viable direction for computing and scaling wavefield propagation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports a highly precise, magnetic-field-calibration-free determination of the hyperfine constant A for $^{40}	ext{K}$ using ultracold Fermi gases. By measuring the difference between two magnetically insensitive microwave transitions via Ramsey interferometry, the authors significantly reduce the uncertainty in this fundamental constant. This improved reference value is crucial for advancing high-precision spectroscopy and metrology using alkali atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a benchmark measurement for fundamental atomic constants in a highly controlled, quantum gas environment. The technique itself—achieving field independence—is a significant methodological advance for precision measurements in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to determine the ground-state hyperfine constant A for $^{40}	ext{K}$ with extremely high precision, overcoming the limitations imposed by magnetic field calibration in conventional methods.

**Main result.** The authors determined the hyperfine constant A = -h x 285.730536(2) MHz, achieving an absolute uncertainty of only about 2 Hz, which represents an improvement of nearly three orders of magnitude over previous measurements.

**Method.** They developed a magnetic-field-calibration-free method by measuring the frequency difference between two magnetically insensitive ('clock') transitions using Ramsey interferometry on an ultracold Fermi gas.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses an ultracold Fermi gas of $^{40}	ext{K}$ trapped in an Optical Dipole Trap (ODT). Measurements involve microwave spectroscopy between Zeeman sublevels within the ground-state hyperfine manifold.

**Key observables.** Transition frequencies ($\omega_\alpha, \omega_eta$), Ramsey oscillation visibility, and the hyperfine constant A.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement is performed over a range of bias magnetic fields (B) and is designed to be insensitive to magnetic field calibration and atomic density variations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the AC Stark shift can be accurately removed by extrapolating measurements to zero optical intensity, and the uncertainty budget is calculated by combining contributions from magnetic field fluctuations and AC Stark shifts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level diagrams, Ramsey oscillation decay curves, and plots showing the measured hyperfine constant A versus magnetic field and ODT intensity, used for systematic correction.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the use of Ramsey spectroscopy on two insensitive transitions to derive a field-independent expression for A. It then rigorously calculates the total uncertainty budget by propagating errors from magnetic field noise and AC Stark shifts, culminating in the final highly precise value for A.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hyperfine constant $A$ is a key parameter of the hyperfine structure and underpins precision spectroscopy and metrology. In this Letter, we develop a magnetic-field-calibration-free method for determining the ground-state hyperfine constant $A$ in an ultracold $^{40}$K Fermi gas by utilizing a pair of magnetically insensitive ("clock") transitions. This overcomes the stringent magnetic-field calibration requirements of conventional methods. We measure the transition frequency between these two magnetically insensitive transitions with Hz-level resolution over a range of magnetic fields, and obtain the ground-state hyperfine constant $A = -h\times 285.730536(2)\,\mathrm{MHz}$, corresponding to an absolute uncertainty of about $2\,\mathrm{Hz}$. Our value reduces the uncertainty by nearly three orders of magnitude compared with previous determinations, providing a substantially improved reference for high-precision spectroscopy and metrology with $^{40}$K.

</details>

**Authors:** Farrokh Labib

**Summary.** This paper presents a significant algorithmic improvement for Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) by optimizing both the measurement schedule and the data processing step. By replacing power-of-two ladders with a geometrically spaced ladder ($r \approx 1.45$) and using an exact maximum-likelihood estimator, the authors achieve state-of-the-art query complexity. This advancement makes quantum estimation more efficient and practically viable for quantum computing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides concrete, improved algorithmic tools for quantum estimation, which is a fundamental building block for quantum simulation and sensing protocols relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) by overcoming limitations in conventional non-adaptive estimation schedules and post-processing techniques.

**Main result.** The authors propose a fully parallel, deterministic-schedule estimator using an exact maximum-likelihood estimate and a geometric ladder with ratio $r \approx 1.45$, achieving query complexities matching the best adaptive methods.

**Method.** The methodology involves replacing subspace-based post-processing with an exact maximum-likelihood estimate and optimizing the measurement schedule from power-of-two steps to a geometrically spaced ladder.

**Model / system.** The underlying model is quantum state estimation for the amplitude $a$ of a marked component, utilizing the Grover iterator $G$ applied $n$ times.

**Key observables.** Total query complexity ($C_\delta$), maximum sequential depth ($n_L$), and the achievable error percentile ($\epsilon_{95}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimal geometric ladder ratio $r \approx 1.45$, and the target error $\varepsilon$ (ranging from $3.5	imes 10^{-3}$ to $10^{-6}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the standard shot-noise model, and the derived bounds are compared against the Cramér-Rao Lower Bound (CRLB).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare total-query constants and basin-flip rates against the ladder ratio $r$, showing the optimal balance near $r \approx 1.45$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing QAE methods, then introduces the improved ML estimator and geometric ladder, deriving the optimal ratio by balancing aliasing and redundancy costs, and finally confirming the performance against state-of-the-art benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-adaptive quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) fixes its Grover depths in advance, so every circuit can run in parallel, but it has so far needed more queries than the best adaptive methods. We show that most of this gap comes from two conventional choices: subspace-based post-processing and power-of-two depth ladders. We replace the first by the exact maximum-likelihood estimate, one matrix multiplication per batch of estimates, and the second by a geometric ladder with ratio $r \approx 1.45$. The result is a fully parallel, deterministic-schedule estimator with total query complexity $2.8$-$3.1/\varepsilon$ at 95% confidence for target errors from $3.5\times 10^{-3}$ to $10^{-6}$. This matches the average-case complexity of chebAE, the best benchmarked adaptive method, within statistical uncertainty (with the lower point estimate at every scale tested), beats its maximum-observed complexity by $1.6\times$, and needs a maximum sequential depth of only $0.21/\varepsilon$ against chebAE's $2.9/\varepsilon$. Relative to csAE, the best non-adaptive benchmark, the constants improve by 30-35% at 95% and $1.5$-$1.7\times$ at 99% confidence. The optimal ratio has a simple origin. Doubling is the fastest depth growth at which the data can still tell neighboring candidate values apart, so power-of-two ladders sit at the edge of confusion and must buy reliability with extra shots; a slightly denser ladder checks every scale redundantly. An error-probability analysis reproduces the measured failure rates and locates the optimum. The likelihood formulation extends directly to noise-aware estimation, and uniformly scaling the capped ladder covers the depth-limited regime, realizing the optimal trade-off $M N_{\mathrm{tot}} \approx (0.4$-$0.6)/\varepsilon^2$ within $\sim 1.1\times$ of the schedule's Cramér-Rao limit.

</details>

**Authors:** Huidan Zheng, Ilkwon Sohn, Jun Heo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02098v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an entanglement-assisted stabilizer framework for sensing local quantum phases across spatially separated nodes. By leveraging the structure of EAQEC codes, it enables simultaneous phase estimation and error syndrome identification. Crucially, the method guarantees that the encoded logical information survives the sensing process, making it highly relevant for scalable quantum networking protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, structured method for performing distributed quantum measurements while maintaining coherence, which is crucial for scaling quantum networks and distributed quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust framework for performing distributed quantum sensing of local phases using entanglement assistance.

**Main result.** The framework successfully allows for local phase estimation while ensuring the logical quantum information remains available for subsequent operations, achieving the expected scaling of $1/\sqrt{M}$.

**Method.** The authors utilize an entanglement-assisted stabilizer framework built upon extended EAQEC codes, employing joint measurements on remote stabilizer pairs to extract phase information and error syndromes.

**Model / system.** The system models spatially separated quantum probes (remote ebit halves) interacting with local phases via Z-axis rotations, followed by an X-type noise channel. The structure is formalized using graph-code theory, exemplified by the [[5,1,3;2]] code.

**Key observables.** Phase-dependent measurement statistics from the $X_{B_j}$-containing stabilizer and the return-error syndrome from the $Z_{B_j}$-containing partner stabilizer.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The estimation error scales as $1/\sqrt{M}$ for large repetitions $M$, and the framework achieves the available quantum Fisher information.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal pre-shared ebits, the remote probes are the active sensing elements, and the return noise is restricted to independent X errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the graph-code realization (e.g., [[5, 1, 3; 2]]), the structure of the remote leaves, and the necessary adjacency blocks for defining the stabilizer generators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the need for the framework. It then details the mathematical structure using graph-code theory to define the necessary stabilizer measurements. Finally, it analyzes the measurement outcomes, proves the state preservation property, and analyzes the metrological scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum sensing requires spatially separated probes to acquire local parameters while maintaining compatibility with network-level quantum information processing. We develop an entanglement-assisted stabilizer framework based on the extended structure of entanglement-assisted quantum error-correcting (EAQEC) codes, in which the remote halves of pre-shared ebits are used directly as local phase probes while the joint state simultaneously carries an encoded logical subsystem. Each remote probe acquires a local Z-axis phase and subsequently returns through an X-type noise channel. Within the extended EAQEC stabilizer structure, the stabilizer containing $X_{B_j}$ provides phase-dependent measurement statistics, whereas its partner containing $Z_{B_j}$ records the corresponding return-error syndrome. A graph-code formulation is introduced to make this structure explicit, together with an illustrative [[5,1,3;2]] construction. We further show that, conditioned on the joint sensing-and-syndrome measurement record, the post-sensing state differs from the original encoded state only by a known Pauli transformation, so that the logical information remains available for subsequent encoded operations. For the local-phase model considered here, the stabilizer readout attains the available quantum Fisher information, while the finite-shot estimator approaches the corresponding $1/\sqrt{M}$ scaling as the number of repetitions increases. The framework therefore provides a common entanglement-assisted stabilizer structure for distributed local-phase sensing, restricted return-error identification, and post-sensing logical-state retention, without relying on an intrinsic metrological enhancement from EAQEC itself.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a robust, information-conditional auditing framework to assess whether claims made by Quantum Machine-Learning classifiers remain valid when subjected to real-world systematic uncertainties and finite quantum measurement noise. Using a collider physics benchmark, the authors show that simple classifier performance metrics are insufficient for scientific inference, concluding that no demonstrable quantum advantage exists over classical methods under their rigorous testing protocol.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on HEP, the rigorous statistical framework for certifying claims under systematic uncertainty and finite sampling is highly relevant to any field using quantum measurements or complex ML models where systematic error control is paramount.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the conditional validity of Quantum Machine-Learning (QML) classifiers when deployed in real-world high-energy physics inference pipelines, considering both classical systematic shifts and quantum measurement uncertainties.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that classifier validity does not guarantee scientific inference validity, and that apparent quantum advantages can be reproduced by matched classical controls, leading them to claim no quantum advantage.

**Method.** They develop an information-conditional, fail-closed auditing framework that rigorously tests claims by propagating results through various information sets ($I_0$ to $I_3$) and accounting for finite-shot quantum estimation noise.

**Model / system.** The analysis is benchmarked on collider physics data (Higgs-to-$	au	au$ benchmark) using quantum-kernel classifiers (e.g., fidelity kernel $K_Q$) trained on high-dimensional kinematic variables.

**Key observables.** Classifier metrics (e.g., AUC), signal-strength coverage, and the status of claims (SUPPORTED, REFUTED, UNRESOLVED) under various systematic variations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves multiple nuisance parameters ($	heta$) for feature-level systematics (TES, JES, soft-MET) and varying levels of signal-strength degradation ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes a 'frozen finite-population batch deployment' snapshot, and the validity guarantee is conditional on the declared sampling protocol and information sets.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the information-set hierarchy ($I_0 	o I_3$) and the fail-closed decision rule governing the auditing process.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for information-conditional certification, details the fail-closed auditing framework, applies it to collider data under systematic variations, and concludes by comparing QML results against classical controls to assess true advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Claims about a deployed quantum machine-learning classifier can fail when target data shift or when finite-shot quantum evaluation randomizes the model itself. We develop an information-conditional, fail-closed auditing framework that returns supported, refuted or unresolved verdicts with anytime-valid per-claim error control under a declared sampling protocol. On a Higgs-to-tau-tau collider benchmark, stable classifier metrics do not guarantee valid signal-strength inference: at the studied finite-template statistics, the fixed-template profile can lose coverage, even in shift-free controls, when its templates are estimated independently and template-statistical uncertainty is not modeled explicitly. Across 30 frozen finite-shot quantum-kernel deployments, every realized Gram matrix is propagated through refitting, calibration and threshold selection; in the primary raw pipeline these perturbations leave ranking nearly unchanged yet move thresholded target metrics by about 0.02, flipping ideal-anchored claims, and the diagonal-loading sensitivity decomposes differently. Matched classical controls remove apparent quantum-specific nominal-performance and sensing effects. We claim no quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates controlled quantum teleportation, focusing on two metrics: average fidelity and fidelity deviation. It demonstrates that while control can optimize average fidelity, the fidelity deviation behaves differently, showing that the benefit of control is not uniform across all input states or state classes. These contrasting effects highlight the complexity of characterizing quantum channel performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation into fidelity deviation provides a more nuanced diagnostic tool than average fidelity alone, revealing state-dependent effects of quantum control mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper contrasts how quantum control mechanisms affect both the average teleportation fidelity and the fidelity deviation in controlled quantum teleportation.

**Main result.** The fidelity deviation depends on the controller's measurement choice even when the average fidelity is maximized, and controller assistance does not universally reduce the fidelity deviation, especially for W-class states.

**Method.** The authors derive lower bounds for the fidelity deviation and compare controlled teleportation protocols with direct teleportation using reduced state analysis.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes controlled quantum teleportation involving three-qubit pure states ($ho_{ABC}$), where a controller performs a measurement on one qubit.

**Key observables.** Average Teleportation Fidelity ($F$), Fidelity Deviation ($\Delta$), Concurrence of Assistance ($C_a$), and the reduced state $ho_{BC}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Three-qubit pure states, W-class states, and GHZ-class states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed specifically for three-qubit pure states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows that for the W state, the fidelity deviation ($\Delta_{BC}$) varies substantially with the controller measurement, even when the average fidelity ($F_{BC}$) is constant. Figure 2 compares the difference in fidelity deviation with and without control for W and GHZ classes.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews standard teleportation fidelity, introduces and analyzes the fidelity deviation ($\Delta$) in controlled teleportation, and finally compares the effects of controller assistance on both $F$ and $\Delta$ for different state classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Teleportation performance is commonly characterized by the average teleportation fidelity, while its variation over input states provides additional information captured by the fidelity deviation. In controlled teleportation, the controller's measurement introduces an additional source of fidelity variation through its measurement outcomes. We investigate the fidelity deviation in controlled teleportation with three-qubit pure states. We derive a lower bound on the fidelity deviation and show that it is attainable together with the maximal average teleportation fidelity. Among the measurements attaining the maximal average fidelity, however, the fidelity deviation can vary depending on the controller's measurement. We further examine the effect of controller assistance by comparing controlled teleportation with direct teleportation using the reduced state. Unlike the maximal average fidelity, which cannot decrease with controller assistance, the minimal fidelity deviation is not necessarily reduced by controller assistance. In particular, for any W-class pure state, controller assistance cannot reduce the fidelity deviation. These results reveal contrasting effects of control on teleportation fidelity and its deviation in controlled teleportation.

</details>
