**Summary.** This paper explores how structural disorder in the Heusler alloy FeRuMnGe dictates its magnetic properties. By combining X-ray diffraction and DFT calculations, the authors show that antisite disorder introduces competing magnetic interactions. This competition ultimately drives the material into a characteristic cluster-glass magnetic state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation of disorder-driven magnetic frustration and the resulting glassy state in a crystalline solid provides a model system for understanding complex many-body interactions in disordered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how atomic disorder influences the magnetic ground state of the quaternary Heusler alloy FeRuMnGe, specifically focusing on the emergence of glassy magnetic behavior.

**Main result.** The research establishes a direct link where antisite disorder drives the system to exhibit competing magnetic interactions, resulting in a cluster-glass state coexisting with long-range magnetic order.

**Method.** The work combines experimental techniques like X-ray diffraction and magnetization measurements with theoretical calculations using DFT to model the effects of disorder.

**Model / system.** The system is the quaternary Heusler alloy FeRuMnGe, which adopts the LiMgPdSn-type structure. Disorder is modeled by considering B2-type antisite intermixing of Fe–Ru and Mn–Ge sites.

**Key observables.** B2-type antisite disorder, competing ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic couplings, cluster-glass state signatures (ZFC/FC separation), and changes in electronic character (half-metallic to metallic).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (especially below 161 K), magnetic field (up to 5 T), and the degree of antisite disorder (e.g., 25% to 50%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetic behavior is directly governed by the structural disorder introduced via antisite mixing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate PXRD patterns showing disorder, electronic band structures comparing ordered vs. disordered states, and magnetization curves showing ZFC/FC separation indicative of spin-glass behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of presenting experimental evidence of disorder (XRD, magnetization), followed by theoretical modeling (DFT calculations of electronic structure and magnetic interactions) to explain the observed magnetic frustration and glassy state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this combined experimental and theoretical study, we investigate the role of disorder in governing the magnetic ground state of the quaternary Heusler alloy FeRuMnGe. In the FeRuMnZ (Z = Ga, Si) series, chemical substitution modifies atomic ordering and electronic structure, resulting in distinct magnetic ground states. Motivated by this, we extend the series to FeRuMnGe. X-ray diffraction reveals B2-type antisite disorder, where Fe--Ru and Mn--Ge intermix. Theoretical calculations show that such disorder modifies exchange interactions, leading to competing ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic couplings, and drives the system from half-metallic to metallic. Magnetic measurements reveal competing interactions, giving rise to a cluster-glass state coexisting with long-range magnetic order. The absence of a thermodynamic signature at $T_f$, together with ac susceptibility and relaxation measurements, supports the presence of short-range magnetic interactions and a glassy magnetic state. The compound exhibits an enhanced magnetic response below $\sim 161$ K and a maximum magnetization of $\sim 1.64~μ_B$/f.u. at 5 T. Overall, this work establishes a direct correlation between antisite disorder, competing exchange interactions, and glassy magnetism in quaternary Heusler alloys. Combined experimental results and theoretical calculations reveal that disorder drives the system from an AFM-dominated state in FeRuMnSi to an FM-dominated state in FeRuMnGe, providing deeper insight into the role of the extent of disorder in governing the magnetic properties of QHAs.

</details>

### [GadIR: A Spatial-Topology Preserving Compiler for Quantum Many-Body Systems Simulation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01771v1)

**Authors:** Xiangyu Ren, Yuexun Huang, Zhaohui Yang, Yuchen Zhu, Tsung-Wei Huang, Tsung-Yi Ho, Zhiding Liang, Antonio Barbalace  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01771v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces GadIR, a novel intermediate representation designed to compile quantum many-body Hamiltonians while explicitly preserving the underlying spatial topology of the physical system. By using Pauli gadgets, the compiler frontend performs hardware-agnostic group reductions, leading to significant reductions in circuit overhead when synthesizing programs for various quantum hardware architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the crucial bottleneck of mapping complex physical Hamiltonians onto abstract quantum hardware instructions, providing a structured, topology-aware compilation pipeline relevant to all quantum simulation efforts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently compiling the Hamiltonians of quantum many-body systems into quantum circuits suitable for diverse quantum hardware architectures.

**Main result.** The proposed compiler, utilizing GadIR, achieves a significant reduction in compilation overhead across multiple quantum hardware platforms compared to previous state-of-the-art methods.

**Method.** The authors introduce GadIR, a Pauli gadget-based intermediate representation that explicitly preserves the spatial topology of the physical model. The compiler uses a hardware-independent group reduction frontend and a topology-guided backend for synthesis.

**Model / system.** The framework is tested on canonical quantum many-body systems, including the Bose-Hubbard model and Lattice Gauge Theories, which are mapped onto qubit Hamiltonians defined on specific spatial lattices.

**Key observables.** Compilation overhead reduction (e.g., CNOT/CZ count, depth, non-Clifford gate count) across superconducting, neutral-atom, FTQC, and MBQC backends.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity and structure of the physical Hamiltonian, the specific quantum hardware architecture targeted, and the lattice size/dimensionality.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that preserving the spatial topology via GadIR leads to a more optimized compilation path than standard Pauli string representations. The analysis relies on specific encoding techniques (e.g., Jordan-Wigner) and Trotterization approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow from physical model to Hamiltonian to the GadIR representation, comparing the topological awareness of the proposed method against prior art. Other figures show quantitative comparisons of gate counts and circuit metrics across different hardware backends.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of existing Pauli string representations, proposes the GadIR IR to retain spatial topology, details the compiler frontend (group reduction) and backend (Trotterization/scheduling), and validates the approach across multiple physical models and quantum hardware backends.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating quantum many-body systems has been one of the most important applications of quantum computation. For simulation, the Hamiltonian of a physical system is compiled into quantum programs with native instructions for quantum hardware. In previous works, the Hamiltonian is represented as Pauli strings, then compiled and optimized based on the quantum circuit model. Such representation paradigm neglects the spatial topology of original physical models, which is vital information to reducing the overhead of compiling many-body systems Hamiltonians.   To address such neglect, we introduce a spatial-topology preserving compiler for quantum many-body simulation. Using Pauli gadgets as the representations of the Hamiltonian, we introduce our intermediate representation -- GadIR, to preserve the spatial-topology information of original physical models. Our compiler frontend performs the group reduction algorithm based on Pauli gadget model, which is a hardware-independent optimization. Our compiler backend performs trotterization and scheduling on Pauli gadgets, then synthesizes the Pauli gadgets into hardware-native quantum programs. We evaluate our compiler on all the canonical quantum many-body system models, while achieving a significant reduction on compilation overhead regarding four major quantum architectures. Overall, our spatial-topology preserving IR exploits the compilation optimization space for quantum many-body systems Hamiltonian.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, efficient computational framework to optimize Quantum State Tomography (QST) by reformulating the measurement setting selection as a Graph Coloring problem. By applying heuristic graph coloring algorithms, the authors drastically reduce the number of required measurements compared to exhaustive methods. This enables practical, priority-based scheduling for characterizing complex quantum systems on NISQ devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a scalable, computational framework for optimizing the data acquisition stage of quantum characterization, which is crucial for experimental quantum computing platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently determining the minimal and prioritized set of measurement settings required for Quantum State Tomography (QST) for an arbitrary set of operators.

**Main result.** The framework successfully uses Graph Coloring heuristics to substantially reduce the number of required measurement settings compared to brute-force methods, enabling priority-based scheduling that maximizes information gain.

**Method.** The problem is reformulated as a Graph Coloring (GC) problem, solved using efficient heuristic algorithms like DSATUR and RLF, followed by synthesizing Clifford circuits for joint measurements.

**Model / system.** The work is validated on multi-qubit (up to five qubits), multi-qutrit (up to three qutrits), and hybrid qubit-qutrit systems, focusing on reconstructing the density matrix $\rho$.

**Key observables.** The estimated density matrix ($\rho_{est}$), the number of required measurement settings (color classes), and the information gain per experiment.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the Hilbert space (up to 5 qubits/3 qutrits) and the number of operators to be measured.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the problem can be effectively modeled and solved using graph coloring heuristics, bypassing the intractable nature of brute-force search.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the workflow: partitioning observables via GC, compiling quantum circuits, and using scheduling to reconstruct the density matrix, showing fidelity improvement with more settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews standard QST, introduces the GC formulation for measurement setting optimization, and details the measurement-scheduling strategy for maximizing information gain.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A key task in many quantum-computing applications, e.g., quantum simulation and quantum state tomography (QST), is to partition an arbitrary set of operators into mutually commuting subsets for efficient measurements. However, brute-force approaches to this task quickly become intractable as the number and dimensionality of operators grow. Here, we reformulate operator partitioning as a graph-coloring (GC) problem and develop an efficient computational framework to solve it, balancing accuracy and efficiency. Our framework enables leveraging a range of GC algorithms, which we benchmark for operator partitioning. Then, we demonstrate their utility in optimizing QST experiments, where determining non-overlapping data acquisition settings for QST is a major challenge, and prioritizing among these settings, i.e., selecting the experiments that provide the most information. We further show how to perform these experiments by synthesizing Clifford circuits for joint measurement of commuting Pauli operators in multi-qubit systems. We validate our framework across multi-qubit (up to five qubits), multi-qutrit (up to three qutrits), and hybrid qubit-qutrit systems. Our results show that heuristic GC methods substantially reduce the number of required measurement settings for QST and enable priority-based scheduling that maximizes the information gain per experiment. The optimization converges within minutes on a student-grade laptop, providing speedups of several orders of magnitude over brute-force methods already for these relatively small quantum systems. This demonstrates the potential of GC heuristics as a scalable and practical tool for characterization of noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices. We have made the Python implementation of our GC framework to optimize and schedule QST experiments publicly available at https://github.com/ssm8015/QST_GT.git.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01940v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates that the effective interactions between microwave-shielded dipolar molecules are not simply the sum of pairwise interactions. By analyzing the many-body potential energy for small clusters, the authors show that non-additive, many-body effects dominate the attractive potential well, which is critical for forming self-bound quantum clusters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the limitations of simple mean-field or pairwise interaction models in strongly correlated quantum systems, which is crucial for designing stable quantum matter like dipolar BECs.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the validity of assuming pairwise additivity when calculating the effective interactions between multiple microwave-shielded dipolar molecules.

**Main result.** The pairwise approximation fails for planar configurations, as many-body effects significantly deepen the attractive potential well, necessitating the use of the full many-body potential energy.

**Method.** The authors calculated the many-body Potential Energy Surface (PES) for three and four molecules, comparing the full calculation against approximations based only on pair interactions.

**Model / system.** The system consists of dipolar molecules interacting via dipole-dipole forces, whose interactions are modified (shielded) by an external microwave field. The analysis uses the Born-Oppenheimer approximation and focuses on the resulting effective many-body Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** The depth and position of the attractive potential well, and the repulsive barrier, as a function of molecular geometry (planar vs. out-of-plane).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of molecules (N=3, 4), the geometry (planar vs. out-of-plane), and the strength of the microwave field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to single-MW shielding and assumes the validity of the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) for time-averaging.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PESs for 3 dipoles in an equilateral triangle and 4 dipoles in a square, showing the dependence on side length and highlighting the many-body nature of the attractive well.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general Hamiltonian formulation to the effective time-independent Hamiltonian via RWA, then systematically compares the full many-body PES against lower-order approximations (pairwise, triplet) for small clusters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Theoretical models of the effective interactions between microwave-shielded polar molecules typically rely on the assumption of pairwise additivity. In this work, we test the validity of this pairwise approximation by evaluating the exact many-body potential energy for configurations of three and four molecules. We demonstrate that this approximation fails for molecule configurations in the plane of the circularly polarized microwave field. In these planar geometries, many-body effects significantly increase the depth of the attractive potential well and shift the position of the repulsive barrier. Although out-of-plane interactions in three-dimensional arrangements weaken this non-additivity, the effective interactions between microwave-shielded dipoles are not a simple sum of pair interactions. In particular, their attractive well, crucial for self-bound clusters, can only be described by the full many-body potential energy.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work provides a detailed spectral analysis of three interacting bosons on a 2D lattice, determining the asymptotic behavior of bound states and the spectral gap in the strong-coupling limit. Using advanced spectral methods, the authors establish precise energy formulas and prove exponential localization of the ground state. These results are highly relevant for understanding quantum simulation experiments using optical lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous spectral analysis of few-body systems in optical lattices provides fundamental insights into quantum many-body physics, directly impacting the theoretical understanding of quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the spectral properties, including bound states and spectral gaps, of the three-boson Schrödinger operator on a two-dimensional lattice.

**Main result.** The study derives precise asymptotic formulas for the bound state energies at zero and pi quasimomenta, and establishes exponential localization of the ground-state wavefunction in the strong-coupling limit.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced spectral theory tools such as the Birman-Schwinger principle, invariant subspace decomposition, the discrete Agmon comparison method, and the Krein-Rutman theorem.

**Model / system.** The model is a three-boson system interacting via pairwise contact interactions on the two-dimensional integer lattice ($\mathbb{Z}^2$). The Hamiltonian is analyzed in both real space and momentum space.

**Key observables.** Bound state energies ($z_s(\mu)$), spectral gap ($\Delta(\mu)$), and the decay rate ($\alpha(\mu)$) of the ground-state wavefunction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\mu$) in the strong-coupling limit ($\mu 	o \infty$), and the total quasimomentum ($K=0$ and $K=\pi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on systematic invariant subspace decomposition and rigorous remainder estimates to control strong-coupling asymptotics; the sharp asymptotic rate for localization is conjectured.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes mention Table 1 comparing spectral properties across different few-body lattice systems, and Table 5 comparing Fredholm determinant asymptotics.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian via direct integral decomposition over quasimomentum $K$, focusing on the extremal points $K=0$ and $K=\pi$ to derive asymptotic results for bound states and spectral gaps.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the three-boson Schrodinger operator on the two-dimensional integer lattice with pairwise contact interactions. First, we obtain precise asymptotics of the two bound states below the essential spectrum at zero total quasimomentum in the strong-coupling limit mu to infinity: the ground state energy is -3 mu + 6 + O(1/mu), and the first excited state energy is - mu + C + O(1/mu), where C = 4 - delta approximately 3.96458, with delta determined by the transcendental equation b0(delta) = 1/(1+delta) involving the lattice Green's function. The associated spectral gap is 2 mu + O(1). Second, using the discrete Agmon comparison method and the Paley-Wiener theorem, we establish exponential localization of the ground-state wavefunction with a logarithmic upper bound on the decay rate alpha(mu) at most ln(3 mu) + O(1/mu), reflecting the bounded nature of the lattice dispersion; the sharp asymptotic rate is conjectured. Third, at quasimomentum pi, the parity symmetry is preserved, but the odd quadratic form becomes positive definite with a unique eigenvalue of order 1/mu that never reaches the Birman-Schwinger threshold, so the odd subspace yields only a virtual level. The even subspace supports exactly one bound state with energy -2 mu + 6 + 8/mu + O(1/mu^2), and the spectral gap to the essential spectrum is mu - 2 - 8/mu + O(1/mu^2). The reduction from two bound states at zero to one at pi preserves the total spectral flow and is a lattice-specific phenomenon. Our approach uses an invariant subspace decomposition of the Birman-Schwinger operator and the Krein-Rutman theorem to guarantee uniqueness and strict positivity of the ground state. These results have direct implications for quantum simulation of few-boson systems in optical lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum MeanFlow (QMF), a novel quantum generative model designed to overcome the sequential circuit overhead of standard Quantum Flow Matching (QFM) on NISQ devices. By learning the average velocity over a time interval, QMF enables single-step sample generation, making quantum generative modeling significantly more viable in practice. The work demonstrates that this single-shot approach maintains competitive performance while drastically reducing quantum resource consumption.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical implementation gap between theoretical quantum algorithms and current NISQ hardware constraints by proposing a fundamentally different, more efficient sampling mechanism.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing efficient generative sampling methods for quantum computation that overcome the high cost and sequential nature of multi-step sampling on Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum MeanFlow (QMF) formulation allows for single-step sample generation, significantly reducing the required quantum circuit evaluations per generated sample compared to standard Quantum Flow Matching (QFM).

**Method.** QMF is introduced as the quantum analogue of MeanFlow, learning the average velocity over a time interval rather than the instantaneous velocity, enabling a single-shot sampling process.

**Model / system.** The models utilize Parameterized Quantum Circuits (PQCs) to learn velocity fields in a latent space derived from data like MNIST. The process is benchmarked on IBM quantum computers, operating within the framework of quantum generative models.

**Key observables.** Image quality (compared across single-step QMF, multi-step QFM, and single-step QFM), and the number of quantum circuit evaluations required per sample.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The latent space dimension (32-dimensional vector), the number of time steps (for comparison), and the quantum circuit depth/ansatz parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that reformulating the generative process to use average velocity (QMF) can circumvent the hardware limitations imposed by sequential circuit execution required by instantaneous velocity methods (QFM).

**Figures summary.** The figures compare the sampling quality and efficiency of QFM versus QMF, highlighting QMF's single-shot capability.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of sequential QFM sampling, proposes QMF based on MeanFlow theory, details the training using the MeanFlow identity loss, and benchmarks the method on MNIST, concluding with a comparison of computational cost.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum generative models offer a promising framework for exploring whether quantum computation can enhance generative machine learning. Flow matching is a generative method in which samples are generated by transporting a simple, known distribution to the target data distribution with a learned velocity field. Its quantum counterpart, known as quantum flow matching (QFM), was introduced recently, and, like its classical counterpart, requires integrating an ordinary differential equation over many time steps during inference. As each step requires the output from the previous step, the circuit submission is sequential and a drawback on quantum computers as they have high input/output costs. To alleviate this problem, we introduce Quantum MeanFlow (QMF), the quantum analogue of the MeanFlow formulation, which allows single-step sample generation. While the QFM learns an instantaneous velocity field at each time step, QMF learns the average velocity over a time interval. We use a parameterized quantum circuit to learn these velocity fields and benchmark the two methods on the MNIST dataset. We show that while single-step QMF has lower image quality compared to multi-step QFM, it performs better than the single-step QFM sampling at every shot count. Both of our models are executed on IBM quantum computers and best-of-N rejection sampling recovers most of the accuracy lost to device noise without modifying the circuit. This is especially advantageous for QMF which has only one circuit evaluation per image. Here, We establish QMF as a viable method for single-step quantum generative sampling, saving on quantum circuit evaluations per generated sample.

</details>

### [Reliable Sample-Level Quantum Error Mitigation via Dominance-Aware Clustering](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01744v1)

**Authors:** Mohsen Ghodrati, Kausthubh Chandramouli, Dror Baron  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01744v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

Curves show mean recall and 95% confidence intervals over five independent streams. The clustered and uniform sweeps end at S = 131,072 and S = 32,768, respectively.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel, classical post-processing technique called dominance-aware (DA) refinement for Quantum Error Mitigation. It tackles the difficult problem of recovering specific quantum states (bitstrings) from noisy measurement distributions. By combining responsibility thresholding with a local dominance screen, the method significantly improves the accuracy of center recovery in simulated quantum optimization tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical post-processing, the rigorous statistical framework for identifying underlying quantum states from noisy measurements is highly relevant to understanding the limitations and potential of quantum characterization techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of performing reliable quantum error mitigation (QEM) when the goal is to recover specific bitstrings (samples) from noisy measurements, rather than just estimating expectation values.

**Main result.** The proposed dominance-aware (DA) refinement, combining responsibility thresholding and a local dominance screen, successfully recovers true centers in simulated MaxCut-QAOA experiments, significantly outperforming standard clustering methods.

**Method.** The core method involves formulating the problem as a mixture model and applying DA refinement, which uses source responsibilities and a dominance score to ensure that majority voting recovers the correct center with exponentially decreasing error probability.

**Model / system.** The model assumes the measured probability mass is a mixture of contributions from several latent, task-relevant bitstrings (centers), each associated with a source and characterized by specific bit-flip error rates.

**Key observables.** Dominance Score ($\Delta_A^k$), Precision ($	ext{P}_C$), Recall ($	ext{R}_C$), and the combined F1 score ($	ext{F}_C^1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the bitstring ($n$), the number of centers ($K$), and the noise parameters (bit-flip rates $\epsilon_{k,i}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dominance condition (source $k$ contributing $>50\%$ probability mass at every coordinate) is a sufficient, but not necessary, condition for successful center recovery.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but the evaluation benchmarks include synthetic $n=10$ streams, QAOA on $n=10$ MaxCut instances, and high-dimensional $n=100$ tests.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework for sample-level QEM, introduces the DA refinement module (combining responsibility thresholding and local dominance screening), and validates this approach through extensive synthetic and simulated QAOA benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many quantum algorithms for classically difficult optimization tasks must return high-quality bitstrings from finitely many circuit executions, whereas most quantum error-mitigation methods target expectation values. We study sample-level recovery when measured probability mass is distributed around multiple latent bitstrings, called centers. Each component of the measured probability mass is called a source and we assume that each center is associated with one source. We identify dominance-at every coordinate, more than half of a retained region's probability mass comes from one source and agrees with its center-as a sufficient condition under which majority voting recovers that center with exponentially decreasing error probability. We show that nearest-center assignment, as used in clustering algorithms such as the $k$-modes algorithm, can fail to produce dominated regions even when the true centers are known. This failure motivates responsibility thresholding and a local dominance screen, whose combination we call dominance-aware (DA) refinement. Synthetic and simulated MaxCut-QAOA experiments show that DA refinement favors precision, while $k$-modes with DA refinement improves overall center recovery. All procedures are classical post-processing and require no additional quantum-circuit executions.

</details>

</figure>

**Summary.** This work develops a thermodynamic theory for the Electric Toroidal Dipole (ETD), a bulk order parameter characterizing ferroaxial electronic phases. By coupling the ETD to the spin-induced polarization, the authors establish a direct, quantitative relationship between the ETD and the intrinsic longitudinal spin conductivity. This provides a new theoretical tool to diagnose and quantify ferroaxial order in crystalline solids.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on topological/symmetry physics, the rigorous derivation of a bulk, gauge-invariant order parameter and its direct link to a measurable transport coefficient (spin conductivity) provides a powerful theoretical tool for characterizing novel electronic phases in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of defining a gauge-invariant, bulk thermodynamic order parameter for the Electric Toroidal Dipole (ETD) in periodic crystals, and linking it to intrinsic longitudinal spin transport.

**Main result.** The ETD is shown to be a finite order parameter exclusively in ferroaxial phases, and its chemical-potential derivative is directly proportional to the intrinsic longitudinal spin conductivity.

**Method.** The authors formulate the ETD thermodynamically by coupling a spatially varying electric field to the relativistic spin-induced electric polarization, utilizing Green's function and Kubo formalism techniques.

**Model / system.** The study uses a minimal ferroaxial extension of the Kane--Mele model, which incorporates symmetry-breaking hopping terms ($H_{FA}$) to realize ferroaxial order in a honeycomb lattice.

**Key observables.** Electric Toroidal Dipole (ETD, $G_k$), intrinsic longitudinal spin conductivity ($\sigma_{z}^{xx}$), and the longitudinal spin current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ferroaxial coupling strength ($\lambda_{FA}$), the chemical potential ($\mu$), and the band gap size (controlled by hopping parameters like $t'$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conventional definition of ETD fails in periodic crystals due to the ill-defined position operator, necessitating a thermodynamic formulation. The analysis also uses semiclassical Boltzmann theory in some contexts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the honeycomb structure and the effect of ferroaxial hopping on the band structure. Subsequent figures show the dependence of the ETD ($G_z$) and spin conductivity ($\sigma_{z}^{xx}$) on the chemical potential and the strength of the ferroaxial coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the thermodynamic definition of the ETD. It then applies this framework to the Kane-Mele model, demonstrating that the ETD is non-zero only when ferroaxial symmetry is broken. Finally, it derives a quantitative relationship linking the ETD's derivative to the longitudinal spin conductivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electric toroidal dipoles (ETDs) characterize ferroaxial order, yet their bulk definition in periodic crystals has remained elusive because conventional multipole operators involve the ill-defined position operator. Here we formulate a thermodynamic ETD by coupling a spatially varying electric field to the relativistic spin-induced electric polarization. The resulting expression is gauge invariant and provides a bulk order parameter for ferroaxial phases. We further establish a direct relation between the chemical-potential derivative of the ETD and the intrinsic longitudinal spin conductivity in insulating systems. To demonstrate the formulation, we construct a minimal ferroaxial extension of the Kane--Mele model. The ETD becomes finite exclusively in the ferroaxial phase and is strongly enhanced near a small band gap, accompanied by a sizable longitudinal spin current. Our results establish a thermodynamic theory of ETDs in crystalline solids and identify the longitudinal spin conductivity as a direct transport manifestation of ferroaxial order.

</details>

Circles are extracted from simulations (α ≤0.9; nqw ≤19); the solid line in (a) is D(L=2) A from Eq. (30), and the stars in (b) are the exact DM from Eqs. (S23) and (S31) at integer nqw ≤1280. The pole of Eq. (32) appears as a plateau, marked by dashed lines at (2 − √</figcaption>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes hybrid quantum walks, which combine quantum and classical random walk steps. By comparing two distinct operational protocols (additive vs. multiplicative), the authors show that while both lead to diffusion, the specific temporal organization dictates the amplitude of the diffusion coefficient in the quantum limit. This highlights that the structure of the quantum-classical coupling is crucial for determining transport dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The comparison between the two protocols (QRW-A vs. QRW-M) provides deep insight into how the temporal ordering of quantum and classical operations dictates the macroscopic transport properties, a key concept in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the universal scaling and protocol-dependent amplitude of diffusion coefficients in hybrid quantum-classical random walks.

**Main result.** Both additive and multiplicative hybrid quantum walks exhibit diffusive spreading for any classical admixture, and their diffusion coefficients diverge with a first-order pole in the quantum limit, with the pole amplitudes differing by a factor of two.

**Method.** The analysis uses the transfer operator method in momentum space, determining long-time behavior from the leading eigenvalue of the evolution operator for both additive (QRW-A) and multiplicative (QRW-M) protocols.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid quantum walk on a 1D ring, combining unitary quantum steps (using Hadamard coin) with classical steps. Two protocols are compared: additive (QRW-A) and multiplicative (QRW-M).

**Key observables.** Mean Squared Displacement (MSD), transport exponent ($eta$), and the long-time diffusion coefficient ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantum admixture ($\alpha$ or $r$), coherence timescales ($t^*_A, t^*_M$), and the ratio of amplitudes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** QRW-A requires truncation of the transfer matrix at a finite size $L$, and QRW-M assumes the long-time density matrix approaches a specific form.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the MSD vs. time for both protocols, confirming the transition from ballistic to diffusive spreading.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by analyzing the evolution via a linear transfer operator in momentum space. It derives the leading eigenvalue expansion for both QRW-A and QRW-M, leading to explicit formulas for the diffusion coefficients in the large-time limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum walks spread ballistically but become diffusive with classical admixture. We compare two hybrid walks using identical quantum and classical steps at the same classical-step rate but ordered differently in time. Any nonzero admixture yields diffusion. Near the quantum limit, both diffusion coefficients have first-order poles, but their amplitudes differ by about a factor of two. The coherence timescale sets the pole order, while temporal organization sets the amplitude. We expect our analytical framework to apply broadly to quantum--classical hybrid systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes diffusive search processes in complex media by incorporating the realistic effect of finite target reactivity alongside stochastic resetting. It demonstrates that the optimal strategy for enhancing transport efficiency is not universal but depends critically on the specific kinetics of the reactive targets. The findings provide a crucial theoretical tool for understanding reaction-diffusion dynamics in systems where interactions are not instantaneous.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical diffusion, the mathematical framework involving non-equilibrium dynamics, boundary conditions, and optimization criteria for transport efficiency is highly relevant to open quantum systems where dissipation and boundary interactions are key.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how stochastic resetting affects diffusive transport in complex environments containing targets with finite reactivity, moving beyond the idealization of perfectly absorbing boundaries.

**Main result.** The target reactivity significantly modifies the optimization landscape, leading to distinct regimes where the optimal resetting rate becomes intrinsically dependent on the target's chemical kinetics.

**Method.** The authors employ theoretical analysis using the Fokker-Planck equation and renewal equations to derive expressions for first-passage time properties under both diffusion and intermittent resetting.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional diffusion process (Brownian motion) confined between two reactive targets. The process incorporates stochastic resetting, which periodically reinitializes the particle's position.

**Key observables.** Mean First Passage Time (MFPT), conditional MFPT, survival probability, and the optimal resetting rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target reactivity ($\Pi$), the ratio of reactivities ($\Pi_l / \Pi_0$), and the resetting rate ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption addressed is moving from perfectly absorbing targets (ideal) to partially absorbing targets (realistic, modeled via Robin boundary conditions).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the competition between diffusion, resetting, and target reactivity, showing parameter regimes where resetting enhances efficiency or where the MFPT dependence on the resetting rate is monotonic.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the basic diffusion process with reactive boundaries (no resetting) to incorporating the renewal equation for the survival probability under resetting, culminating in the analysis of optimal control parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study search processes in a complex environment using the strategy of stochastic resetting. A common occurrence in these environments is targets with finite reactivity. Stochastic resetting has emerged as a powerful mechanism for optimizing search processes by curtailing long, unproductive excursions inherent to diffusive dynamics. Most theoretical studies, however, assume perfectly absorbing targets -- an idealization that overlooks the finite reactivity commonly encountered in realistic chemical and biological systems. In this work, we investigate the interplay between stochastic resetting and finite-target reactivity in reaction - diffusion processes. Considering a one-dimensional system with multiple reactive targets, we demonstrated that the target reactivity modifies the optimization landscape. We uncover distinct regimes in which resetting enhances transport efficiency towards specific targets based on their chemical kinetics. As a consequence, the optimal resetting rate becomes intrinsically reactivity dependent. Our results identify target reactivity as a crucial control parameter governing stochastic transport and provide insights into reaction-diffusion processes in complex media.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously compares three quantum error-correcting codes (RHG, FFCC, rFFCC) for use in a photon-atom quantum computer designed for MBQC. By employing a circuit-level loss model that accounts for correlated photon loss, the authors determine that code performance is highly sensitive to the specific hardware connectivity and loss mechanism, rather than just the code's graph degree.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a highly detailed, hardware-aware analysis of QEC performance, moving beyond idealized IID loss models to incorporate realistic correlated error propagation from photonic loss in a photon-atom setting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the circuit-level loss performance of three quantum error-correcting codes (RHG, FFCC, and reduced FFCC) when implemented in a specific hardware architecture.

**Main result.** RHG achieves the highest circuit-level threshold under uniform loss, but the optimal code depends on the specific loss mechanism and connectivity, showing that lower graph degree is not universally superior.

**Method.** The authors use an architecture-aware circuit-level loss model to simulate error thresholds by analyzing how mid-circuit photon loss propagates correlated Pauli errors.

**Model / system.** The study is set in a compound photon-atom quantum architecture, utilizing reusable atom-cavity units to generate measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC) resources via near-deterministic photon-atom CZ gates.

**Key observables.** Circuit-level thresholds (e.g., 2.75% for RHG), Logical Error Rate (LER), and the comparison between bipartite and STAP generation schemes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective loss probability ($p$) associated with photon loss during CZ gates, STAP, and measurement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes photon loss is the dominant error mechanism, neglecting photonic Pauli errors, atomic loss, and intrinsic atomic errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of the codes (RHG, FFCC, rFFCC) and show logical correlation surfaces, while supplementary figures compare LER thresholds for different generation schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform and loss model, compares the code structures (RHG vs. FFCC variants), and then evaluates performance by comparing circuit-level thresholds and LERs across different generation schemes (bipartite vs. STAP).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compare the Raussendorf-Harrington-Goyal (RHG) code, the Foliated Floquet Color Code (FFCC), and the reduced FFCC in a compound photon-atom architecture that directly generates measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC) resources with near-deterministic photon-atom CZ gates. RHG serves as a natural benchmark, while the FFCC variants allow us to study whether reduced graph degree improves performance under an architecture-aware circuit-level loss model with delayed heralding and correlated bond-loss propagation. We construct two generation schemes compatible with the compound hardware and evaluate circuit-level thresholds under periodic boundary conditions. RHG achieves the highest circuit-level threshold, 2.75%, and its threshold falls below that of reduced FFCC only for large excess loss on intermodule CZ connections. RHG also achieves the lowest logical error rate in most resource-matched comparisons, but some low-loss windows favor reduced FFCC. Overall, we show that when the hardware supports the native gates and connectivity required for MBQC, the benefits of lower graph degree must be weighed against each code's intrinsic IID loss tolerance, generation-scheme details, and hardware-aware resource overhead.

</details>

### [Kelvin-Helmholtz instability in annular superfluids across the UFG-BCS crossover](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01831v1)

**Summary.** The authors study the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability in annular Fermi superfluids across the UFG-BCS crossover using time-dependent superfluid density functional theory. They find that while the instability growth rates match predictions in the unitary regime, the dynamics fundamentally change in the BCS regime due to competing anti-vortex proliferation. This offers crucial insights into the non-ideal, fermionic nature of superfluid dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a microscopic, fermionic benchmark for understanding hydrodynamic instabilities in quantum superfluids, directly addressing discrepancies between theory and experiment in a highly tunable quantum system.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability (KHI) in annular superfluids across the Unitary Fermi Gas (UFG) to Bardeen-Cooper-Schrieffer (BCS) crossover.

**Main result.** The KHI growth rates are qualitatively consistent with point-vortex models in UFG/moderate BCS regimes but systematically higher. In the deep BCS regime, the dynamics change due to faster proliferation of boundary-generated antivortices, which destroys the initial vortex necklace.

**Method.** Time-dependent superfluid density functional theory (TDSLDA) is used to simulate the real-time evolution of the superfluid system.

**Model / system.** The system consists of counter-rotating annular Fermi superfluids, modeled using a time-dependent extension of the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations. The analysis spans the crossover from strongly interacting UFG to weakly attractive BCS regimes.

**Key observables.** KHI growth rate ($\sigma$), characteristic timescale ($	au_{KHI}$), structure factor $S(m, t)$, and the density/order parameter evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relative flow velocity ($\Delta v$), interaction strength (parametrized by $\xi_{aF}^{-1}$), and temperature (up to $0.3 T_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of TDSLDA functional theory, which handles contact interactions via regularization; the initial state is assumed to be an idealized vortex necklace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the stages of KHI, show the exponential growth of the structure factor $S(m, t)$ over time, and depict the qualitative change in dynamics in the deep BCS regime due to anti-vortex proliferation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using TDSLDA, simulates the initial vortex necklace formation, analyzes the KHI growth rates across different interaction regimes (UFG $	o$ BCS), and finally details the qualitative change in dynamics observed in the deep BCS regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability of counter-rotating annular Fermi superfluids using time-dependent superfluid density functional theory. When the two initially separated superflows merge, a necklace of quantized vortices forms at their interface and subsequently becomes unstable. We extract mode-resolved instability growth rates and study their dependence on relative flow velocity, interaction strength, and temperature across the strongly interacting unitary regime and the weakly attractive BCS regime. In the unitary gas and moderate BCS regimes, the growth rates remain qualitatively consistent with the point-vortex model, though systematically at or above its predictions, with no significant dependence on interaction strength or temperature up to approximately $0.3 T_c$. Deeper in the BCS regime, the dynamics change qualitatively: boundary-generated antivortices proliferate faster than the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability itself, destroying the initial vortex necklace before the instability can fully develop. These results provide a microscopic benchmark for Kelvin-Helmholtz dynamics in fermionic superfluids and help constrain possible explanations of the existing experiment-theory discrepancy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how dimensionality controls the extreme nonlinear optical response, specifically High-Harmonic Generation, in strongly correlated cuprate insulators. By combining experiments on 1D and 2D materials with advanced Hubbard model simulations, the authors demonstrate that dimensionality dictates the coherence dynamics of charge carriers. The findings establish dimensionality and spin-charge coupling as critical control parameters for manipulating light-matter interactions in these complex quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a direct link between fundamental many-body physics (spin-charge coupling) and measurable, extreme nonlinear optical phenomena (HHG), which is highly relevant for understanding correlated quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the dimensionality of a strongly correlated electron system affects its extreme nonlinear optical response, specifically High-Harmonic Generation (HHG).

**Main result.** A pronounced dimensional contrast was found: quasi-1D $	ext{SrCuO}_2$ exhibits a robust, plateau-like HH spectrum, while quasi-2D $	ext{Pr}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ shows strong thermal suppression, linking this to dimensionality-dependent doublon–holon dephasing.

**Method.** The research combines experimental HHG measurements on cuprates with theoretical nonequilibrium simulations using the Hubbard model (iTEBD and DMFT).

**Model / system.** The physical systems are quasi-1D ($	ext{SrCuO}_2$) and quasi-2D ($	ext{Pr}_2	ext{CuO}_4$) charge-transfer insulators, modeled by an effective single-band Hubbard Hamiltonian. The dynamics are simulated using advanced quantum many-body techniques.

**Key observables.** High-harmonic generation (HHG) spectrum, temperature dependence of harmonic yield, and subcycle spectra revealing doublon–holon coherence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality (1D vs 2D), temperature (thermal suppression), and spin-charge coupling strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical analysis relies on mapping the system to an effective single-band Hubbard model and using approximations like NCA in DMFT.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare HH spectra and temperature dependence for 1D vs 2D systems, showing the characteristic plateau vs. monotonic decay. Subcycle spectra illustrate the time evolution of coherence, which is suppressed in 2D.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by contrasting 1D and 2D cuprates, presents experimental observations (HHG spectra and temperature dependence), and then uses nonequilibrium simulations (iTEBD/DMFT) to microscopically explain the observed dimensional contrast via spin-charge coupling effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dimensionality strongly affects elementary excitations in correlated quantum materials, yet its impact on extreme nonlinear optical responses remains largely unexplored. Here, we combine high-harmonic generation (HHG) experiments on quasi-one-dimensional SrCuO$_2$ and quasi-two-dimensional Pr$_2$CuO$_4$ with nonequilibrium simulations of Hubbard models. We find a pronounced dimensional contrast: SrCuO$_2$ exhibits a robust plateau-like high-harmonic spectrum with weak temperature dependence, whereas Pr$_2$CuO$_4$ shows a monotonic decrease in harmonic yield and strong thermal suppression, especially at higher harmonics. The simulations qualitatively reproduce these trends and identify dimensionality-dependent doublon--holon dephasing, governed by spin--charge coupling, as their microscopic origin. These results establish dimensionality and spin--charge coupling as key control parameters for extreme nonlinear optical responses in correlated insulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses advanced DFT+DMFT calculations to map the electronic structure of nickel dichalcogenides (NiX2). By fitting to experimental photoemission and XAS data, the authors revise the understanding of the charge-transfer energy, concluding that substantial charge transfer occurs from the chalcogen ligands to the Ni centers. This work provides a detailed picture of how orbital interactions govern the electronic properties across the NiS2-xSe_x solid solution.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state materials, the use of DMFT to model strong local correlations and the detailed analysis of orbital hybridization and charge transfer mechanisms are highly relevant to understanding open quantum systems and correlated dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electronic structure and charge-transfer mechanism in nickel dichalcogenides (NiX2) to understand their insulating behavior and Metal-Insulator Transitions.

**Main result.** The calculations reveal a smaller charge-transfer energy than previously thought, leading to significant ligand-to-Ni charge transfer and a reduction in the Ni local magnetic moment.

**Method.** The research employs Density Functional Theory combined with Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DFT+DMFT) to model electron correlations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is NiS2 and NiSe2 in the pyrite structure, with the solid solution NiS2-xSe_x being analyzed. A tight-binding model spanning Ni 3d, S/Se p\pi, and p\sigma states is used within the DFT+DMFT framework.

**Key observables.** Valence-band photoemission spectra (PES), Ni 2p core-level PES, Ni L-edge XAS, and the Ni local magnetic moment.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The double-counting correction ($\mu_{dc}$) is a key parameter determined by fitting to experimental PES data; the charge-transfer energy ($\Delta_{dp}$) is also crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on fitting the double-counting correction to reproduce experimental spectra, and simplified models are used to isolate orbital contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show DFT spectral densities for NiS2 and NiSe2, and simulated K-edge absorption spectra comparing results for different assumed charge-transfer energies.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using DFT+DMFT, calibrates the model against multiple experimental spectra (PES/XAS), derives key electronic parameters (like the CT energy), and then analyzes the orbital hybridization and spectral evolution across the NiS2-xSe_x series.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the electronic structures of NiS$_2$ and NiSe$_2$ using density functional theory combined with dynamical mean-field theory (DFT+DMFT). A realistic electronic structure within DFT+DMFT was determined by optimizing the double-counting correction to reproduce experimental valence-band photoemission spectra. The validity of the present model is further confirmed by its successful description of the Ni 2$p$ core-level photoemission and Ni $L$-edge x-ray absorption spectra of NiS$_2$. Our results reveal a smaller charge-transfer energy than previously assumed, resulting in substantial ligand-to-Ni charge transfer and a reduced Ni local moment. We clarify how the relative position and interaction between the Ni upper Hubbard band and the antibonding chalcogen-dimer states shape the evolution of the low-energy electronic structure across the NiS$_{2-x}$Se$_x$ series.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to determine the sensitivity limit of using a fluorescent protein as a spin-1 qubit to detect neural radicals. The analysis shows that the sensor's performance is fundamentally limited by its own intrinsic, phonon-mediated relaxation rate. By modeling the relaxation dynamics and proposing environmental stiffening, the authors provide concrete pathways to improve the qubit's coherence time for practical biological sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced open quantum system theory (Redfield/Lindblad) to a solid-state, bio-molecular quantum sensor, providing quantitative design rules for enhancing coherence times in a complex, noisy environment.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a detection-limit theory for using a fluorescent-protein spin-qubit (FPSQ) to sense paramagnetic neural signaling radicals via spin relaxometry.

**Main result.** The native room-temperature sensor is limited by its intrinsic, phonon-limited T1, falling short of physiological sensitivity by 6 to 8 orders of magnitude. Improving the chromophore environment stiffness by 2x is predicted to recover T1 sufficient for micromolar sensing.

**Method.** The authors derived a transition-resolved Redfield relaxation matrix for the spin-1 qubit coupled to a diffusing radical bath, analyzing the resulting relaxation dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is an enhanced yellow fluorescent protein (EYFP) acting as an optically addressable spin-1 qubit in its metastable triplet state. Sensing occurs via spin relaxometry, where the radical's fluctuating field shortens the qubit's longitudinal relaxation time (T1).

**Key observables.** Longitudinal relaxation time (T1), relaxivity ($\kappa$), and the effective relaxation rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phonon-limited intrinsic T1, correlation time ($	au_c$), and the ratio of Raman to direct relaxation processes ($\sim 720:1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the noise spectrum is Lorentzian and that the system can be treated in the single-exponential decay limit for fast radicals.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes mention comparisons to Lindblad simulations and Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) benchmarks.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (FPSQ), develops the theoretical framework (Redfield theory for spin relaxation), analyzes the limiting factors (phonon-limited T1), and proposes design rules for improvement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The demonstration that enhanced yellow fluorescent protein hosts an optically addressable spin-1 qubit in its metastable triplet state raises the prospect of genetically encoded quantum sensing at molecular length scales. We develop a detection-limit theory for using this fluorescent-protein spin qubit (FPSQ) to sense paramagnetic neural signaling radicals by spin relaxometry. We derive the transition-resolved Redfield relaxation matrix of the zero-field-split triplet coupled to a diffusing radical bath, establish the regime in which it collapses to a single exponential, and validate it against Lindblad simulations and nitrogen-vacancy benchmarks. Propagating the effects of photon shot noise, photobleaching-grounded photon budget, and finite measurement bandwidth, we find that the native room-temperature sensor falls short of physiological sensitivity by six to eight orders of magnitude with the bottleneck being the phonon-limited intrinsic $\Tone$. Analyzing the underlying direct and two-phonon Raman processes, we show that room-temperature relaxation is Raman-dominated by $\sim\!720\!:\!1$ and that, because the Raman coefficient scales as $v^{-10}$ with sound velocity, a $\sim\!2\times$ stiffening of the chromophore environment recovers $\Tone\sim\SI{100}{\micro\second}$, sufficient for micromolar sensing. Nanomolar sensing is obstructed by a direct-process ceiling of \SI{79}{\micro\second} that vibronic decoupling alone cannot breach. We obtain quantitative design rules, identify photon yield as a co-equal bottleneck, and propose a frequency-resolved protocol for chemical specificity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a generalized Foldy-Wouthuysen approach to systematically derive Non-Relativistic Effective Field Theories (EFTs). By first constructing a Lorentz-covariant relativistic EFT and then applying a generalized reduction, the authors ensure that hidden Lorentz invariance constraints are automatically incorporated. This method provides a robust tool for analyzing complex, low-energy physics in various quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on particle physics EFTs, the systematic handling of symmetry breaking and scale separation via generalized transformations is a powerful theoretical tool applicable to many-body systems where low-energy effective Hamiltonians are constructed.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a systematic method to derive Non-Relativistic (NR) Effective Field Theories (EFTs) for complex physical systems, especially when the underlying symmetries are difficult to track, such as in non-inertial backgrounds.

**Main result.** The authors propose a generalized approach involving constructing a full relativistic EFT first, followed by a generalized NR reduction using an extended Foldy-Wouthuysen transformation.

**Method.** The method involves a two-step process: 1) Constructing a Lorentz-covariant relativistic EFT, and 2) Applying a generalized NR reduction scheme to systematically derive the low-energy, non-relativistic Lagrangian.

**Model / system.** The framework is illustrated using Quantum Chromo-Electrodynamics (QCED) for inertial flat spacetime, but is claimed to be generalizable to theories involving non-Standard Model physics, such as axion couplings or Dirac fields in curved spacetime.

**Key observables.** The resulting Lagrangian terms, organized by powers of momentum/energy, and the emergence of hidden Lorentz invariance constraints.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-relativistic limit ($|p| \ll m$), the characteristic scale $\Lambda$ associated with heavy degrees of freedom, and the coupling constants of the underlying gauge theories (QED, QCD).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes the existence of a well-defined NR limit and relies on the success of the generalized FW transformation to correctly map the relativistic structure to the non-relativistic one.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically contrasts the proposed derivation route (relativistic EFT $ightarrow$ generalized NR reduction) against direct NR construction, showing the systematic nature of the new method.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of the generalized approach, details the two-step methodology (relativistic EFT construction followed by NR reduction), applies this method to QCED to reproduce known results, and concludes by discussing its general applicability to complex theories.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Effective field theories (EFTs) are a powerful framework for performing high-precision calculations at reduced complexity compared to their fundamental counterparts. A particularly important class of EFTs arises in the non-relativistic (NR) regime. Their construction relies on a different realization of the underlying symmetries, since Lorentz invariance is no longer manifest in covariant form in the NR regime. This behavior imposes a link between certain matching coefficients, and therefore additional constraints, commonly referred to as hidden Lorentz invariance. These constraints are established in quantum field theories on inertial flat spacetime, such as NR quantum electrodynamics. However, deriving these constraints becomes considerably more involved for theories involving physics beyond the Standard Model or formulated in non-inertial spacetime backgrounds, where the hidden symmetry structure is less transparent. In this work, we present an approach to obtain the NR EFT by first constructing a relativistic EFT and then performing a generalized NR reduction based on an extended Foldy-Wouthuysen transformation. We illustrate this method by a quantum chromo-electrodynamics EFT for inertial flat spacetime, describing both electromagnetic and strong interactions, and show how it reduces to the established Lagrangian of NR quantum chromodynamics and electrodynamics. The hidden Lorentz invariance emerges as a direct consequence of the construction. This approach provides a route to obtain the NR limits of more complex theories, \eg Dirac fields in non-inertial spacetime or extensions involving physics beyond the Standard Model. As an example, we apply the method to add the coupling of a pseudoscalar axion field in a simplified model and derive its NR limit.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> FIG. 4. Out-of-distribution learning and generaliza- tion. All error bars denote the standard deviation over ten random seeds. (a) OOD angular mean absolute error of tied CCA using the complete two-probe pairwise-fidelity represen- tation F, the representation regularized by normalized second moment F + eq−, and a control in which eq−is shuffled across examples. Results are shown for exact observables, finite- shot sampling, the moderate gate-error model, and an equal- total-shot comparison. (b) Exact-expectation probe-number boundary. For generic drifting overlap, a balanced third fi- delity probe restores identifiability. When the relevant tem- poral modes are exactly orthogonal, however,...</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper proposes moving beyond pairwise fidelity measurements in Quantum Representation Learning by introducing a higher-order observable, $q^-$. This observable captures multi-state interference effects that are invisible when only pairwise overlaps are considered. By demonstrating that $q^-$ can distinguish between physically distinct quantum states (like different topological orders) and significantly improve phase estimation accuracy, the authors establish a new, powerful signal for quantum machine learning.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the limitations of current quantum machine learning protocols by providing a concrete, measurable, and trainable observable that captures higher-order quantum correlations beyond simple pairwise overlaps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum Representation Learning (QRL) often loses crucial relational information by relying only on pairwise transition probabilities (fidelity). The goal is to identify and train on higher-order, phase-sensitive relational invariants.

**Main result.** A multi-state observable, $q^-$, built from coherent overlap amplitudes, successfully encodes inequivalent modular data even when all pairwise fidelities are identical. This observable significantly improves phase generalization in out-of-distribution settings.

**Method.** The authors introduce a 'batch operator' ($\Sigma^-$) to construct $q^-$, which is then shown to be measurable via two-copy interference and trainable using parameter-shift gradients within a variational learning framework.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes quantum states, specifically using relational quartets derived from topological codes (toric-code and double-semion states) and a four-photon benchmark, to test the limits of information extraction.

**Key observables.** Pairwise fidelities ($F_{ij}$), the normalized second moment ($q^-$), and the mean out-of-distribution phase error ($	ext{MAE}_{	ext{ang}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The continuous variation of the phase angle ($\varphi$) in the complex-Hadamard family, and the total shot budget for measurements.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that higher-order, multi-state observables ($q^-$) capture information lost when only pairwise fidelities are used as the learning interface.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from pairwise fidelity to multi-state interference via $q^-$, show circuit implementations for measuring $q^-$, and compare the performance of different representations (fidelity vs. coherent) in OOD phase estimation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the information bottleneck (pairwise fidelity), introducing the higher-order observable $q^-$, demonstrating its physical measurability and trainability, and finally validating its superiority over fidelity in distinguishing topological orders and improving generalization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum contrastive, metric, and self-supervised learning often expose encoded quantum states to the learner through transition probabilities, especially fidelity. Quantum states are known to possess higher-order relational invariants, but their consequences for learned representations remain unclear. Here we show that a transition-probability-only learning interface can possess exact continuous blind directions in certain quantum-state families. We recover this missing information with a batch operator, built from coherent overlap amplitudes and negative masking, where its second moment $q_-$ retains four-state interference. Moreover, $q_-$ is directly measurable through two-copy interference and can enter variational learning via methods like parameter shift. In relational quartets derived from toric-code and double-semion states, this fidelity-blind signal encodes inequivalent modular data despite identical pairwise fidelities. Finally, in a four-photon benchmark with preparation drift, augmenting all six pairwise fidelities at two orthogonal probes with the corresponding normalized $q_-$ reduces the mean out-of-distribution phase error by $86\%$ at equal total shot budget. These results establish multistate relational observables as measurable, trainable, and physically consequential signals for quantum representation learning beyond pairwise fidelity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces BP-DMRG, a novel method that merges Belief Propagation with the DMRG algorithm to calculate ground states of quantum many-body systems. It overcomes the limitations of standard DMRG by allowing calculations on arbitrary lattices, demonstrated successfully on the TFI model. While highly accurate, the authors caution that the method's reliability degrades significantly when the underlying graph contains loops or when operators are non-definite.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable numerical technique (BP-DMRG) to tackle quantum many-body problems on complex geometries where traditional DMRG methods fail due to dimensionality or lattice structure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently calculating the contraction of large Tensor Networks (TNs) for ground-state problems in quantum many-body systems, especially on higher-dimensional or arbitrary lattices.

**Main result.** The combination of Belief Propagation (BP) with DMRG (BP-DMRG) successfully extends DMRG to arbitrary lattices, achieving high fidelity (0.9-0.99) for the transverse-field Ising model on a $2	imes 2$ hexagonal lattice.

**Method.** The authors combine the DMRG optimization framework with the Belief Propagation algorithm to approximate the difficult TN contractions required for calculating state norms and energy expectation values.

**Model / system.** The work is benchmarked on the transverse-field Ising model (TFI) and is designed for general quantum many-body systems whose ground states are represented by Tensor Networks (TNS).

**Key observables.** Fidelity (to the true ground state) and the relative error of energy estimates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lattice structure (e.g., $2	imes 2$ hexagonal lattice) and the transverse field strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accuracy of BP suffers significantly in the presence of loops in the TN graph, and convergence is not guaranteed for non-definite operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the message update process for TN contraction, the calculation of the approximate contraction value $Z_{BP}$, and the construction of TNOs for the TFI model on hexagonal lattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the challenge of TN contraction, then details the use of BP as an alternative to exact contraction. It formalizes the combination into BP-DMRG, demonstrates its application to the TFI model on specific lattices, and concludes by discussing the inherent limitations related to graph topology and operator definiteness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tensor networks have attracted much attention as a powerful tool for modeling quantum many-body systems. Their contraction is a significant challenge, however, especially in highly connected networks, as memory requirements become prohibitive and the optimal contraction order is increasingly hard to find. The belief propagation (BP) algorithm has emerged as an alternative to exact contraction. Being formulated in a graph-agnostic way, it offers great flexibility, but its accuracy suffers in the presence of loops. In this work, we combine BP with the DMRG algorithm to solve ground-state problems, thereby extending DMRG to higher dimensions and arbitrary lattices. We demonstrate the viability of BP-DMRG on the transverse-field Ising model on a $2\times 2$ hexagonal lattice, finding that it produces states with a fidelity between $0.9$ and $0.99$ to the true ground state, and energy estimates with a relative error between $10^{-2}$ and $10^{-3}$. Additionally, BP-DMRG can find ground states on randomly generated lattices, with fidelity improving as the transverse field increases. We conclude with a discussion of the limitations we encounter when using belief propagation, highlighting that the TFI tensor network operators lead to larger errors during BP iterations in BP-DMRG.

</details>
