**Summary.** This paper details a method to implement high-fidelity quantum gates in superconducting qubit arrays by utilizing Floquet engineering with periodic driving pulses. By mapping the problem to quasi-energy resonance conditions and optimizing parameters, the authors achieve robust gate operations that scale to larger systems. This framework provides a powerful theoretical tool for advancing practical quantum hardware control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges advanced time-periodic control theory (Floquet engineering) with the practical engineering challenges of superconducting quantum hardware, providing concrete, scalable protocols for quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving high-fidelity quantum gate operations and state transfer protocols in superconducting multi-qubit processors.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated high-fidelity iSWAP gate synthesis in a three-qubit system and extended the protocol to a seven-site chain using periodic Gaussian driving pulses.

**Method.** The work employs Floquet theory to analyze the time-periodic Hamiltonian, mapping the control problem onto quasi-energy resonance conditions, which are then optimized using CMA-ES.

**Model / system.** The system is an extended superconducting linear chain of transmon qubits (scaling from three to seven sites), modeled by a time-dependent Hamiltonian driven by periodic pulses.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F_{i	o f}$), quasi-energy spectrum, and the ability to achieve clear scale separation from energy-relaxation times ($T_1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kick strength ($\lambda$), driving period ($T$), static coupling strengths ($g_1, g_2$), and the disorder parameter ($\eta \sim 10^{-3}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially uses the idealized delta-like pulse model, but extends to finite-width Gaussian pulses; stability is benchmarked against static parameter disorder.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit scheme, the poor performance of the undriven system, and the optimized, high-fidelity state transfer achieved via Floquet driving.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian, applying Floquet theory to derive the stroboscopic evolution operator, optimizing parameters via CMA-ES to achieve resonance, and finally benchmarking the resulting gate fidelity against realistic hardware imperfections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Precise control of multi-qubit architectures remains a critical bottleneck in superconducting quantum processors. In this work, we investigate the synthesis of high-fidelity quantum operations and state transfer protocols within an extended superconducting linear chain, scaling from three to seven sites. Using Floquet theory, we model the periodic drive as a train of delta-like pulses, mapping the quantum control problem onto quasi-energy resonance conditions. Combining the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff expansion with Floquet spectral decomposition, we analytically identify optimal driving parameters, refined via the Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES). In the three-qubit architecture, this enables high-fidelity synthesis of the iSWAP gate. Extending to a seven-site chain, we implement periodic trains of finite-width Gaussian pulses to activate distinct double-excitation transport channels with ultra-short gate durations t_gate (~170 ns). This achieves a clear scale separation from energy-relaxation times (T1) typical of fixed-frequency transmon devices with tunable couplers, such as IBM Quantum hardware. Finally, we benchmark stability under realistic imperfections, revealing a heightened sensitivity to static parameter disorder at the sub-percent level (eta ~ 10^-3) driven by spectral crowding, and discuss how closed-loop topologies could mitigate this constraint. This framework bridges time-periodic control theory and practical quantum gate engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a comprehensive co-design framework for fault-tolerant quantum computation using a spin-qubit shuttling bus. By optimizing the physical layout based on logical error correction needs, the authors demonstrate how to achieve all-to-all connectivity and efficiently execute complex logical gates. This establishes a viable path for scaling quantum processors in solid-state systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, hardware-aware framework for scaling fault-tolerant quantum computation, directly linking the abstract requirements of QEC codes to the physical constraints of solid-state qubit platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the open challenge of co-designing physical quantum architectures that simultaneously support robust quantum error correction (QEC) and efficient, scalable logical quantum computation.

**Main result.** They establish a principled co-design framework using spin-qubit shuttling buses, demonstrating that the architecture can achieve all-to-all logical connectivity and optimize complex circuits like magic state distillation.

**Method.** The authors employ Quantum Reverse Mapping methodology at the logical level, optimizing the physical layout based on the requirements of the QEC code and logical gate operations.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a multi-qubit spin-qubit shuttling bus architecture, which is extended from 1D to a 2D grid. This system uses distinct 'storage' and 'operation' zones for qubit management.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), inter-qubit distance, and the efficiency of logical gate operations (e.g., transversal CNOT).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), encoding ratio ($m$), and the physical dimensions (1D vs. 2D grid) of the shuttling tracks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the feasibility of coherent spin shuttling to perform logical gates and the ability to optimize the physical layout based on logical requirements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the synthesis of the architecture using Quantum Reverse Mapping, showing the transition from single-element to multi-element structures, and detailing the shuttling paths for transversal gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the need for co-design, then detailing the physical implementation (shuttling bus, ancilla sharing), optimizing the structure using Quantum Reverse Mapping, and finally demonstrating its utility by applying the framework to optimize a complex circuit like magic state distillation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computing requires not only reliable logical qubit storage, but also the ability to perform high-fidelity logical operations between error-corrected qubits at scale. While much of the existing literature focuses on optimizing syndrome extraction for a single logical qubit, the co-design of physical architectures that support both robust error correction and efficient logical computation remains an open challenge. In this work, we propose a multi-qubit spin-qubit shuttling bus architecture that addresses both requirements simultaneously. The architecture optimizes the physical qubit layout for syndrome extraction and supports transversal two-qubit logical gates between an arbitrary number of logical qubits, achieving all-to-all logical connectivity through coherent spin shuttling. We further propose an ancilla-sharing scheme that encodes multiple logical qubits within a single logical element, compressing the physical footprint of the processor and improving long-range gate fidelity. Extending the architecture from a one-dimensional bus to a two-dimensional grid of shuttling tracks reduces the inter-qubit distance, yielding consistent improvements in logical error. Finally, we apply the Quantum Reverse Mapping methodology at the logical level to optimize the layout of a \textit{15-to-1} magic state distillation circuit, demonstrating how the transversal capabilities of the proposed architecture can be leveraged for universal fault-tolerant computation. Taken together, these results establish a principled co-design framework that bridges the physical, error-correction, and logical computation layers of the quantum stack, and demonstrate that spin-qubit shuttling architectures are a viable and flexible substrate for scalable fault-tolerant quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a quantum algorithm to compute the radical of a Lie algebra, which corresponds to finding conserved directions in the dynamics of a closed quantum system. By encoding the necessary projector into a quantum circuit, it offers a resource-efficient method to test whether the system's center is trivial. This is significant for understanding the internal symmetries and conserved quantities governing quantum evolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the mathematical structure of conserved quantities (the center/radical) in quantum dynamics, providing a quantum computational tool to diagnose internal symmetries in closed quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum algorithm to find the radical (largest solvable ideal) of a Lie algebra, particularly focusing on the compact case where the radical is the center.

**Main result.** It constructs a quantum circuit that approximates the coefficient-space projector onto the radical, providing a controlled test for internal conserved directions in compact quantum dynamics.

**Method.** The approach uses quantum linear algebra techniques, specifically constructing a quantum circuit that implements a kernel projection, leveraging tools like QSVT to filter out zero-singular-value components.

**Model / system.** The system is the Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA) of a closed quantum system, which is treated as a compact Lie algebra generated by skew-Hermitian matrices within the unitary algebra u(N).

**Key observables.** The radical (center) of the Lie algebra, the coefficient-space projector, and the condition number ($\kappa$) of the associated operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The input requires certified metadata bounds on structure constants ($F, 	ext{sad}_j, 	ext{sint}$) and an error tolerance ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The test for a trivial center requires the promise that any nontrivial center has at least a stated minimum dimension.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from the general theory of radical finding via kernel projection to the specific implementation for compact algebras, deriving structural theorems regarding the condition number and culminating in a test for the triviality of the center.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Every finite-dimensional real or complex Lie algebra has a largest solvable ideal, its radical. In the compact dynamical Lie algebra (DLA) of a closed quantum system, this radical is the center; projecting onto it isolates directions that commute with the supplied algebra. With sparse Lie-bracket data and a known spectral gap, we construct a quantum circuit that approximately encodes this coefficient-space projector. The general construction combines the derived-algebra map with the Killing form, whereas compactness makes the Killing form alone sufficient. This distinction matters for numerical sensitivity, and our main theorem gives its exact law under an invariant-orthonormal basis adapted to the center and semisimple part. In a compact real algebra with a nonzero semisimple part, the condition number of the general operator is the three-halves power of the Killing-form condition number on that part. The compact projector in turn yields a bounded-error test for whether the center is trivial, provided that any nonzero center has at least a stated minimum dimension. This gives a controlled test for internal conserved directions in compact quantum dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed analytical characterization of the maximum quantum violation achievable for generalized GHZ states using the extended MABK inequality. By developing a correlation-tensor approach and utilizing singular value analysis, the authors establish a tight, piecewise analytic upper bound. This work is significant because it demonstrates how the extended inequality resolves ambiguities in the standard MABK test, providing a clearer separation between quantum and classical correlations even in partially entangled regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of entanglement witnesses via generalized Bell inequalities is fundamental to quantum information theory, providing precise measures of non-locality beyond standard entanglement measures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analytically characterizes the maximal quantum violation of the extended Mermin-Ardehali-Belinskii-Klyshko (EMABK) family of inequalities for $n$-qubit generalized Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states.

**Main result.** They derive a piecewise analytic upper bound for the quantum violation, which is saturated by constructing two complementary measurement strategies. Crucially, this method eliminates the non-violation region of the standard MABK inequality for partially entangled GHZ states.

**Method.** The authors employ a correlation-tensor approach, expressing the Bell value as a Frobenius inner product, and deriving the bound using singular value decomposition and the von Neumann trace inequality.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on $n$-qubit generalized GHZ states, $|\Psi(n)	hetaangle = \cos 	heta |0angle^{\otimes n} + \sin 	heta |1angle^{\otimes n}$. The analysis involves constructing and analyzing high-order correlation tensors ($T$) and effective coefficient tensors ($C$).

**Key observables.** The Bell value ($B$), the correlation tensor ($T$), and the effective coefficient tensor ($C$). The bound is governed by the two largest singular values of the reshaped correlation tensor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entanglement parameter $	heta \in [0, \pi/2]$, and the number of qubits $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on a rank constraint on the effective coefficient tensor, specifically assuming at least one party has only two measurement settings ($m_1=2$).

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the correlation-tensor formalism, derives the general upper bound using singular values, and then constructs two explicit measurement strategies (purely equatorial and hybrid) to demonstrate saturation of the bound across the entire parameter range.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analytically characterize the maximal quantum violation of the extended Mermin-Ardehali-Belinskii-Klyshko (EMABK) family of inequalities by $n$-qubit generalized Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states. We develop a correlation-tensor approach in which the Bell value is expressed as the Frobenius inner product of the quantum correlation tensor and an effective coefficient tensor. A rank constraint on the latter, together with the von Neumann trace inequality, yields an upper bound governed by the two largest singular values of the reshaped correlation-tensor. For generalized GHZ states, we determine the complete singular spectrum and obtain a piecewise analytic upper bound. We then construct two complementary measurement strategies that saturate the bound for EMABK throughout the entire parameter range. The first is a purely MABK strategy in which all measurement directions lie in the equatorial plane of the Bloch sphere. The second is a hybrid strategy that recursively combines lower-order MABK anti-diagonal operators with the fully-$σ_z$ tensor product. The resulting piecewise analytic expression recovers the standard MABK Tsirelson bound $2^{(n-1)/2}$ in the maximally entangled limit and approaches the classical local-hidden-variable bound in the product-state limits. It further shows that every entangled generalized GHZ state exhibits a strict quantum-classical separation under the EMABK inequality, thereby eliminating the nonviolation region of the standard MABK inequality in the partially entangled regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes correlation functions in Fluctuating Gaussian States exhibiting anti-unitary symmetries, identifying two competing ordered phases: a saddle point phase and a non-local contraction phase. The authors use advanced correlation techniques and entanglement bounds to determine which phase dominates, showing that the non-local phase imposes severe constraints on the system's structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between anti-unitary symmetries, the emergence of different ordered phases (saddle point vs. non-local contraction), and the constraints imposed by entanglement structure (PEPS/bond dimension) provides deep insights into the nature of quantum ground states and steady states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates bilinear order-parameter correlations in a specialized class of sign problem-free quantum systems known as Fluctuating Gaussian States (FGS). The core goal is to characterize the competition between different broken-symmetry phases.

**Main result.** The system generally exhibits two competing phases—a saddle point phase and a non-local contraction phase—whose dominance depends on the stability of the fluctuation saddle point. Furthermore, the non-local contraction phase imposes stringent constraints on the required entanglement structure.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating two-point correlation functions using Wick's theorem and decomposing observables into symmetry-related matrix bases. Theoretical bounds, such as those involving bond dimension, are also derived.

**Model / system.** The model is Fluctuating Gaussian States (FGS) possessing anti-unitary symmetries, applicable to both fermionic and bosonic systems. These states are defined via a path integral decomposition over imaginary time slices.

**Key observables.** Bilinear order-parameter correlations, two-point Green's functions, von Neumann entropy, and the average bond dimension ($\langle D_\phi angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The competition is governed by parameters related to the stability of the fluctuation saddle point, and the analysis involves varying coupling strengths ($\Delta$) and correlation lengths ($z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of anti-unitary symmetries and utilizes approximations like the spectral function approximation for low-temperature properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the relationship between anti-unitary symmetry and the order parameter set for different particle types, and a phase diagram is used to show the transition between the saddle point and non-local contraction phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the FGS framework and symmetries, calculating correlation functions for the two competing phases, deriving theoretical bounds relating entanglement to correlations, and finally discussing implications for non-equilibrium dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study bilinear order-parameter correlations in a general class of sign problem-free systems that are described by what we call Fluctuating Gaussian States (FGS) with anti-unitary symmetries, where the weight of each Gaussian measurement in the space-time path integral is positive-definite. Supported by Monte Carlo simulations on fluctuating Gaussian fermionic and bosonic examples, we argue that such systems generally exhibit two types of broken-symmetry phases: a \emph{saddle point phase} and a \emph{non-local contraction phase}, with the competition between the two determined by the stability of the fluctuation saddle point of FGS. Due to the Fermi liquid instability in fermionic FGS with anti-unitary symmetries, we also discuss the possibility of representing fermionic FGS with a particular Projected Entangled-Pair State (PEPS) ansatz, which we argue a certain construction provides an efficient description for the saddle point phase, but not for the non-local contraction phase. Finally, we discuss the potential implication of our result in non-equilibrium systems and stabilizing a target bilinear order.

</details>

**Summary.** This study develops a robust computational framework to define ecological 'sites' from raw GPS tracking data for two raven species. By applying this framework and modeling movement using the Individual Mobility Model, the authors successfully quantify distinct space-use strategies, showing that the species differ in their rates of site discovery and spatial predictability.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous statistical framework developed for quantifying memory and site fidelity in complex, non-equilibrium spatial processes (like animal movement) shares conceptual parallels with modeling memory effects or non-Markovian dynamics in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of defining ecologically meaningful residence sites from raw GPS tracking data, which is complicated by heterogeneous sampling and uncertain boundaries.

**Main result.** The study reveals distinct space-use strategies between two raven species: Brown-necked ravens show higher site entropy and a higher rate of novel site discovery compared to Fan-tailed ravens.

**Method.** It employs a three-stage method involving greedy nearest-neighbor clustering on stop detections (derived from speed thresholds) to create non-overlapping, polygon-based site visit sequences.

**Model / system.** The system is the movement ecology of two sympatric raven species (Fan-tailed and Brown-necked) in the Dead Sea region, analyzed using the non-Markovian Individual Mobility Model (IMM).

**Key observables.** Site visit sequences, IMM parameters ($eta$ and $\alpha$), site entropy, and conditional entropy of site-to-site transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Maximum site size ($	ext{Rmax}$), stop detection thresholds ($v_{	ext{trig}}$), and the parameters governing the IMM ($eta$ for novel discovery, $\alpha$ for preferential return).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that sites are repeatedly and collectively used, and the method assumes that the non-Markovian IMM accurately captures the balance between novel site discovery and preferential return.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spatial distribution of inferred stops using three different detection methods (speed-threshold, residence-time, recurse) and compare cluster-diameter distributions across these methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first details the site inference framework (greedy clustering on stop detections) and then applies this framework to model movement dynamics using IMM. It includes extensive validation by comparing the results against established, but computationally slower, site detection methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central challenge in movement ecology is to describe ecologically meaningful residence sites from raw tracking data due to heterogeneous sampling frequency and uncertain site boundaries. Here, we develop a greedy nearest-neighbor clustering approach with local reassignment and polygon-based site construction that generates timestamped sequences of site visits for each tracked individual, and apply it to nine years of GPS data from two sympatric raven species in the Dead Sea region---the Fan-tailed raven (\emph{Corvus rhipidurus}) and the Brown-necked raven (\emph{C. ruficollis}). Using the resulting visitation sequences, we quantify recursion patterns using the non-Markovian individual mobility model (IMM), which captures the balance between novel-site discovery $β$ and preferential return $α$. The inferred dynamics are consistent with IMM predictions and reveal clear interspecific differences: Brown-necked ravens continue to discover new sites at a higher rate (lower $β$), whereas Fan-tailed ravens show a steeper concentration of visits among top-ranked sites. Entropy analyses further separate the species, with Brown-necked ravens exhibiting higher site entropy and higher conditional entropy of site-to-site transitions. Together, these results provide a robust framework for describing interspecific differences in movement strategies that can be used to infer memory from GPS data and suggest distinct space-use strategies in two closely related sympatric species.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the long-time decay of survival probability for Brownian motion in a wedge with partially reactive boundaries. Using matched asymptotic analysis, the authors derive the power-law decay exponent and explicitly calculate the prefactor for specific wedge angles. The work provides deep insights into boundary-induced transport phenomena in confined geometries.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of anomalous diffusion and boundary effects in confined geometries is crucial for understanding transport phenomena in mesoscopic systems, which has relevance to quantum transport and open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the long-time power-law decay of the survival probability of a Brownian particle diffusing in a planar wedge whose sides exhibit partial reactivity.

**Main result.** The long-time survival probability decays as $S_q(t|\mathbf{x}_0) \simeq B_\alpha(q\mathbf{x}_0) (q/\sqrt{Dt})^{-\pi/\alpha}$, and the prefactor $B_\alpha$ is explicitly determined for specific angles $\alpha = \pi/n$.

**Method.** The primary method is matched asymptotic analysis, relating the long-time behavior to a stationary harmonic Robin problem near the apex. Numerical methods (FEM) and conformal mapping are also used to verify conjectures.

**Model / system.** The system is a Brownian particle diffusing in an infinite planar wedge of angle $\alpha$. The boundaries are modeled by Robin boundary conditions, introducing a characteristic length scale $q^{-1}$ related to the partial reactivity.

**Key observables.** Survival probability $S_q(t|\mathbf{x}_0)$, persistence exponent ($\pi/\alpha$), and the prefactor $B_\alpha(q\mathbf{x}_0)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wedge opening angle $\alpha$, Robin parameter $q$, and time $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on matched asymptotic expansions, assuming separation between the reaction scale ($q^{-1}$) and the diffusion scale ($\sqrt{Dt}$).

**Figures summary.** Table 1 provides numerical values for connection coefficients $1/v_{
u,R}(0, 0)$ and compares them to the conjectured theoretical value $1/\Gamma(1 + 
u)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the asymptotic formula for survival probability using matched asymptotics, derives explicit prefactors for specific angles, and then uses numerical/conformal mapping techniques to confirm conjectures regarding the connection coefficients and derive the PDFs for boundary local time and first-crossing time.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the power-law decay of the survival probability of a Brownian particle diffusing in an infinite planar wedge whose two sides are partially reactive and described by Robin boundary conditions. We employ matched asymptotic analysis to relate the long-time asymptotics to a stationary harmonic Robin problem near the apex. This approach determines the persistence exponent for arbitrary opening angles and yields the prefactor explicitly for the family of wedges with $α=π/n$ ($n=1,2,\ldots$). A simple extension of the apex prefactor to arbitrary angles is conjectured and supported numerically. This asymptotic behavior describes the crossover to the well-known result for perfectly absorbing wedges. As immediate applications, we also deduce the long-time behavior of the probability density function of the boundary local time on wedge sides, as well as the probability density function of the associated first-crossing time.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes error accumulation in quantum processors by modeling them as dissipative random quantum circuits. Using fidelity as a diagnostic, the authors derive a universal scaling law for the average fidelity decay dependent only on dissipation strength and circuit depth. This provides essential theoretical insight into noise resilience for current quantum computing hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial theoretical tools for characterizing noise resilience in NISQ devices, showing how fundamental physical parameters govern decoherence regardless of specific gate implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how information degrades in quantum processors due to dissipation and decoherence within random quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The average fidelity decay is found to be universal, depending only on the dissipation strength, system size, and circuit depth, and is independent of the microscopic details like the Kraus rank.

**Method.** The authors use random-matrix approximations and derive closed-form expressions for the ensemble-averaged fidelity, analyzing the decay across different noise regimes.

**Model / system.** The model is a dissipative random quantum circuit (RQC) where each two-qubit gate is treated as a 'diluted unitary' channel, interpolating between the ideal unitary and a random dissipative channel.

**Key observables.** State Fidelity ($F$), which quantifies the deviation between the ideal and noisy output states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation strength ($\kappa$), coherent two-qubit gate-error strength ($\alpha$), and connectivity-error probability ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the random permutations with Haar-random global unitaries to derive an exact closed-form expression for the average fidelity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the circuit layer comparing faultless, dissipative, and combined dissipative/noisy circuits. Figure 2 compares the theoretical mean fidelity decay against different ensemble assumptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the noisy evolution via diluted unitaries, deriving the average fidelity using random matrix techniques, establishing the universal scaling laws, and finally deriving a closed-form expression incorporating multiple error parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Present-day quantum processors are open systems in which dissipation and decoherence degrade the information carried by a quantum state as it propagates through a circuit. We study this degradation in dissipative random quantum circuits, modeling each two-qubit gate as a diluted unitary that interpolates between the intended unitary operation and a random dissipative quantum channel. Using the fidelity between the ideal and noisy output states as a diagnostic, we show that sufficiently random unitary gates or dissipative Kraus operators lead to a universal decay of the average fidelity. This decay is determined only by the dissipation strength, system size, and circuit depth, while microscopic details of the gates and noise, including the Kraus rank, appear only in subleading corrections to higher moments. We derive a closed-form expression for the average fidelity in terms of three physically meaningful error parameters, dissipation strength, coherent two-qubit gate-error strength, and connectivity-error probability. Finally, we identify the cases when dissipative effects can be reproduced by an unitary-noise model and when dissipation-induced decoherence remains distinguishable from coherent noise at the level of the average fidelity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a multivariate extension of hyperdensity functional theory to analyze multiple correlated observables in inhomogeneous fluids. It derives exact theoretical relationships, such as hyper-Ornstein-Zernike equations, governing the statistical interrelations between these observables. By coupling this rigorous theory with machine learning, the authors provide a powerful, systematic tool for predicting complex many-body behavior from simulation data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the rigorous mathematical framework for calculating higher-order correlation functions and the explicit integration with machine learning for functional approximation are highly relevant methodologies for developing advanced theoretical tools in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a multivariate generalization of hyperdensity functional theory (HDFT) to systematically investigate the equilibrium properties and interrelations of multiple, arbitrary observables in inhomogeneous classical many-body systems.

**Main result.** The framework establishes universal density functionals for averages, variances, and covariances of these observables, and derives exact hyper-Ornstein-Zernike equations linking these correlation measures.

**Method.** The theory is built upon extending the Mermin-Evans classical density functional mapping to an extended multivariate ensemble, and its practical implementation is achieved by integrating the functional structure with supervised machine learning via neural networks.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to classical many-body systems, illustrated specifically by studying the clustering behavior in confined hard rod fluids.

**Key observables.** Hyperobservables ($\{\hat{A}_\ell\}$), equilibrium averages, variances, covariances, and hyperfluctuation profiles ($\chi_{\hat{A}}(\mathbf{r})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (via $eta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and the coupling constants ($\lambda_\ell$) associated with the hyperobservables.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on the formal structure derived from the Mermin-Evans map, and practical calculation requires approximating the functionals using machine learning techniques.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the multivariate HDFT framework via the extended ensemble, derives the universal density functionals and the hyper-Ornstein-Zernike equations, and then details the mathematical machinery using functional derivatives (Gateaux derivatives) to compute these higher-order correlation functions, concluding with an application to hard rod fluid clustering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hyperdensity functional theory facilitates the investigation of the equilibrium behavior of a general order parameter or statistical mechanical observable in spatially inhomogeneous classical many-body systems. The approach is based on applying the exact Mermin-Evans classical density functional mapping to an extended ensemble. Here we present the multivariate generalization for investigating simultaneously the properties and interrelations of several different hyperobservables of choice. The resulting framework gives rise to a systematic characterization and prediction scheme for general many-body phenomena. All pertinent equilibrium averages, variances, and covariances constitute universal density functionals, as we demonstrate explicitly. Associated one-body hyperfluctuation profiles quantify the degree of correlation of the local density with first- and second-order combinations of hyperobservables. These multivariate hyperfluctuation profiles are accessible in many-body simulations and they satisfy exact hyper-Ornstein-Zernike equations, which we derive from the minimization principle in the extended multivariate ensemble. The formal structure of the theory integrates naturally with supervised machine learning, which renders all hyperdensity functionals accessible in practice via training of neural networks on simulation data. We demonstrate all salient techniques using the illustrative case of clustering in confined hard rod fluids, thereby choosing the total number of particles and the largest cluster size as the representative hyperobservables of interest. Our numerical methodology enables the efficient and successful prediction of all statistical quantities induced by the chosen hyperobservables, which we verify via comparison to test data and which we attribute to the tight interplay of first-principles and machine-learning concepts that our general approach combines.

</details>

**Summary.** This work numerically investigates how amorphous solids respond mechanically when subjected to localized force dipoles, or 'pinching.' The study reveals a clear transition from linear elastic behavior at small deformations to complex plastic rearrangements at larger extensions. The results show that the resulting plasticity depends critically on the initial stability of the glass, providing a minimal local probe into the material's collective mechanical response.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics, the study of amorphous solids, yielding, and the transition between elastic and plastic regimes shares conceptual links with understanding energy landscapes and structural relaxation in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mechanical response of amorphous solids to localized force dipoles (pinching) under athermal quasistatic conditions.

**Main result.** The response transitions from an elastic, long-range power-law decay regime to a plastic regime where rearrangements can be localized (stable glasses) or system-spanning (poorly annealed glasses).

**Method.** Numerical simulations using athermal quasistatic methods, complemented by theoretical analysis based on linear elasticity theory and multipolar expansions.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an amorphous solid (model glass) composed of interacting particles, whose mechanical response is probed by applying a controlled force dipole (pinching).

**Key observables.** Displacement field u(x), plastic activity patterns (localized vs. delocalized), potential energy evolution, and the principal axis of plastic deformation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Imposed extension (tuning from elastic to plastic), glass stability (well-annealed vs. poorly annealed), and the force dipole strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the continuum description of linear elasticity remains valid down to particle-scale distances, and the protocol involves athermal quasistatic loading.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically show the pinching process (initial contact to force dipole separation) and illustrate the evolution of potential energy and displacement fields under different loading protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the elastic response to pinching, then analyzing the transition to plasticity based on glass stability, and finally introducing advanced diagnostics like the principal axis of deformation to characterize the resulting rearrangements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We numerically investigate the mechanical response of amorphous solids to localized force dipoles, referred to as pinching, using athermal quasistatic simulations of model glasses with varying degrees of stability. We employ a control parameter, the imposed extension, corresponding to the increase in rest length between the two pinched particles relative to their initial separation. Increasing this extension allows us to continuously tune the system from the elastic to the plastic regime. For small extensions, the response remains elastic. In this regime, the displacement field induced by pinching is well described by linear elasticity and exhibits a long-range power-law decay consistent with dipolar forcing. Averaged responses display anisotropic, quadrupolar-like patterns, with quantitative agreement between simulations and analytical predictions. This continuum description remains valid down to particle-scale distances. As the imposed extension increases, the response becomes plastic. Pinching can then trigger either localized or system-spanning rearrangements, depending on glass stability. Well-annealed glasses exhibit localized plastic events, whereas poorly annealed systems display delocalized cascades. We introduce a method to extract the principal axis of plastic deformation and analyze the associated displacement fields and plastic activity. Overall, our results demonstrate that pinching provides a minimal local probe of amorphous solids. The resulting response, governed by both glass stability and imposed extension, offers insight into the interplay between elasticity, elementary rearrangements, and the emergence of collective plasticity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models the training dynamics of deep neural networks using the framework of stochastic gradient flow and percolation theory. It shows that structural symmetries force the network to undergo discrete, block-wise collapses, manifesting as variance spikes. These transitions exhibit Discrete Scale Invariance, providing a quantitative link between optimization dynamics and critical phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between optimization dynamics (SGD) and concepts from statistical physics, such as percolation and phase transitions, provides a powerful analogy for understanding complex system organization, which is relevant to understanding emergent structure in quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the dynamics of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) in deep neural networks, aiming to understand how structural symmetries force the network to collapse toward simpler, invariant subnetworks.

**Main result.** The structural transitions are modeled as percolation processes leading to Discrete Scale Invariance (DSI), characterized by variance spikes and a geometrically scaling cascade.

**Method.** The authors model the optimization process using the Stochastic Gradient Flow (SGF) and analyze the structural evolution using percolation theory, supermartingale properties, and spectral entropy measures.

**Model / system.** The system is deep neural networks trained via SGD (and extended to Adam/AdamW). The dynamics are analyzed by mapping the optimization path onto a percolation process defined on the network's architectural symmetries.

**Key observables.** Variance spikes in a macroscopic order parameter, the structural order parameter $O(p)$, and the DSI scaling factor $\lambda$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise level ($\eta$), the number of merging components ($n$), and the conserved fractional spectral variance mass ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes stochastic attractivity (drift dominance over diffusion) near invariant sets, and in later sections, it assumes mean-field percolation baselines or the conservation of spectral variance mass.

**Figures summary.** Figures show experimental validation of the DSI scaling factor $\lambda \approx 2.00$ across controlled kinematic simulations ($K=3$) and empirical SGD runs ($K=6$), demonstrating a consistent scaling law.

**Paper structure.** The argument progresses from defining invariant sets and establishing stochastic attractivity, to modeling the structural collapse via percolation theory, and finally generalizing the findings to derive and empirically test the continuous fractional scaling law $\lambda_{	ext{eff}} = nm\sigma$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the dynamics of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD), which is known to steer deep neural networks toward invariant sets that correspond to simpler subnetworks. How this steering unfolds over time remains poorly understood. We answer this by modeling the stochastic gradient flow (SGF) as a percolation process, in which architectural symmetries force subnetworks to merge in discrete simultaneous blocks rather than one at a time. These structural transitions register as variance spikes in a macroscopic order parameter, echoing physical phase transitions. We further show this trapping mechanism and its associated scaling cascade extend to Adam and AdamW under an explicit heavy-tailed noise model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced geometric formalism using the N-bein to study the parameter space of quantum systems with degenerate spectra. It introduces a torsion tensor capturing non-commutativity and derives several gauge-invariant invariants. The framework is successfully applied to coupled harmonic oscillators, providing new tools to quantify correlations in degenerate quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general geometric machinery for analyzing the parameter dependence of quantum systems, especially those with degeneracies, which is crucial for understanding topological phases and quantum control.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends the N-bein formalism, originally for quantum parameter spaces, to analyze systems exhibiting degenerate spectra.

**Main result.** The formalism successfully defines new gauge-invariant tensors and invariants that quantify state transitions and correlations in degenerate quantum systems, even when the standard QGT breaks down.

**Method.** The authors employ concepts from Cartan geometry, introducing an N-bein as the 'square root' of the Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT) and defining a torsion-like tensor via the covariant derivative.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is applied to a concrete model of coupled harmonic oscillators immersed in an electric field, which naturally leads to a degenerate energy spectrum.

**Key observables.** N-bein, Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT), Torsion-like tensor, and several derived scalar invariants (e.g., $(N\Xi\Theta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parameters ($\mathcal{N}$), the quantum numbers defining the energy subspaces ($n_1, n_d$), and the coupling strengths in the oscillator model.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a slowly varying parameter dependence and relies on extending the formalism from Abelian to non-Abelian matrix-valued descriptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the degeneracy structure of the Hilbert space and show how state changes occur via parameter variation, particularly highlighting jumps in quantum numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the initial N-bein definition to analyzing the two-state QGT for transitions, introduces the torsion tensor, develops a geometrical formulation using differential forms, and concludes with calculating and analyzing multiple gauge-invariant scalar observables for the coupled oscillator model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, we introduced a geometric object analogous to an orthonormal frame in the Cartan formalism to study the parameter space of quantum systems; we called it $\mathcal{N}$-bein, with $\mathcal{N}$ being the number of parameters that characterize the quantum system. Acting as the ``square root'' of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT), the $\mathcal{N}$-bein allows us to define new tensors to improve our understanding of the structure beneath the parameter space of quantum mechanics. In this work, we extend this mathematical framework surrounding the $\mathcal{N}$-bein to analyze the parameter space of quantum systems with degenerate spectra. As in the non-degenerate case, we define a non-Abelian two-state QGT to identify possible transitions between degenerate states after two consecutive parameter variations. Additionally, using the Wilczek-Zee connection, we introduce a torsion-like tensor as the covariant derivative of the $\mathcal{N}$-bein. This torsion captures the noncommutativity of successive parameter variations and coincides with the antisymmetric part of the two-state QGT. We also present a geometrical formulation using differential forms and discuss the physical implications of the newly defined tensors. Furthermore, we construct several gauge-invariant observables from the $\mathcal{N}$-bein and its derivatives to highlight the utility of the new tensors. Finally, to illustrate the convenience and applications of this formalism, we apply the theoretical framework to a system of coupled harmonic oscillators immersed in an electric field. The coupling between the oscillators results in a degenerate system. Thus, using the new formalism, we found correlations among the quantum states quantified by the new invariants.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper solves the bound-state problem for a spin-1/2 fermion in a generalized harmonic oscillator potential incorporating scalar, vector, and tensor couplings. The authors derive exact wavefunctions using specialized mathematical techniques and determine the conditions for bound energy levels by analyzing the roots of the resulting energy equation. This work provides a comprehensive framework that unifies several previously studied, but distinct, relativistic quantum oscillator problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is relativistic quantum mechanics, the mathematical techniques used—analyzing the roots of complex transcendental equations derived from physical constraints—are highly relevant to finding exact solutions in complex quantum models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper calculates the exact bound-state wavefunctions and determines the conditions for the existence of bound solutions for a novel generalized harmonic oscillator problem involving a spin-1/2 fermion.

**Main result.** The general solution encompasses several known spherically symmetric cases, and the existence of bound states is determined by analyzing constraints on quantum numbers derived from the energy equation.

**Method.** The authors use advanced mathematical techniques, including solving coupled radial equations via specific Ansätze and employing real analysis on the resulting energy equation to determine the number of real roots corresponding to bound energies.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 fermion governed by a Dirac-like equation in 3+1 dimensions, subjected to simultaneous scalar, vector, and tensor couplings (including harmonic oscillator and Dirac oscillator potentials) acting in a plane of motion.

**Key observables.** Bound-state energy eigenvalues ($\varepsilon$), and the classification of solutions as particle or antiparticle.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential coupling constants ($\mu, \omega, a, b$), quantum numbers ($n, 	ilde{k}$), and the dimensionality of the motion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The potentials are assumed to be circularly symmetric, and the analysis relies on specific Ansätze for the radial functions and the mathematical rigor of analyzing the energy equation's roots.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference several tables (II through VII) detailing the conditions on quantum numbers required to determine the number of real roots (0, 1, or 2) for the energy equation under various potential parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the generalized Hamiltonian and restricting motion to a plane, deriving coupled radial equations, solving specific solvable configurations (I and II) that violate symmetries, and finally using real analysis on the resulting energy equation to provide a complete classification of bound states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The exact bound-state wavefunctions and the corresponding energy equation are calculated for a new generalized harmonic oscillator problem describing a spin-1/2 fermion in 3+1-dimensions, involving scalar, vector, and tensor couplings acting simultaneously within a particular plane of motion. For the scalar and vector coupling, singular harmonic oscillator shapes are considered, such that the singular term is needed to allow analytical solutions for the wavefunctions while preserving binding under adequate conditions. For the tensor sector,the Dirac oscillator potential is employed, which adds another independent binding mechanism to the problem. The exact bound-state solutions are computed by specifically tuning coefficients for an appropriate pair of \textit{Ansätze} for the radial functions, which leads to wavefunctions in terms of generalized Laguerre polynomials. Although the energy equation cannot provide a general expression for the energy spectrum, specific constraints on the quantum numbers as simple functions of the external potential parameters can be derived for it, which determines the conditions for bound solutions to exist, and of what type: particle, antiparticle or both. It is shown that the results can be simply mapped to the spherically symmetric analogue problem, and this is used to show that the general result encompass several previous particular cases of spherically symmetric harmonic oscillator problems in the Dirac equation available in the literature.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02139v1>

**Summary.** This work proposes using a Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) as a quantum oracle within a cutting-plane method to solve Semidefinite Programming (SDP) problems. While the quantum approach offers logarithmic qubit scaling, the study reveals that classical memory constraints and the need for full state tomography limit its practical advantage over classical solvers. It serves as a key case study for hybrid quantum-classical optimization algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, albeit limited, blueprint for integrating quantum computation (VQE) into a well-established classical optimization framework (cutting planes), which is highly relevant for near-term quantum algorithm development.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational bottleneck in solving Semidefinite Programming (SDP) problems, which are crucial in optimization theory.

**Main result.** The authors propose a VQE-based cutting-plane framework, showing that while the VQE oracle generates valid cuts, the overall advantage over classical methods is limited by classical memory bottlenecks and the need for state tomography.

**Method.** They integrate a Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) into a polyhedral outer-approximation cutting-plane algorithm to replace the computationally expensive linear-algebraic step of checking positive semidefiniteness.

**Model / system.** The model is the abstract mathematical problem of solving an SDP, $	ext{SDP (1.1)}$, constrained by the positive semidefinite cone ($	ext{S}_n^+$). The quantum component uses VQE to estimate the minimum eigenvalue of the candidate matrix.

**Key observables.** The minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}(X^*)$) of the candidate matrix $X^*$, and the resulting fraction of the initial optimality gap closed.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The qubit requirement scales logarithmically ($\lceil\log_2 Nceil$) with the matrix dimension $N$, contrasting with the classical $O(N^2)$ storage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve the feasibility of realizing the VQE oracle and the use of a polyhedral outer-approximation scheme, which does not guarantee finite convergence.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the construction of the end-to-end cutting-plane loop, where the separation oracle is realized by the VQE, followed by comparative analysis against exact methods and discussion of practical hardware limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semidefinite programming plays a key role in optimization, with broad impact across control theory, machine learning, and combinatorial optimization. Although semidefinite programs are polynomially solvable, several commonly used algorithms rest on a linear-algebraic step whose running time grows cubically with the matrix dimension and which requires the matrix itself to be held in memory, at quadratic cost. In this study, we propose replacing it with a variational quantum eigensolver, whose qubit requirement is logarithmic in the matrix dimension, and present the first end-to-end implementation of such an approach within a cutting-plane framework, together with an operator-derived ansatz whose entanglement structure is read directly from the Pauli support of the candidate matrix. Evaluated on the control family of SDPLIB against an identical scheme driven by an exact eigendecomposition, the variational oracle produces valid cuts throughout, closing 32 to 82% of the initial optimality gap against a near-constant 75 to 82% for the exact oracle. Implementing and measuring the method end to end surfaces several effects not visible from theoretical analyses alone: where memory is actually consumed, how the padding required to fit a matrix onto a quantum register can mislead the variational optimizer, and why the candidate matrices prove dense in the Pauli basis, reducing the operator-derived ansatz to full entanglement. We report these findings and discuss their implications for near-term hybrid quantum-classical approaches.

</details>

**Authors:** Lei-Yi-Nan Liu, Jian Cui  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.01993v1>

(b) Monte Carlo results for the algebraically decaying spectrum at p = 1, with L = log2 N. The fitted slope is 2.736 ± 0.017, approaching the asymptotic value 3. (c) Monte Carlo results for the C-L spectrum, with fitted slope 1.413 ± 0.020, approaching the predicted value 3/2.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper unifies the study of entanglement and nonlocal nonstabilizerness by developing a spectral framework based on the entanglement spectrum. It derives universal bounds for the nonlocal stabilizer Rényi entropy using generalized anti-flatness. The framework successfully predicts distinct scaling laws for various spectral types, offering a general tool for analyzing quantum correlations in many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful, general spectral tools to characterize complex non-local correlations (nonstabilizerness) in critical quantum systems, linking it rigorously to measurable quantities like entanglement entropy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unified spectral framework to understand the relationship between entanglement, anti-flatness, and nonlocal nonstabilizerness in quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The nonlocal SRE is shown to be controlled by complementary spectral structures derived from the entanglement spectrum, yielding universal scaling laws, such as double-logarithmic growth at 1D conformal critical points.

**Method.** The authors derive universal upper and lower bounds on the nonlocal stabilizer Rényi entropy using generalized anti-flatness and introduce a 'dyadic-shell sandwich construction' to bound the ordered entanglement spectrum.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on bipartite pure states in quantum many-body systems, specifically examining spectra that decay exponentially or algebraically, including the Calabrese--Lefevre spectrum relevant to 1D critical points.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal $\alpha$-Rényi Entropy ($	ext{M}_{	ext{NL}}^{\alpha}$), $\alpha$-Rényi Entanglement Entropy ($	ext{S}_{\alpha}$), and Generalized Anti-flatness ($\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** The decay exponent $p$ in algebraic spectra, the entanglement parameter $\alpha$, and the system size $N$ or length $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the properties of the entanglement spectrum and the applicability of spectral bounding techniques like the dyadic-shell sandwich construction.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention schematic illustrations of the sandwich construction and results comparing the asymptotic scaling of $S_2$, $A_2$, and $M_{NL}^2$ for different decay regimes ($p>1, p=1, p \in(0, 1)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the spectral framework by introducing bounds relating $	ext{M}_{	ext{NL}}^{\alpha}$ to $	ext{S}_{\alpha}$ and $\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}$. It then applies this framework to specific spectral types (exponential, algebraic, C-L) to derive concrete scaling laws and demonstrate the general applicability of the spectral tools.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement and nonstabilizerness capture distinct aspects of quantum complexity, yet their relation through the entanglement spectrum remains only partially understood. Here we develop a unified spectral framework for bipartite nonlocal nonstabilizerness. We introduce a generalized anti-flatness and derive universal upper and lower bounds on the nonlocal stabilizer Rényi entropy (SRE) in terms of Rényi entanglement entropy and spectral non-uniformity. We apply these bounds to exponentially and algebraically decaying spectra, revealing distinct relations between entanglement and nonlocal nonstabilizerness. For the marginal algebraic spectrum and the Calabrese--Lefevre spectrum, we further introduce a dyadic-shell sandwich construction that bounds the ordered entanglement spectrum by upper and lower shell-flat spectra and determines the asymptotic nonlocal SRE scaling. At one-dimensional conformal critical points, this yields a universal hierarchy of double-logarithmic scaling laws. Our spectral bounds and dyadic-shell sandwich construction provide general tools for analyzing nonlocal SRE, offering a flexible framework that can be applied to a wide range of entanglement spectra in quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the successful epitaxial synthesis of EuIn2Sb2, a material predicted to be a magnetic topological insulator. By characterizing its novel trilayer crystal structure and measuring its magnetic and transport properties, the authors establish it as a promising new platform for investigating axion and higher-order topological insulating phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of magnetic ordering (antiferromagnetism) and strong spin-orbit coupling in a crystalline solid is central to realizing topological phases, which is a major focus in modern condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally realize and characterize EuIn2Sb2, a predicted axion insulator and higher-order topological insulator candidate within the EuA2X2 family.

**Main result.** The authors successfully stabilized EuIn2Sb2 films via MBE, revealing a novel trilayer structure with an In-on-In stacking configuration, which makes it an ideal platform for studying magnetic topological phases.

**Method.** The study employed epitaxial growth using Molecular Beam Epitaxy (MBE), followed by structural characterization using XRD/TEM and magnetic/transport measurements using SQUID and four-probe methods.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the EuIn2Sb2 thin film, belonging to the EuA2X2 family of magnetic topological insulators. The investigation focuses on how strong spin-orbit coupling and the specific crystal structure stabilize exotic topological states.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal resistivity (rho_xx) showing a kink at TN=10.5K; magnetic susceptibility (chi); and structural confirmation of the trilayer phase via XRD RSM and TEM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic ordering temperature (TN=10.5K) and the strong spin-orbit coupling regime are critical parameters defining the topological nature of the material.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed trilayer structure and the presence of in-plane antiferromagnetic ordering are sufficient to establish the material as a candidate for axion/HOTI phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structures of EuA2X2 compounds, the successful growth and characterization of the trilayer EuIn2Sb2 film (using XRD/TEM), and the magnetic phase diagram showing temperature and field dependence of resistivity and magnetization.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the theoretical context of EuA2X2 topological insulators, detailing the MBE growth process to stabilize the novel trilayer EuIn2Sb2 structure, and finally characterizing its magnetic and transport properties to confirm its suitability for topological phase exploration.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Eu triangular lattice layer compounds described by the general formula EuA2X2 have attracted growing attention due to a wide range of layered crystal structures and corresponding magnetic topological phases, arising from the coexistence of large Eu magnetic moments and energetically inverted A and X bands. Among the EuA2X2 family, EuIn2Sb2 has been predicted to host robust axion insulator and higher-order topological insulator states by stronger spin-orbit coupling but has not been experimentally realized, including the structural identification. Here we report the epitaxial stabilization of EuIn2Sb2 by adopting the molecular beam epitaxy technique. Structural characterization reveals that EuIn2Sb2 films are based on a unique In-on-In stacking, forming a new trilayer structure distinct from previously predicted structures in the EuA2X2 family. In addition to the in-plane antiferromagnetic ordering at TN=10.5K, the stronger spin-orbit coupling and enhanced In-In hybridization in EuIn2Sb2 make it an ideal candidate of axion insulator and higher-order topological insulator phases. These results establish EuIn2Sb2 as a new member of the EuA2X2 family and offer an advanced platform for exploring these magnetic topological phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates the phase shift imparted to fast electrons by a laser field using three independent methods: Dirac theory, Volkov solutions, and the Hamilton-Jacobi equation. The key finding is the agreement across all formalisms, yielding a Lorentz-invariant result that is crucial for applications like laser phase plates in TEMs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous comparison between quantum field theory methods (Dirac/Klein-Gordon) and classical mechanics (Hamilton-Jacobi) to derive a single, Lorentz-invariant result for an electron-laser interaction is highly relevant for understanding strong-field quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits the calculation of the phase shift experienced by swift electrons as they pass through the electromagnetic field of a high-intensity laser.

**Main result.** The calculated phase shift is found to be independent of the relative orientation of the electron and laser beams, and the results from quantum (Dirac, Klein-Gordon) and classical (Hamilton-Jacobi) theories show agreement.

**Method.** The authors employ three distinct theoretical frameworks—perturbation theory on the Dirac equation, the Volkov solution to the Klein-Gordon equation, and the relativistic Hamilton-Jacobi equation—to calculate the phase shift.

**Model / system.** The system involves a relativistic electron interacting with a classical, time-varying electromagnetic field generated by a laser. The analysis is framed using the electron's 4-potential A and its resulting phase shift $\phi$.

**Key observables.** Laser-induced phase shift ($\phi$), which is the primary observable, and the electron's trajectory.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron energy ($E$), laser frequency ($\omega$), interaction time ($\Delta t$), and the degree of relativistic motion ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation often assumes that the scattering process leaves the electron and photon states unchanged, which is justified by considering the high photon density limit analogous to stimulated emission.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 graphically illustrates the boundary conditions for the radiation field A, comparing confinement in time versus a $	au$-parameterization, and showing the required correction factor.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results derived from three methods: perturbation theory on the Dirac equation, using Volkov solutions to the Klein-Gordon equation, and finally, employing the relativistic Hamilton-Jacobi equation to establish consistency and analyze Lorentz invariance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the calculation of the phase shift experienced by swift electrons on passing through the electromagnetic field of a laser. Such phase shifts are now utilized in the form of `laser phase plates' in transmission electron microscopes (TEMs), for example. We calculate the phase shift using three different methods, namely, perturbation theory applied to the Dirac equation, the Volkov solution to the Klein-Gordon equation, and the relativistic Hamilton-Jacobi equation. We find that all three methods are in agreement, and that the calculated phase shift is independent of the relative orientation of the electron and laser beams. The agreement between the quantum and classical theories is explained. Our Lorentz invariant result for the phase shift differs from certain results published in the literature.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel quantum entropy, the adjusted von Neumann entropy ($	ilde{S}$), designed to correctly quantify 'localizable information' in quantum systems, unlike the standard von Neumann entropy. The authors prove that $	ilde{S}$ is the unique entropy satisfying key physical constraints, and they use it to provide a definitive characterization of entangled Bell states. This advances the foundational understanding of information measures in quantum theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic tool ($	ilde{S}$) to distinguish physically meaningful entropy measures from mathematically defined ones, which is crucial for understanding entanglement and information flow in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper seeks to define a quantum entropy measure that accurately quantifies 'localizable information' in quantum systems, a property that standard von Neumann entropy lacks.

**Main result.** The authors prove that an adjusted von Neumann entropy, $	ilde{S}$, is the minimum quantum entropy satisfying the localizability monotonicity condition and uniquely characterizes Bell states.

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing the axioms of classical Shannon entropy with quantum entropy measures using finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras and establishing necessary and sufficient conditions via algebraic constraints.

**Model / system.** The study models finite quantum systems using finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras, $A$, which can possess superselection sectors. The focus is on bipartite systems described by states $ho \in A \otimes B$.

**Key observables.** Adjusted von Neumann entropy ($	ilde{S}$), Shannon entropy, and the characterization of Bell states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the system ($n$), which dictates the additive constant in the adjusted entropy ($\log n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras, and the definition of $	ilde{S}$ requires an additive correction to standard von Neumann entropy to achieve localizability.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the failure of standard von Neumann entropy to quantify localizable information, then proposing and proving the properties of the adjusted entropy $	ilde{S}$ based on monotonicity, and finally using $	ilde{S}$ to provide a structural characterization of Bell states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The idea that information can be localized is pervasive in physics. In thermodynamics, entropy flows from one reservoir to another, and in quantum gravity, the entropy content of some spatial regions is bounded. In quantum information theory, information is transmitted from one place to another, and entanglement can provide an advantage when there are constraints on such transmission.   Shannon entropy quantifies localizable information in the sense that marginalization defines an outer measure on the set of parts of a classical multipartite system. In contrast, von Neumann entropy does not quantify localizable information in this sense. However, a variant of von Neumann entropy, which originates in noncommutative geometry, does.   We prove that this adjusted von Neumann entropy is the minimum quantum entropy that quantifies localizable information. We also prove that this is the unique quantum entropy that characterizes Bell states in terms of redundant information, directly generalizing the classical case. We work with finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras throughout, modeling finite quantum systems that may have superselection sectors.

</details>

(b) Meyer-Neldel compensation plot of the pre-exponential factor D0 versus activation energy Ea; the line is a linear fit to ln D0 versus Ea (R2 = 0.997), corresponding to a compensation temperature TMN ≈358 K.</figcaption>
