<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02824_figures/2609.02824_fig4.jpg" alt="Figure 4">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> Figure 4: Temperature dependence of the solvent self-diffusion coefficients in the simulated 1 M LiPF6 electrolytes: (a) EC, (b) EMC and (c) FEC. Each species is shown for every composition in which it is present; colors follow the electrolyte color coding used throughout. Error bars denote one standard deviation of the windowed Einstein fit.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02824_figures/2609.02824_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 5: (a) NE and (b) GK ionic conductivities for 1 M LiPF6 in EC, EMC and EC/EMC (3:7, w/w), plotted against inverse temperature. Error bars denote one standard deviation; GK values and uncertainties are obtained with the STACIE spectral estimator.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work uses Molecular Dynamics simulations combined with Machine Learning to study how lithium-ion conductivity in battery electrolytes degrades at low temperatures. The researchers found that the transport penalty is highly dependent on the solvent blend and the concentration of FEC additives. By training ML models, they created a fast tool to predict these complex transport properties across different operating conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electrochemistry, the application of ML surrogates to model complex, multi-parameter physical phenomena (like conductivity vs. T and composition) falls under the umbrella of ML-physics, which shares methodological overlap with disordered systems analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the limitations imposed by low-temperature operation on lithium-ion transport in battery electrolytes, focusing on the coupled effects of solvent composition and fluorinated additives.

**Main result.** Cooling causes a pronounced transport penalty, particularly in EMC-containing electrolytes, and the study quantifies the temperature and composition dependence using ML surrogates.

**Method.** The research combines classical Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations with Machine Learning (ML) techniques, specifically Gaussian-process surrogates, to model transport properties.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1 M LiPF6 electrolyte containing mixtures of ethylene carbonate (EC), ethyl methyl carbonate (EMC), and fluoroethylene carbonate (FEC), simulated across temperatures from 298 K down to 233 K.

**Key observables.** Li+ self-diffusion coefficients, Nernst-Einstein (NE) and Green-Kubo (GK) conductivities, and local coordination analyzed via RDFs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (233 K to 298 K), FEC concentration (0-10 mol%), and solvent ratios (EC:EMC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that temperature and host-solvent composition are the dominant factors governing bulk transport response, and it notes that direct Li+-FEC coordination is not quantified.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows a representative initial simulation configuration for a specific electrolyte mixture, and other figures/tables detail the temperature dependence of density and transport parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically simulates the electrolyte across varying compositions and temperatures using MD, calculates transport metrics (D, $\sigma_{NE}$, $\sigma_{GK}$), and then employs ML surrogates to build predictive models for these properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-temperature operation imposes severe limitations on lithium-ion transport in battery electrolytes, yet the coupled effects of solvent composition and fluorinated additives in the cold-temperature regime remain insufficiently resolved. Here, we combine classical molecular dynamics (MD) and machine learning (ML) to investigate 1 M LiPF$_6$ electrolytes containing ethylene carbonate (EC), ethyl methyl carbonate (EMC), and EC/EMC (3:7), with 0-10 mol% fluoroethylene carbonate (FEC), from 298 to 233 K. MD simulations quantify Li$^+$ self-diffusion, Nernst-Einstein (NE) and Green-Kubo (GK) conductivities and local coordination, while Gaussian-process surrogates model conductivity across composition and temperature. Cooling produces a pronounced transport penalty, particularly in EMC-containing electrolytes, whose GK conductivity decreases by more than 98% at 233 K, compared with approximately 90% in EC-rich systems. Li$^+$ self-diffusion activation energies are 0.49-0.54 eV for EMC-containing systems and 0.27-0.29 eV for EC-based systems. Within the EC family, 0-2 mol% FEC gives comparable cold-temperature transport, whereas 5-10 mol% FEC shows lower conductivity retention at the coldest simulated temperature. The analyzed coordination channels remain solvent dominated, while direct Li$^+$-FEC coordination is not quantified in the present RDF set.The Gaussian-process surrogates achieve composition-disjoint cross-validated RMSE values of 0.56 and 0.57 mS cm$^{-1}$ for NE and GK conductivity. Within the simulated liquid-state trajectories, temperature and host-solvent composition dominate the bulk-transport response, with FEC acting as a secondary modifier.

</details>

### [Memory as an Energy Landscape---Hopfield](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02195v1)

**Authors:** Nima Dehghani  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02195v1>

**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. A selective lineage of associative-memory theory. Hopfield did not invent threshold neurons, Hebbian association, correlation memories, or the first recurrent binary network. The 1982 synthesis made content-addressable recall the descent dynamics of a symmetric many-body system and presented its collective computational consequences in a form that physicists could generalize. The later high-capacity models change the interaction function and sometimes the state space, while retaining the idea that memories organize a dynamical landscape. This is an original schematic, not data reproduced from the cited literature.</figcaption>
</figure>

<img src="2609.02195_figures/2609.02195_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">

A cue obtained by flipping 28% of one memory begins with overlap m1 = 0.44 and converges to the stored target under random sequential updates. The energy decreases at every sampled interval while the overlap grows; in this run the final overlap is 1. A single successful cue demonstrates a basin, not a storage-capacity theorem. Source: code/hopfield replication.py.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02195_figures/2609.02195_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. Two complementary replications of capacity. Left: numerical continuation of Eqs. (30)–(32); the nonzero retrieval branch terminates at α ≈0.1376 at the plotted resolution. Right: finite-N synchronous simulations from cues with 10% flipped bits. The finite network rounds and shifts the transition, and the mean-overlap and m &gt; 0.9 criteria do not coincide. The dashed line is a thermodynamic reference, not a fit to finite-N points. Source: code/hopfield replication.py.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02195_figures/2609.02195_fig4.jpg" alt="Figure 4">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> FIG. 4. Replication of the graded-response Lyapunov mechanism. Four random patterns are embedded in N = 120 units with g(u) = tanh(2.2u). From a corrupted continuous cue, the energy in Eq. (36) decreases to numerical precision and the target overlap approaches 0.966. The units remain continuous even though the attractor lies close to saturated values. This figure tests the 1984 structural claim, not a biological fit. Source: code/hopfield replication.py.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02195_figures/2609.02195_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">

Right: direct finite-N tests of whether every bit of a designated random target is stable under the exact energy-difference rule Eq. (43), with K set to the asymptotic no-error scale. The finite-size probabilities are noisy and the asymptotic coefficient is conservative at these sizes; the robust claim is the changed power of N. Source: code/hopfield replication.py.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This chapter treats the Hopfield network as a rigorous physical theory of memory, deriving its energy function and proving that its dynamics converge to stable memory states. It meticulously analyzes the theoretical limits of this energy-based approach using mean-field theory, while critically assessing its assumptions regarding symmetry and locality compared to complex biological systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The analysis of energy landscapes, stability, and phase transitions in the Hopfield model shares deep mathematical parallels with understanding ground states and collective behavior in quantum spin glasses or disordered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reconstructs the Hopfield network as a physical theory of memory, aiming to understand how stable collective states emerge from recurrent dynamics.

**Main result.** It establishes that the energy function provides a global statement guaranteeing convergence to a fixed point, while also detailing the theoretical limits (e.g., $\alpha_c \simeq 0.138$) of this energy-based approach.

**Method.** The analysis uses statistical mechanics, deriving Lyapunov functions for energy decrease in the binary case, and employing mean-field theory (replica method) for analyzing retrieval at extensive load.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Hopfield network, characterized by a symmetric coupling matrix $J_{ij}$ and an associated energy function $E(\mathbf{s})$. Dynamics are governed by asynchronous updates, which ensure energy non-increase.

**Key observables.** Memory (stable attractors), retrieval overlap ($m$), energy function $E(\mathbf{s})$, and the critical storage capacity ($\alpha_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The load parameter $\alpha = p/N$, the temperature $eta$ (for stochastic updates), and the critical capacity $\alpha_c \simeq 0.138$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Critical assumptions include the symmetry of couplings ($J_{ij} = J_{ji}$), the absence of self-coupling ($J_{ii} = 0$), and the point-neuron approximation, which limit its biological scope.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the selective lineage of associative-memory theory, showing Hopfield's synthesis of prior concepts.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the historical context and the energy-based framework for the binary Hopfield network, deriving the Lyapunov function. It then extends this analysis through mean-field theory to calculate capacity limits, concluding with a critical assessment of the model's assumptions and open problems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This chapter reconstructs the Hopfield network as a physical theory of memory rather than merely an early neural-network algorithm. It begins with the problem as it stood before 1982-threshold logic, Hebbian association, correlation memories, and recurrent binary networks-and isolates what Hopfield's synthesis added: a dynamical definition of content-addressable memory, a symmetric recurrent architecture with a Lyapunov function, a Hebbian embedding of patterns in its couplings, and a physical account of basins, robustness, and graceful degradation. The binary and graded-response energy functions are derived in full, together with the signal-crosstalk decomposition governing pattern stability, the mean-field theory of retrieval at extensive load, and the zero-temperature retrieval spinodal at (alpha 0.138) established by Amit, Gutfreund, and Sompolinsky. The energy-based program is then followed through analog optimization networks, polynomial dense associative memories, exponential interactions, and modern continuous Hopfield updates, including the precise conditions under which the update becomes scaled dot-product attention. Throughout, capacity claims are tied to their disorder ensemble, scaling limit, and success criterion, showing why numerically different storage limits need not conflict. A closing assessment distinguishes established results from surviving principles, assumption-bound limitations, and open problems, treating the Hopfield network as an effective theory whose symmetry, locality, and point-neuron assumptions delimit its biological reach. Fixed-seed numerical experiments expose the mechanisms discussed but do not substitute for analytical results.

</details>

**Authors:** Nima Dehghani

This is an original schematic, not a claim that every paper followed a single linear plan.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">

The Hixon marker dates McCulloch’s 1948 lecture; the Jeffress proceedings appeared in 1951. Dates otherwise label births, deaths, publications, or institutional transitions, not exclusive moments of invention. Original schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02183_figures/2609.02183_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. Threshold logic. (a) In the original-style rule, excitation is summed to threshold and any active inhibitory input vetoes firing. (b) XOR is not linearly separable and cannot be computed by one weighted threshold unit. (c) A two-stage construction computes XOR exactly. Each edge crossing a logical stage also crosses a unit delay. Truth tables are generated by the supplied code.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02183_figures/2609.02183_fig4.jpg" alt="Figure 4">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> FIG. 4. Circularity and topology. (a) Exact state-transition graph for the three-bit map in Eq. (13); blue nodes lie on attractors and purple nodes are transient. (b) From 101, a length-three transient feeds a period-three orbit. Finite autonomous nets are eventually periodic, but need not be invertible or begin on an attractor. (c) Cyclic preference cannot be represented by one scalar value function. Original computational demonstrations.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02183_figures/2609.02183_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> FIG. 5. Two routes to a universal. Top: translated and rescaled presentations belong to one transformation orbit; their average is invariant but blurred. Bottom left: max matching over an orbit reduces nuisance sensitivity relative to one template. Bottom right: negative feedback drives a presentation parameter toward a canonical value, reducing mismatch. These are modern computational realizations of the two mechanism classes in Pitts and McCulloch (1947), not reconstructions of their exact diagrams.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep theoretical reconstruction of neural computation, starting from the McCulloch-Pitts model. It rigorously analyzes the network's capabilities as a finite-state machine, distinguishing its limitations from universal computation. The work traces the field's evolution through concepts like invariance and biological observation, framing computation as a causal, discrete-time process.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical computation, the rigorous analysis of finite-state dynamics, periodicity, and the comparison between idealized mathematical models and physical constraints (like dendritic voltage) provides a framework for understanding the limitations of complex, constrained dynamical systems, which has parallels in analyzing quantum state evolution in finite-dimensional systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reconstructs the theoretical foundation of neural computation, tracing the evolution from early logical models to modern understanding of dynamics, learning, and memory in biological and artificial systems.

**Main result.** It establishes that fixed, finite recurrent neural networks are deterministic finite automata, capable of realizing any finite Boolean function but fundamentally limited to finite state spaces, unlike unbounded Turing computation.

**Method.** The analysis uses formal calculus, treating the system as a deterministic switching machine, and comparing the original McCulloch-Pitts logic with later generalizations involving invariance and group theory.

**Model / system.** The core model is the McCulloch-Pitts network, initially defined by a thresholded excitatory summation combined with an absolute inhibitory veto. The system is analyzed as a discrete-time, synchronous, finite-state dynamical machine.

**Key observables.** State space periodicity, realizable Boolean functions, and transformation invariance (e.g., in the retina).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Thresholds ($	heta_i$), connection weights ($w_{ij}$), and the finite number of units ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The 1943 model's strict separation of excitatory summation and inhibitory veto is crucial; the analysis often treats the system as a qualitative model rather than a detailed biophysical reconstruction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the progression from material substrate to formal dynamics, showing the historical development of the research program across key milestones (1943, 1945, 1947, 1959).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses logically from the initial 1943 logical calculus (causal system) to addressing limitations like the heterarchy (cyclic preference) and invariance (group theory), culminating in the analysis of biological vision (retina) and modern computational theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This chapter reconstructs the McCulloch-Pitts program as a physics of neural computation rather than the familiar cartoon of a binary neuron. The 1943 logical calculus is developed in both directions: given a net, characterize the propositions realized by its activity; given an admissible logical expression, construct a net that realizes it. We recover the original distinction between thresholded excitatory summation and absolute inhibitory veto-one the weighted-threshold form cannot preserve for arbitrarily large excitatory inputs-and read unit-time delay as the physical realization of logical depth. Recurrence is treated exactly: an autonomous, deterministic network of finitely many binary units has a finite state space, so every trajectory eventually enters a periodic orbit-a fact about finite-state dynamics, not unbounded Turing computation. A single threshold element realizes only linearly separable Boolean functions, whereas finite feedforward networks of them synthesize any Boolean function on a finite domain. We then follows McCulloch and Pitts beyond threshold logic. The 1945 heterarchy paper turns cyclic preference into an obstruction to representation by a scalar utility. The 1947 work on universals asks how a physical network can identify inputs related by nuisance transformations, developed here via group averaging and feedback canonicalization. The 1959 frog-retina study makes the adequate-stimulus question experimental, revealing parallel invariant operations before the brain proper. Spike-triggered analysis shows how a nonlinearly driven neuron can have a vanishing first-order average while second-order statistics recover its hidden selectivity: methodological failure can masquerade as physiological absence. Modern mathematical tools are used without projecting their notation onto the historical papers, and limitations of the idealization are stated explicitly.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents purification schemes designed to enhance the fidelity of photonic graph states, which are essential building blocks for photonic quantum computation. The methods utilize linear optics and photon detectors to correct for various noise sources, including Pauli errors and photon loss. By combining multiple purification rounds, the authors demonstrate a robust way to maintain high-quality entanglement in photonic quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists working on quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides concrete, resource-efficient protocols to combat decoherence in photonic quantum computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of noise in photonic graph states, which are crucial resources for quantum computation with photons.

**Main result.** The authors develop and analyze purification schemes that can boost the fidelity of arbitrary photonic graph states, requiring only linear optics and photon detectors.

**Method.** The techniques involve applying multi-round purification protocols, combining bit-flip and phase-flip error correction steps, and optimizing the sequence of these steps.

**Model / system.** The system involves photonic graph states, which are generated using quantum emitters with hosted spins. The purification process is modeled using density matrices diagonal in the generalized Bell-state basis.

**Key observables.** Fidelity (F), Success Probability, and the relative fraction of the desired state term in the resulting density matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise sources include phenomenological Pauli errors (bit- and phase-flip) and physical noise from emitters; the analysis considers small loss parameters ($p_l$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The schemes rely on post-selection based on measurement outcomes (heralding) and assume the initial state can be described by a density matrix diagonal in the generalized Bell-state basis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical setups for purifying GHZ or general CSS states using type-I fusion; Figure 2 details the effect of cross-terms during bit-flip and phase-flip purification rounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the need for purification, detailing the general purification protocols for GHZ/CSS states, then analyzing the combined bit-flip and phase-flip correction rounds, and finally examining the robustness of these protocols against photon loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Graph states constitute the main building block for quantum computing with photons. Quantum emitters with a hosted spin can deterministically generate photonic graph states, strongly lowering the overhead of multiplexing highly probabilistic linear-optics graph state generation. However, they generally suffer from various noise sources, resulting in reduced fidelities of the produced states. To mitigate this issue, we develop purification schemes for entangled photonic states. We first develop purification schemes to purify arbitrary photonic GHZ and other CSS states, which we generalize to all photonic graph states and stabilizer states. The proposed purification schemes have a high success probability of up to $1/2$ and require only linear optics and photon detectors. We optimize cascaded purification schemes for various graph states, taking into account phenomenological Pauli errors or physical noise in time-bin-encoded graph state generation with quantum emitters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously connects the Renormalization Group framework to the Conditional Mutual Information (CMI) to diagnose the structure of mixed quantum states. It proves that CMI remains bounded (UV-finite) under specific conditions of local reversibility in the RG flow. By applying this to hierarchical models, the authors derive scaling laws for correlations, providing deep insights into how quantum correlations behave across different length scales.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between information geometry (CMI) and RG flow is crucial for understanding how correlations decay or persist across different length scales in complex quantum systems, which is relevant for understanding critical phenomena and entanglement structure in many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental relationship between the Renormalization Group (RG) flow and the Conditional Mutual Information (CMI) in characterizing mixed quantum states.

**Main result.** It establishes that the CMI between nonadjacent regions is Ultraviolet (UV)-finite if the quantum state admits a locally reversible RG with a fixed, finite local Hilbert space dimension.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical techniques involving RG coarse-graining maps, Strong Subadditivity (SSA), and analyzing specific hierarchical lattice structures to derive bounds on information-theoretic quantities.

**Model / system.** The study applies to general mixed quantum states, analyzing specific hierarchical models such as variants of the Ising model on lattices and tree structures.

**Key observables.** Conditional Mutual Information (CMI), Mutual Information (MI), Relative Entropy, and correlation functions ($\langle ac angle_x$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), noise strength ($p$), system size ($N$), and the RG scale parameter ($r$ or $k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of an 'ideal' RG scheme that is locally reversible and maintains a fixed local Hilbert space dimension.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the steps of the proof for UV-finite CMI using annular tripartitions and show the flow diagrams for RG in various tree models (SSB, SWSSB).

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around establishing the general RG/CMI relationship, followed by detailed applications to specific hierarchical models (e.g., Ising models and tree structures) to derive scaling laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A departure of a mixed quantum state from a local Gibbs description is generally invisible to local observables but can be detected by the conditional mutual information (CMI). Here we study the relationship between the renormalization group (RG) and CMI, and in particular, how RG constrains CMI. We first show that the CMI between nonadjacent regions $A$ and $C$, conditioned on the buffer region $B$, is UV-finite whenever the state admits a locally reversible RG with a fixed on-site Hilbert space dimension. We then study two hierarchical models that have long-range CMI and yet admit a simple RG description. The first model has a divergent Markov length at every temperature $0<T<\infty$ but nevertheless flows to an infinite-temperature product state under RG. The second model satisfies the local Markov condition while violating the global one and is stable against weak noise. At the critical noise strength, the two-point CMI decays only polynomially as a function of the system size, while the two-point mutual information vanishes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models the coherence of a boron-vacancy spin qubit in hBN, showing that the coherence time is highly sensitive to the number of surrounding layers. By using advanced correlation expansion techniques, the authors demonstrate a significant enhancement in $T_2$ when moving from bulk to monolayer hBN, establishing layer number as a key design knob for quantum information devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a quantitative, predictive method for engineering quantum coherence in solid-state defects by manipulating the host material's structure, which is highly relevant for building scalable quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine how the coherence time ($T_2$) of the negatively charged boron vacancy ($	ext{V}^{-	ext{B}}$) spin qubit in hexagonal boron nitride ($	ext{hBN}$) can be tuned by controlling the number of surrounding layers.

**Main result.** The coherence time increases significantly from the bulk limit (199 ns) to a monolayer (653 ns), demonstrating that layer number is a crucial design parameter for enhancing qubit coherence.

**Method.** The study employs a generalized cluster-correlation expansion with an extended central-spin block to calculate the Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$).

**Model / system.** The system is the $	ext{V}^{-	ext{B}}$ spin qubit embedded in $	ext{hBN}$. The qubit is treated as a two-level system within an $S=1$ triplet ground state, and decoherence is modeled via the fluctuating magnetic field from the nuclear spin bath.

**Key observables.** Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$), layer contrast, and the coherence function $L(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number (monolayer, bilayer, etc.), external magnetic field ($B_0$), and the degree of alignment (twist angle).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation focuses solely on nuclear-spin-induced decoherence, neglecting other sources like spin-lattice relaxation or paramagnetic defects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the defect structure, the qubit energy levels, and the secular hyperfine couplings from the surrounding nuclear spins, which dominate the decoherence mechanism.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the full spin Hamiltonian, applying the extended central-spin cluster-correlation expansion to calculate the coherence function $L(t)$, and then analyzing the resulting $T_2$ as a function of the surrounding layer thickness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The negatively charged boron vacancy ($\VB$) in hexagonal boron nitride (\hBN{}) was the first optically addressable spin qubit identified inside a van der Waals crystal, allowing atomically defined placement relative to a target. Its coherence in bulk \hBN{} is limited by the boron nuclei of the layers flanking the defect plane, which a thin flake removes. Here, we use a generalized cluster-correlation expansion with an extended central-spin block to calculate the Hahn-echo coherence time of $\VB$ in h$^{11}$B$^{15}$N as a function of layer number. Our results show that $T_2$ rises from $199\ns$ in the bulk limit to $653\ns$ in a monolayer, a factor of $3.3$ that is already saturated at three layers and that a sublattice decomposition attributes entirely to boron. The enhancement is confined to low field, is insensitive to stacking registry and twist angle, and requires alignment to within about two degrees, an onset we reproduce with no free parameters. Under dynamical decoupling, a $1/e$ threshold returns $206\ns$ for every thickness, but this is a zero of the first-shell modulation rather than a decay: measured without a threshold, the layer contrast survives and reaches a factor of $170$. Layer number thus emerges as a design parameter for $\VB$-based sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'unfolded Krylov complexity' as a superior diagnostic tool for quantum chaos. By mathematically unfolding the energy spectrum, the authors successfully remove non-universal, smooth spectral contributions that cause false positives in integrable systems. This allows researchers to reliably probe the genuine, universal signatures of many-body quantum scrambling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a mathematically rigorous tool to filter out model-dependent spectral artifacts, offering a cleaner diagnostic for identifying true quantum chaos in complex many-body systems, which is relevant for understanding thermalization and scrambling in quantum matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is distinguishing genuine many-body quantum scrambling (chaos) from spurious signals arising from kinematic effects or integrable systems when using complexity diagnostics.

**Main result.** Unfolding the spectrum prior to calculating Krylov complexity successfully suppresses false-positive peaks in integrable systems while preserving the universal spectral signatures characteristic of genuinely chaotic quantum dynamics.

**Method.** The authors propose and implement 'spectral unfolding,' which separates the smooth, non-universal density of states from the microscopic spectral correlations, allowing for the construction of 'unfolded Krylov complexity.'

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to various quantum systems, including Random Matrix Theory (RMT) ensembles (GOE, GUE, GSE), quantum billiards (stadium, circular), and the logarithmic model, using the Lanczos iteration framework.

**Key observables.** Krylov state complexity (spread complexity), Spectral Form Factor (SFF), and the unfolded spectrum $\xi_i$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the infinite-temperature limit ($eta=0$) and the distinction between the smooth density $ar{ho}(E)$ and the fluctuating part $\deltaho(E)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the smooth component of the density of states can be accurately estimated and removed via unfolding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the raw vs. unfolded complexity/SFF for RMT spectra and quantum billiards, demonstrating the removal of spurious peaks in non-chaotic cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically reviews the theory of unfolding and complexity, applies it to RMT and quantum billiards, develops the polynomial framework using the Lanczos iteration, and concludes with specific applications like the logarithmic model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central challenge in diagnosing quantum chaos is to distinguish genuine many-body scrambling from kinematic effects of the spectrum. Krylov state complexity, or spread complexity, has emerged as a powerful diagnostic, with its characteristic growth, peak, and relaxation often taken as signatures of chaos. However, previous work has shown that this criterion can give false positives: saddle-dominated integrable systems may display prominent peaks even without random-matrix level correlations. We argue, based on complementary numerical and analytical evidence, that this ambiguity can be resolved by unfolding the spectrum prior to constructing the ensuing Krylov dynamics. By removing the non-universal smooth density of states while retaining microscopic spectral correlations, unfolding suppresses spurious peaks in integrable systems while preserving the universal spectral signatures of chaotic systems. Analytically, the formulation of the Lanczos iteration in terms of orthogonal polynomials yields an exact complexity kernel with a robust near-diagonal structure whose fine-grained features reflect the underlying spectral correlations. Moreover, for the logarithmic model, unfolding can be performed exactly, mapping the spectrum to a uniform lattice and yielding an analytic spread complexity that removes the false-positive peak. These findings establish unfolded Krylov complexity as a more reliable probe of genuine many-body scrambling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of preparing complex Thermofield Double states for SYK models using deep quantum circuits. The authors introduce and test sequential angle-pruning algorithms applied to the multi-angle QAOA ansatz. These methods significantly reduce circuit depth while maintaining high fidelity, demonstrating a practical pathway toward implementing quantum simulations on near-term noisy quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource limitations of quantum computation by developing systematic, data-driven methods to compress variational quantum circuits used for simulating complex many-body physics models like SYK.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is preparing Thermofield Double (TFD) states using quantum circuits that are deep and resource-intensive, necessitating effective circuit reduction while maintaining high fidelity.

**Main result.** The proposed sequential angle-pruning algorithms (SAP/OSAP) successfully reduce the circuit depth by removing a large fraction of Pauli-string evolutions (up to 92.1%) while retaining an average fidelity near 95% for SYK models.

**Method.** The study employs the multi-angle QAOA (ma-QAOA) ansatz to approximate the TFD state, followed by two top-down sequential angle-pruning techniques that iteratively remove the least significant Pauli-string evolutions and reoptimize the remaining parameters.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) models, tested in both Gaussian and binary forms, across dense and sparse connectivity regimes. The TFD state is the target state, relevant for simulating quantum phenomena in holographic duality.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of the prepared TFD state, circuit depth (number of gates/blocks), and the percentage of removed nonlocal Pauli-string evolutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), number of qubits ($N$), and the pruning strength/candidate pool size ($S, N_C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that removing Pauli-string evolutions with small optimized angles does not significantly degrade the fidelity of the target TFD state, provided reoptimization is performed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the SYK models and the pruning procedures (SAP-ma-QAOA and OSAP-ma-QAOA). Other figures track infidelity trajectories as a function of pruning strength or candidate pool size.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for circuit reduction in TFD state preparation, introduces ma-QAOA, and then details the two sequential angle-pruning algorithms (SAP and OSAP). The methods are validated by applying them to various SYK models, comparing fidelity retention against circuit depth reduction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational preparation of thermofield double (TFD) states can require deep quantum circuits, particularly for interacting many-body systems. Reducing these circuits while retaining high fidelity is therefore crucial for TFD-state preparation on noisy quantum processors. We study this problem by applying the multi-angle quantum approximate optimization algorithm (ma-QAOA) to TFD-state preparation and introducing two top-down sequential angle-pruning algorithms. Starting from the optimized initial ma-QAOA circuit, both algorithms sequentially remove Pauli-string evolutions with small optimized angles and reoptimize the remaining parameters after each removal. We apply these algorithms to Gaussian and binary Sachdev--Ye--Kitaev (SYK) models in both dense and sparse cases. We find that ma-QAOA prepares the target TFD states with high fidelity and that sequential small-angle pruning retains high fidelity while reducing the circuit depth, particularly at low temperature. Moreover, using the post-reoptimization cost in sequential small-angle pruning further improves the fidelity. For the binary sparse $N=10$ SYK model at $β=10$, $88.8\%$--$92.1\%$ of the nonlocal Pauli-string evolutions are removed while retaining an average fidelity of approximately $95\%$. Finally, we propose extensions of the sequential pruning algorithms toward quantum--classical hybrid implementation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a blueprint for a hybrid quantum internet by integrating satellite-based free-space quantum communication with terrestrial optical fiber networks. It leverages single-photon sources from hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) emitters operating at 854 nm, a wavelength chosen to maintain high performance during daylight hours while ensuring compatibility with existing fiber infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of scaling quantum networks by proposing a concrete, multi-link architecture and identifying a practical operating wavelength that maximizes compatibility between different physical media (vacuum, air, fiber).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a practical, hybrid free-space optical (FSO) quantum communication network capable of connecting satellites to ground stations and bridging the 'last mile' to end-users.

**Main result.** The paper proposes using hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) emitters operating near the Ca-II Fraunhofer line (854 nm) to achieve a balance between daylight FSO performance and compatibility with existing optical fiber infrastructure.

**Method.** The authors developed a conceptual framework combining continuous network operation, hybrid links (space-to-ground-to-fiber), and performance modeling using realistic single-photon sources.

**Model / system.** The system involves a quantum network architecture utilizing hBN-based single-photon sources for quantum key distribution (QKD) over free-space links (satellite to ground) and terrestrial fiber links.

**Key observables.** Secret key rate (in units of secret bit per signal), and the comparison of key rates across different wavelengths (e.g., H$\alpha$ vs. Ca-II).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavelengths (854 nm, 656 nm), link types (FSO, Fiber), and source characteristics (realistic SPS from hBN).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that operating near Fraunhofer lines allows single photons to be separated from strong solar background radiance, and that the 854 nm wavelength balances FSO and fiber compatibility.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the extractable secret bit per signal for a single satellite pass at 500 km altitude, showing performance differences between daylight and nighttime conditions across various wavelengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the challenge of hybrid quantum networking, analyzes the performance trade-offs between different wavelengths (H$\alpha$ vs. Ca-II) for daylight operation, and proposes the 854 nm wavelength as the optimal choice for bridging space-based and fiber-based quantum links.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is known as a promising solid-state platform to host room temperature quantum emitters that produce high purity single photons. The bright and spectrally adaptable hBN emitters are space-compatible, making hBN well-suited for satellite-based free-space optical (FSO) quantum key distribution (QKD) at wavelengths where the atmospheric background is naturally suppressed. This paper presents a pathway to develop hBN emitters operating near the Ca-II Fraunhofer line (at 854 nm), enabling daylight FSO operation. Moreover, due to its compatibility with the first telecommunication window at 850 nm, it is possible to interface with optical fibers to bridge the `last mile' in a scenario where multiple end-users connect through a single optical ground station to a QKD satellite. We therefore introduce a concept that combines continuous operation of a quantum network, hybrid links to minimize deployment costs, and high data rates due to the use of realistic single photon sources. The realization would be an important milestone for the development of the quantum internet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves a major open question in quantum complexity by proving a quantum oracle separation between QMA and QMA(2). By constructing a specific oracle, the authors demonstrate that the ability to use two unentangled proofs provides a computational advantage over standard QMA protocols. This separation also yields a strong, exponential lower bound on the resources required by quantum disentanglers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques used—polynomial methods applied to quantum query complexity and the analysis of subspace projectors—are highly relevant to understanding the fundamental computational power of quantum protocols, which underpins quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine if the quantum complexity class QMA(2) (verifiable with two unentangled quantum proofs) is strictly more powerful than QMA (standard quantum proofs).

**Main result.** They prove a quantum oracle separation, showing that there exists an oracle U such that QMA_U != QMA(2)_U, and consequently establish an exponential lower bound on the input size required by any disentangler for $\epsilon + \delta < 1$.

**Method.** The proof combines the unitarily invariant polynomial method (adapted from She and Yuen) with a novel construction utilizing symmetric and antisymmetric subspace projectors to derive the necessary lower bounds.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted within the framework of quantum query complexity, focusing on the separation between QMA and QMA(2) using a constructed unitary oracle U acting on bipartite Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** Acceptance probability, polynomial degree of the acceptance probability, and the required input size ($m$) for an $(\epsilon, \delta)$-disentangler.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The separation relies on the parameters $\epsilon$ and $\delta$ defining the disentangler approximation, and the ambient dimension $D$ related to the oracle structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The argument assumes the existence of polynomial bounds for running time, witness length, and oracle queries for the underlying QMA verifiers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the polynomial degree lower bound shape, showing $p(0)=0$ and $p(j)$ bounded away from zero for consecutive $j$. Figure 2 is a tensor network diagram related to trace calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the separation QMA_U != QMA(2)_U using a constructed oracle U and the polynomial method. It then leverages this separation to derive the exponential lower bound for disentanglers, resolving the Watrous conjecture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We find a quantum oracle relative to which $\mathsf{QMA} \neq \mathsf{QMA}(2)$. As a consequence, we resolve the no-disentanglers conjecture of Watrous: for every $ε+δ<1$, any $(ε,δ)$-disentangler requires input size exponential in the number of output qubits. Our proof combines the unitarily invariant polynomial method of She and Yuen (ITCS '23) with a new construction based on the symmetric and antisymmetric subspace projectors, reducing the $\mathsf{QMA}$ lower bound to the approximate degree of $\mathrm{OR}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the critical optimization challenge of deploying UAVs for emergency communication networks by formulating it as a complex combinatorial problem. Due to the exponential scaling of classical solvers, the authors reformulate the problem into a QUBO suitable for quantum computation. The research demonstrates the potential of quantum algorithms to solve these time-sensitive, large-scale optimization problems in real-time disaster response scenarios.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct mapping of a complex, real-world combinatorial optimization problem (UAV placement) onto a QUBO framework and testing it with quantum algorithms makes it highly relevant for quantum algorithm development in applied physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the combinatorial optimization problem of strategically deploying UAVs to establish connected communication bridges in disaster zones.

**Main result.** Classical solvers exhibit exponential time-to-solution scaling, necessitating the reformulation of the problem as a QUBO suitable for quantum algorithms like the Quantum Adiabatic Algorithm (QAA).

**Method.** The problem is formulated as an Integer Quadratic Program (IQP) and subsequently mapped to a QUBO, which is then analyzed using both classical solvers (CPLEX) and quantum emulation (QAA).

**Model / system.** The system involves UAVs in a flying ad-hoc network (FANET) needing to maximize signal coverage and maintain connectivity via a backbone structure while mitigating interference using multi-frequency assignments.

**Key observables.** Signal coverage, network connectivity (backbone/leaves), interference levels, and Time-to-Solution (TTS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of candidate sites ($N$), number of available drones ($V$), number of backbone nodes ($B$), and number of frequencies ($F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that quantum computing offers a scalable alternative to classical solvers for this time-critical optimization task; approximations are made regarding higher-order interference overlaps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the network components (coverage, connectivity, interference) and show performance metrics for QAA on small instances, demonstrating the feasibility of quantum approaches.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the c-BUP problem, formulates it as an IQP/QUBO, analyzes the classical computational bottlenecks (exponential TTS), and then proposes and tests the quantum adiabatic algorithm approach.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a combinatorial optimization problem for the strategic deployment of UAVs equipped with 5G antennas to assist rescue operations in regions hit by natural disasters. Our goal is to optimize the placement of UAVs to provide coverage in flying ad-hoc networks among given candidate sites. Our formulation aims to maximize signal coverage and minimize interference while ensuring network connectivity. To mitigate interference effects, we incorporate the use of multiple frequencies. We formulate this problem as an integer quadratic program (IQP). We present numerical solutions obtained via the CPLEX solver and conduct a preliminary analysis of the problem's scalability in realistic network configurations. Our findings reveal a significant exponential increase in Time-to-Solution (TTS) as the number of sites grows, which poses a critical challenge in urgent, time-sensitive scenarios. To address this issue, approximate suboptimal solutions can be produced by enforcing a time limit on the solver. Although these solutions are not optimal, they preserve connectivity in most cases, providing a practical trade-off between solution quality and computational times that remain within feasible limits for real-time UAV redeployment. Recognizing the limitations of classical solvers in these contexts, we explore quantum computing as a promising alternative. Specifically, we reformulate the problem as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem, suitable for most quantum algorithms. Through high-performance computing emulation, we show that the quantum adiabatic algorithm (QAA) can accurately solve small-scale instances, paving the way for future application of quantum computing to large-scale, time-critical optimization problems in disaster response.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep mathematical analysis comparing two independent, recent improvements to the asymptotic upper bound on binary code rates (the MRRW bound). It unifies these results by showing that both proofs fundamentally rely on counting the number of associated subspaces within the ambient space. The work is significant for both coding theory and for establishing rigorous mathematical limits in related quantum information contexts.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is classical coding theory, the paper draws deep parallels between classical bounds and quantum information concepts (like Holevo information and typical subspaces), making it relevant for understanding the mathematical limits of quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the asymptotic upper bound on the rate of binary codes, known as the McEliece–Rodemich–Rumsey–Welch (MRRW) bound.

**Main result.** The authors establish that two recently derived bounds, $\kappa_H(\delta)$ and $RMQC(\delta)$, are strictly better than the original MRRW-1 bound for $0 < \delta < 1/2$, and prove their equality.

**Method.** The analysis involves advanced techniques from coding theory, including moving from packing vectors to packing subspaces, utilizing Delsarte certificates, and employing spectral methods on the Hamming space.

**Model / system.** The mathematical model is based on binary codes $C \subset \{0, 1\}^n$ and their achievable rate $R_2(\delta)$ as a function of relative distance $\delta$. The analysis is generalized to involve subspaces attached to each codeword.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the achievable rate $R(C)$ and the relative distance $\delta$. The bounds themselves, $M_1(\delta)$, $\kappa_H(\delta)$, and $RMQC(\delta)$, are key quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative distance $\delta$ is the main parameter governing the bound. The analysis also involves parameters $a$ and $b$ when considering quantum channel analogies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the multiplicative notation for the Hamming space and the use of adjoint operators to decompose function spaces. A key assumption in one part is $\Lambda > s$ for the main bound to hold.

**Figures summary.** Table 1 is used to numerically compare the values of the original MRRW bound $M_1(\delta)$ against the improved bound $	ext{RMQC}(\delta)$ across various values of $\delta$, demonstrating the improvement.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the context of improving the MRRW bound. It then details the mathematical machinery, comparing the Delsarte certificate approach with the classical-quantum channel approach. The structure progresses from general coding theory bounds to specific spectral and harmonic-analytic derivations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The asymptotic McEliece--Rodemich--Rumsey--Welch bound (1977) limits the largest attainable rate of binary codes as a function of the relative distance. After a nearly half-century hiatus, this result was recently improved in two concurrent works, by OpenAI and by O. Alrabiah and V. Guruswami. The two arguments look entirely different, a Delsarte certificate on the one hand, a classical-quantum channel and the pretty good measurement on the other, and they yield the same bound. The purpose of this note is to explain why: in both proofs, a subspace is attached to every codeword and moved with it, and the bound counts how many such subspaces fit in the ambient space, exactly in the first case and in the probabilistic sense of typicality in the second. We also present the OpenAI proof in the language and context of coding theory, as an extension of the spectral method in which the single vector attached to a codeword is replaced by a subspace.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive comparative study of computational methods used to calculate the single-photon ionization cross sections of molecular hydrogen. By applying time-dependent, time-independent, and complex-scaling techniques, the authors demonstrate strong agreement between fundamentally different theoretical formulations. This work provides robust validation for advanced quantum methods applied to molecular photoionization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it provides rigorous, comparative benchmarks for calculating fundamental molecular properties like photoionization cross sections using state-of-the-art quantum computational techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper focuses on computing the single-photon ionization cross sections of molecular hydrogen ($	ext{H}_2$) in the electric dipole limit.

**Main result.** The computed results show convincing mutual agreement across multiple fundamentally different computational approaches (time-dependent vs. time-independent), validating the methods used.

**Method.** A comparative study employing three main computational techniques: time-dependent Schrödinger equation propagation, time-independent free-boundary methods (CI-based), and complex-scaling geminal methods.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the molecular hydrogen ($	ext{H}_2$) molecule, calculated at its equilibrium internuclear distance. The calculations utilize advanced electronic structure methods based on Configuration Interaction (CI) theory.

**Key observables.** Single-photon ionization cross sections ($\sigma$), ionization yield ($Y$), and optical oscillator strength (OOS) density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Equilibrium internuclear distance ($R = 1.4 	ext{ a}_0$); the calculation is performed in the electric dipole limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions include the clamped-nuclei approximation and the use of various basis sets (e.g., B-splines) to discretize the continuum states.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text references figures for high-energy computations and tables detailing CI configuration series.

**Paper structure.** The paper compares results from time-dependent, time-independent (Free-Boundary), and complex-scaling approaches to calculate the photoionization cross section, validating the consistency between these distinct theoretical formulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single-photon ionization cross sections of molecular hydrogen in the electric dipole limit have been computed. Both time-dependent and -independent approaches within the clamped-nuclei approximation at equilibrium internuclear distance are employed, featuring the explicit time-propagation of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation and newly implemented multi-channel configuration-interaction free-boundary as well as complex-scaling methods using an explicitly correlated geminal basis set. The found results are compared to both experimental and previously published theoretical results, showing convincing mutual agreement despite their entirely different fundamental formulations. The novel CI-based approach demonstrates fast and controllable convergence while being able to provide full channel-resolved information.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses hard and soft X-ray spectroscopy to map the electronic structure of artificial high-Tc superlattices ($	ext{LSCO}/	ext{LCO}$) as the superconducting dome is tuned by changing the layer thickness ratio ($L/d$). The results demonstrate that the optimal superconducting state correlates with spectroscopic signatures indicating the system is tuned near a Van Hove Singularity, providing strong experimental evidence for the electronic mechanism driving high-Tc superconductivity in these engineered structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of advanced X-ray spectroscopy to map out the electronic structure evolution across a quantum-engineered superconducting phase is highly relevant for understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms in correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine how the electronic structure evolves across the superconducting dome in artificial high-Tc superlattices (AHTS) as a function of geometric tuning.

**Main result.** Spectroscopic signatures (DOS enhancement, screening, orbital polarization) are strongest near $L/d = 0.67$, supporting a model where superconductivity emerges from tuning the system toward a Van Hove Singularity (VHS) via an electronic topological transition.

**Method.** The study combines Hard X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (HAXPES) and polarization-dependent Soft X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS) to probe both occupied and unoccupied electronic states.

**Model / system.** The system is the $	ext{La}_{1.55}	ext{Sr}_{0.45}	ext{CuO}_4/	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ superlattice, whose superconducting dome is tuned by varying the geometric ratio $L/d$. The analysis is guided by the Bianconi-Perali-Valletta (BPV) framework, which predicts superconductivity enhancement near a Lifshitz electronic transition.

**Key observables.** Leading-edge spectral weight ($\Delta S$), chemical potential ($\mu$) shifts (from $	ext{La } 3d$), local/non-local screening signatures ($	ext{Cu } 2p$), and in-plane orbital polarization ($	ext{Cu } L_{2,3}	ext{-edge}$ XLD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The geometric ratio $L/d$ (e.g., 0.44, 0.67, 0.78, 0.89) acts as the primary tuning parameter, controlling the effective hole doping and proximity to the predicted optimal doping regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting dome behavior is governed by the BPV framework, linking superconductivity to the proximity of the chemical potential to a Van Hove Singularity (VHS) induced by the superlattice periodicity.

**Figures summary.** The figures likely show systematic trends in core-level shifts, spectral weight enhancements near the Fermi level, and polarization-dependent spectral features that correlate with the measured $T_c$ dome.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence from multiple spectroscopic techniques (HAXPES, XAS) across the series of superlattices, correlating the observed electronic structure changes (e.g., $\mu$ evolution, DOS changes) with the known superconducting dome structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In La$_{1.55}$Sr$_{0.45}$CuO$_4$/La$_2$CuO$_4$ (LSCO/LCO) artificial high-$T_c$ superlattices (AHTS), grown by quantum material design, the superconducting dome can be tuned by varying the geometric ratio $L/d$, where $L$ is the LCO layer thickness and $d$ is the superlattice nanoscale period. Here, we combine hard X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (HAXPES) and polarization-dependent soft X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) to probe how the electronic structure evolves across the $L/d$-tuned superconducting dome. We observe systematic chemical-potential evolution across the series, together with enhanced spectral weight near the Fermi level, enhanced local and non-local screening signatures, and increased in-plane orbital polarization near the top of the dome. Together, these spectroscopic signatures provide insight into the emergence of Fano-Feshbach resonant superconductivity in the LSCO/LCO AHTS series.

</details>

### [Evolution from Kitaev to XXZ spin chains via distortion: Application to BaCo$_2$V$_2$O$_8$](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01805v1)

**Summary.** This work develops a unified microscopic theory for understanding magnetic anisotropy in spin-orbit-coupled chains like BaCo2V2O8. By incorporating octahedral distortions, the authors show that the resulting anisotropic g-tensor, combined with renormalized exchange interactions, is necessary to explain the experimentally observed large anisotropy in the critical magnetic field. This bridges structural chemistry, electronic structure, and quantum magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, unified microscopic framework linking structural distortions (crystal field effects) directly to macroscopic magnetic properties (anisotropy), which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum magnetism in correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to unify the understanding of magnetic anisotropy in spin-orbit-coupled Ising-chain antiferromagnets, resolving discrepancies between simple models and experimental observations in BaCo2V2O8.

**Main result.** The large in-plane critical-field anisotropy in BaCo2V2O8 arises from the cooperative interplay between the microscopic exchange interactions (governed by the distortion-modified Kitaev-Heisenberg-Gamma model) and the crystal-field-induced g-tensor anisotropy, particularly the off-diagonal component g_xy.

**Method.** The authors use a combination of microscopic theory extensions (incorporating octahedral distortions), exact diagonalization (ED) for spin dynamics, and classical analysis of effective magnetic fields derived from the g-tensor.

**Model / system.** The system is BaCo2V2O8, modeled as a spin-orbit-coupled Ising-chain antiferromagnet. The Hamiltonian is extended from the Kitaev-Heisenberg-Gamma (JKΓ) model to include distortions of the CoO6 octahedra, which modifies both the exchange interactions and generates an anisotropic g-tensor.

**Key observables.** Spin dynamical structure factor (DSF), critical field anisotropy h_c(phi), and the components of the g-tensor (e.g., g_xy/g_xx ratio).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of exchange interactions (K/J, $\epsilon$), the distortion-induced staggered term (K'), and the ratio of g-tensor components (g_xy/g_xx).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis simplifies by neglecting g_xz and g_yz in the final classical picture, and the initial microscopic theory relies on point-charge calculations for the g-tensor.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of the bonds in ideal vs. distorted structures, compare calculated DSF with INS data, and show the phase boundary h_c(phi) demonstrating that the anisotropy is strongly dependent on the g_xy/g_xx ratio.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of the ideal JKΓ model (using ED/INS comparison), then introducing distortions to generate both modified exchange terms and an anisotropic g-tensor. Finally, it analyzes the interplay between these two effects to explain the critical field anisotropy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

BaCo$_2$V$_2$O$_8$ is a prototypical spin-orbit-coupled Ising-chain antiferromagnet that provides a unique platform for studying field-induced quantum magnetism. Under a transverse magnetic field, it exhibits unusual magnetic properties, including an anomalous staggered magnetization and a pronounced anisotropy of the critical field with respect to the in-plane field direction. While these phenomena have been attributed phenomenologically to a site-dependent anisotropic $g$-tensor, a recent microscopic theory has shown that spin-orbit coupling naturally generates bond-dependent Heisenberg, Kitaev, and $Γ$ exchange interactions. Here, we unify these two pictures by extending the microscopic theory to incorporate distortions of the CoO$_6$ octahedra. We show that the distortions not only generate the site-dependent anisotropic $g$-tensor but also renormalize the staggered exchange interactions through a distortion-induced contribution that partially compensates the Kitaev-derived staggered term. Using point-charge calculations to estimate the $g$-tensor of BaCo$_2$V$_2$O$_8$, we demonstrate that the strong anisotropy of the critical field originates from the combined effects of the modified exchange interactions and the anisotropic $g$-tensor. Our work provides a unified microscopic framework for understanding the magnetic anisotropy of spin-orbit-coupled Ising-chain materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a groundbreaking quantum algorithm for protein structure prediction that moves beyond restrictive lattice models into continuous, all-atom space. It achieves this by encoding the exponentially large conformational space into a quantum statevector, requiring only logarithmic qubits relative to the number of torsional angles. The success on benchmark proteins proves the feasibility of this scalable, hybrid quantum-classical approach for biophysics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the mapping of continuous physical degrees of freedom (torsions) onto discrete quantum computational resources (qubits/phases), which is a major theoretical challenge in quantum simulation for molecular physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Protein structure prediction (PSP) is computationally challenging due to the vast conformational space, and current quantum methods are limited to simplistic lattice models.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a novel, scalable quantum algorithm capable of performing all-atom, continuous-space PSP using only O(log N) qubits for N torsional degrees of freedom.

**Method.** They propose a logarithmic-scale Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) where molecular torsions are encoded into the relative phases of a quantum statevector, which are then decoded to reconstruct 3D coordinates.

**Model / system.** The system is the protein structure, modeled by its backbone and sidechain torsional angles. The approach uses a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline, employing a custom Hamiltonian derived from molecular mechanics terms to evaluate the energy.

**Key observables.** RMSD (Root Mean Square Deviation) for structural comparison, and the total scalar energy calculated by the hybrid Hamiltonian.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The qubit scaling is $\mathcal{O}(\log_2 N)$ for $N$ torsional degrees of freedom. The method uses a multi-stage relaxation curriculum to mitigate barren plateaus.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve the feasibility of mapping continuous physical constraints into circuit depth constraints and the reliance on a probability-CDF decoder due to the non-observability of relative phases on NISQ hardware.

**Figures summary.** The figures schematically illustrate the four-stage hybrid quantum-classical pipeline: quantum state generation from parameters, differentiable coordinate reconstruction from phases, physical energy evaluation, and variational optimization.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of existing PSP methods, details the novel logarithmic-scale VQE architecture (QTF), outlines the hybrid quantum-classical pipeline, presents benchmarks on specific proteins (chignolin, Trp-cage), and discusses hardware implementation and future scalability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical and current quantum approaches to protein structure prediction (QPSP) face limitations, notably massive qubit requirements restricting near-term models to simplistic on-lattice simulations. We propose a logarithmic-scale variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) that reduces qubit requirements for N torsional degrees of freedom to O(log2N), enabling off-lattice, all-atom simulations. Our architecture extracts molecular torsions from relative phases in statevector simulations. On quantum hardware, a decoder maps the empirical cumulative distribution function (CDF) from basis-state probabilities to bounded torsional variables. These feed a classical algorithm to build heavy-atom coordinates. We use an EfficientSU2 ansatz and multi-stage relaxation to mitigate barren plateaus. Structures are evaluated via a custom hybrid quantum-classical Hamiltonian, alongside Rosetta and OpenMM benchmarks.   Evaluation on chignolin and Trp-cage yielded native-like conformations. Chignolin reached a 0.623 Å Cα RMSD in retained snapshots and 1.199 Å in final models; Trp-cage achieved a 2.501 Å RMSD among snapshots (3.512 Å in final models). Execution on IBM processors (ibm_cleveland, ibm_miami) successfully recovered native-like structures with a best RMSD of 1.758 Å. The custom energy function performed best overall, though energy-ranking imbalances persisted across sampled landscapes for all functions.   This introduces the first all-atom, continuous-space quantum algorithm for QPSP. By converting physical qubit constraints into circuit depth constraints, it proves high-resolution prediction is feasible with exponentially fewer qubits. Despite current limits like computational overhead and energy function sensitivity, it establishes a scalable foundation for hybrid quantum biophysics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the speed limit of a quantum walker confined to a half-line lattice by constraining its initial state to $M$ sites. It shows that the maximal achievable drift velocity approaches the theoretical maximum with a predictable, inverse-square deficit scaling with $M$. The methodology involves advanced spectral analysis, moving from finite matrices to a continuum operator limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous spectral methods to analyze transport phenomena in quantum systems constrained by boundaries, connecting discrete spectral problems to continuous field theory limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines how close the maximal mean drift velocity of a continuous-time quantum walk (CTQW) on a hard-wall half-line can approach the theoretical maximal front speed when the initial state is restricted to a finite number of sites ($M$).

**Main result.** The maximal mean drift velocity exhibits a deficit that decays with an inverse-square dependence on $M$, with the continuum limit yielding a constant prefactor of $\pi^2/4$.

**Method.** The analysis uses moment-generating functions and spectral theory, reducing the optimization problem to finding the largest eigenvalue of explicit, finite-dimensional Hermitian matrices, which is then analyzed in the continuum limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a continuous-time quantum walk (CTQW) evolving on the hard-wall half-line ($\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$) governed by a nearest-neighbor Hamiltonian. The dynamics are analyzed using the sine transform and weak-limit theorems.

**Key observables.** Maximal mean drift velocity ($	ext{vmax}(M)$), asymptotic velocity random variable ($V$), and the moment-generating function ($G_\psi(s)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite support size $M$, the bulk dispersion velocity ($2J$), and the continuum scaling variable $u$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on stationary-phase estimates and the validity of weak-limit theorems for CTQWs, particularly in the large-$M$ limit.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention numerical verification of both the finite-$M$ spectral formulation and the predicted scaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the system and establishing the bulk limit, then characterizing the finite-$M$ spectral problem via matrix diagonalization, followed by deriving the continuum scaling limit, and finally extracting the constant prefactor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-time quantum walks on a lattice spread ballistically and converge to a limiting distribution for the rescaled position. On the hard-wall half line the boundary reflects the walker but does not change the bulk dispersion, so the ballistic front remains set by the maximal group velocity. We ask how close one can get to this front when the initial state is constrained to occupy only the first $M$ sites. We show that optimizing the moment-generating function of the limiting-velocity distribution over this finite-support class reduces to the principal eigenvalue of an explicit $M\times M$ Hermitian matrix, which yields the optimal state by direct diagonalization. For large $M$, a near-front scaling limit produces a continuum description that controls the entire peak region. In particular, the maximal mean drift approaches the front with an inverse-square deficit in $M$ and a constant prefactor $π^2/4$. Numerical results verify both the finite-$M$ spectral formulation and the predicted scaling.

</details>
