**Summary.** This paper introduces NOBOL, a protocol to perform logical CNOT gates between distant qubits using only one Bell pair, circumventing the high resource overhead associated with current fault-tolerant methods. By restricting operations to small logical operator subsets, NOBOL offers a scalable and code-agnostic solution for large-scale quantum computation architectures. This significantly advances the feasibility of distributed quantum networking.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract quantum computation protocols, the resource management and error containment strategies discussed (e.g., stabilizing stabilizers) are highly relevant to designing physical quantum hardware architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is significantly reducing the overhead associated with performing logical CNOT operations between distant qubits in fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors propose NOBOL, a novel protocol that achieves the logical CNOT using only a single pre-shared Bell pair, drastically reducing the resource overhead compared to previous linear or quadratic scaling methods.

**Method.** The method leverages the fact that the operation can be restricted to acting only on the logical X or Z operator subsets of the qubits, which are assumed to be much smaller than the full code block size.

**Model / system.** The work is situated in the context of fault-tolerant quantum computation using arbitrary CSS codes, applicable to both monolithic and distributed quantum processing units (QPUs).

**Key observables.** Logical CNOT operation success, resource overhead (number of Bell pairs/SWAP gates), and circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the logical operator subsets ($	au$), the code distance ($d$), and the physical link fidelity ($p_{	ext{link}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the logical CNOT can be realized by interacting only with the small subsets of physical qubits corresponding to the logical operators, and that error containment procedures can be effectively implemented.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the remote CNOT operation using a distributed Bell pair and show the evolution of surface code stabilizers during the CNOT circuit, distinguishing between different stabilizer types.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the high overhead of remote operations, introduces NOBOL as a solution requiring only one Bell pair, details the circuit realizations (including depth-optimal circuits), and concludes with effective methods for containing error propagation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computation fundamentally relies on encoding a logical qubit into a structured block of physical qubits, typically in the tens to hundreds. As a trade-off for improved fault-tolerance, logical gate operations will incur a linear overhead in terms of both the amount of time and quantum resources than before. For example, in monolithic quantum computing, performing a gate operation on two distant logical qubits will first require using a linear number of SWAP operations in order to move the logical qubits next to each other; while in distributed quantum computing, doing so will first require a linear number of ancilla qubits in order to form entanglement connections (or a logical Bell pair).   In this paper, we focus on significantly reducing the overhead involved in logical CNOT operations, a fundamental primitive. We propose NOBOL, a novel approach that requires only one Bell pair to perform a logical CNOT operation on two distant qubits encoded in arbitrary CSS codes. More importantly, NOBOL only requires performing gate operations on the logical X or Z operator subsets of the logical qubits. For many CSS codes, such as the surface code, these subsets are significantly smaller than the size of the code itself. In this paper, we describe various circuit realizations of NOBOL, including a depth-optimal circuit with logarithmic depth in terms of the size of the logical operators. Finally, we propose effective methods to contain error propagation without incurring much additional overhead.   Since NOBOL can be effectively applied to a wide range of quantum error-correcting (QEC) codes and, in addition, is agnostic to qubit modalities and effective for various architectures, including those based on either a monolithic QPU or distributed QPUs.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> FIG. 5. Ferroic order of magnetic multipoles probed by polarized neutron diffraction.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper reports the direct experimental observation of a $g$-wave electric multipole in the altermagnet CrSb using synchrotron X-ray diffraction. By analyzing the directional anisotropy of the Valence Electron Density, the authors confirm the existence of this higher-order multipole, establishing a real-space fingerprint of altermagnetism. This work connects subtle electric multipole order to measurable magnetic phenomena, advancing the field of quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, measurable link between higher-order electric multipole moments (like the $g$-wave hexadecapole) and observable electronic/magnetic properties, which is crucial for understanding exotic magnetic states beyond simple dipole moments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To directly visualize and establish a real-space fingerprint of altermagnetism, specifically the hidden multipole order, in a material like CrSb.

**Main result.** The study successfully observed a pronounced, alternating directional anisotropy in the Valence Electron Density (VED) around Cr sites, confirming the presence of the predicted $g$-wave electric multipole order.

**Method.** The research combines high-energy synchrotron X-ray diffraction with Valence Electron Density (VED) analysis, complemented by theoretical modeling using symmetry-adapted Wannier functions and polarized neutron diffraction measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the altermagnet CrSb. The theoretical framework models the electronic structure using a tight-binding Hamiltonian expanded in terms of electric multipoles ($\hat{Q}_{lm}$), relating the $g$-wave multipole to the spin splitting.

**Key observables.** Valence Electron Density (VED) anisotropy, the $g$-wave multipole term ($\alpha$), and the magnetic flipping ratio (FR) from polarized neutron diffraction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The $g$-wave multipole amplitude ($	ext{Q}_{43}$), which was quantified to $0.21(6)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The VED is approximated as a linear combination of electric multipoles, and the spin splitting is modeled as being proportional to the $g$-wave multipole amplitude.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the predicted antiferroic pattern of electric hexadecapoles, the crystal structure of CrSb, and the experimentally observed VED anisotropy around the Cr sublattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical necessity of higher-order multipoles in altermagnets, to presenting the experimental VED measurements confirming the $g$-wave multipole, and finally linking this electric order to measurable magnetic properties like spin splitting via polarized neutron diffraction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Over the past few years, altermagnets have emerged as a new class of collinear magnets with broken time-reversal symmetry, offering novel opportunities for spintronics beyond conventional magnets. Rather than from net magnetization, as in ferromagnets, the unconventional time-reversal symmetry breaking of altermagnets originates from antiferroic magnetic dipoles locked to higher-order multipoles. Here we report the direct visualization of a $g$-wave altermagnetic multipole in the canonical altermagnet CrSb. Combining high-energy synchrotron X-ray diffraction with valence electron density (VED) analysis, we uncover a pronounced directional anisotropy of the VED distribution alternating between Cr sublattices. This evidences the antiferroic order of electric hexadecapoles predicted in $g$-wave altermagnets. Its coexistence with antiferroic magnetic dipoles induces ferroic magnetic multipoles, as probed by polarized neutron diffraction. We further identify a microscopic model of altermagnetism that directly relates the $g$-wave multipole and the $g$-wave spin splitting. Through direct observation and quantification of multipoles, this study provides a real-space fingerprint of altermagnetism and establishes a general probe of hidden multipole order in quantum materials.

</details>

### [Optimal Fusion Strategies for Quantum Computation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02559v1)

**Authors:** Kenneth Goodenough, Andrew Landahl, Joon Lee, Antonio Russo, Kevin Thompson  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02559v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.02559_figures/2609.02559_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: An encoded single qubit quantum state undergoes σ-fusions for each pair of qubits in (L, LA). The type of each fusion is labeled as a subscript of the blue edges on the leftmost figure. The successful fusions implement a Bell state measurement on the corresponding qubits while a failure implements single qubit measurements in a basis determined by the type of the fusion. If enough fusions succeed then the logical state on L is teleported to LB (depicted on the right side of the figure).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper characterizes the optimal strategies for performing logical quantum fusions when physical measurements are probabilistic. It establishes necessary and sufficient conditions for 'perfect fusion' in stabilizer codes, solving an open problem for parity-check codes. The work also introduces a graph parameter derived from graph theory that quantifies the robustness of these quantum protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between graph parameters (like the maximum LC degree) and the operational robustness of quantum computation (perfect fusion) provides a deep, structural insight into the limits of quantum networking protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to find optimal 'fusion strategies' to maximize the success probability of logical fusions in quantum computation, especially when physical fusions are inherently probabilistic.

**Main result.** It provides a complete characterization of perfect fusion strategies for single-qubit encoded stabilizer codes and shows that random graph codes admit such strategies with high probability.

**Method.** The analysis uses techniques from quantum error correction theory, relating logical failure to the presence of non-trivial logical operators in the failure measurement outcomes, and employs graph theory concepts like Local-Complementation equivalence.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum information processing using Quantum Error Correcting Codes (QECCs) encoded into stabilizer codes, where logical operations are performed via probabilistic physical fusions between qubits.

**Key observables.** Fusion Strategy, Perfect Strategy, Fusion Distance, $\Delta_{	ext{LC}}(G')$ (maximum LC degree).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The code parameters $[[n, 1, d]]$, the failure basis choices (X, Y, Z), and the graph structure $G'$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to non-adaptive fusion strategies, and the initial results for random graph codes are compared to the general case for random stabilizer codes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates encoded single qubit state fusion processes; Figure 2 shows various submatrices (Laplacian, Adjacency) used in the random graph analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing perfect fusion strategies for stabilizer codes (relating it to logical operators) to analyzing the probability of successful outcomes in random graph settings using advanced random matrix theory techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Logical fusions are important for a number of tasks in quantum information, such as quantum error correction and quantum repeaters. In the photonic setting one must contend with the fact that physical fusions are probabilistic (i.e.~the associated qubits are measured in product bases), which---depending on the failures and the associated bases---can lead to a failure on the logical level. The choice of failure basis of each qubit is known as a fusion strategy, and finding good fusion strategies is important for optimizing performance of fusion-based quantum computation. Here we provide a complete characterization when $k=1$ qubits are encoded, and in particular characterize those codes and fusion strategies such that all but one physical fusion can fail, i.e.~\emph{perfect fusion strategies}. In doing so, we recover previously known perfect fusion strategies, and find perfect fusion strategies for quantum parity-check codes, answering an open question. We furthermore show that perfect fusion strategies are generic: random $[[n, 1, d]]$ graph codes admit a perfect fusion strategy with probability exponentially close to $1$. Additionally we motivate the study of a new graph parameter, namely the maximum degree of a graph at a given vertex taken over all LC-equivalent graphs, by giving a new operationally meaningful interpretation of it.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a quantum meta-complexity framework to characterize one-way puzzles, proving that their existence is equivalent to a specific quantum average-case hardness problem. The authors establish a quantum coding theorem and highlight that string-based complexity is sufficient for puzzles but potentially insufficient for more complex quantum state generators.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the rigorous connection between information-theoretic complexity measures (like Kolmogorov complexity) and the existence of cryptographic primitives provides deep theoretical insights into the limits of quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the existence of one-way puzzles using time-bounded quantum meta-complexity, establishing a necessary and sufficient condition based on quantum average-case hardness.

**Main result.** It proves that one-way puzzles exist if and only if the gap problem for the quantum time-bounded complexity measure ($pKt_q$) is weakly quantum-average-hard at subexponential time bounds.

**Method.** The methodology involves defining the probabilistic time-bounded quantum program complexity ($pKt_q$) and utilizing quantum coding theorems derived via amplitude amplification to link information-theoretic bounds to computational hardness.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum computation models applied to classical strings, specifically analyzing one-way puzzles (minimal search primitives for quantum cryptography) and comparing them to one-way state generators.

**Key observables.** Probabilistic time-bounded quantum program complexity ($pKt_q$), sampling mass ($\delta$), and the hardness of the gap problem ($	ext{Gap-}pKt^*_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time bounds, particularly subexponential time $t^*(n) = 2n/2	ext{poly}(n)$, and the probability $\delta$ of the quantum sampler.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core results rely on several conjectures, notably the polynomial-time coding theorem (Conjecture 5.3), which remains unproven.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 maps cryptographic primitives to complexity measures; Figure 3 shows the surprisal distribution for a random circuit sampler; Figure 4 validates the quantum decoder's scaling against theoretical bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the meta-complexity program by defining $pKt_q$, proving the quantum coding theorem (Theorem 5.1), establishing the main equivalence (Theorem 6.1), and concluding with open conjectures regarding state generators and the barrier conjecture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We initiate the time-bounded meta-complexity program for quantum cryptography. Recent work characterizes one-way puzzles, the minimal search primitive of quantum cryptography without one-way functions, by the average-case hardness of approximating the plain, uncomputable Kolmogorov complexity over quantumly samplable distributions; the classical program of Liu and Pass, by contrast, lives at polynomial time bounds. We define a probabilistic time-bounded quantum program complexity pKq^t for classical strings and prove two unconditional theorems. First, a quantum coding theorem: any string output by a quantum polynomial-time sampler with probability delta admits a description of the information-theoretically optimal length log(1/delta) plus logarithmic terms, decodable by a quantum machine in time O(sqrt(1/delta)) times a polynomial, via amplitude amplification over the coherently executed sampler. Second, an exact characterization at subexponential time: one-way puzzles exist if and only if the gap problem for pKq at time bound 2^(n/2) poly(n) is weakly quantum-average-hard, refining the plain-complexity characterizations. We then isolate the polynomial-time coding theorem as the single load-bearing open conjecture of the program, prove that it implies the full polynomial-time characterization, analyze why the classical derandomization proof resists quantization, and formulate a relativized barrier conjecture delimiting string-valued meta-complexity at one-way puzzles. Conjectures are labeled as such throughout.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study calculates the interaction corrections to the energy levels of a superconducting junction due to coupling with an electromagnetic environment. By employing advanced techniques like the Keldysh formalism and diagrammatic expansions, the authors derive formulas showing that these environmental effects can significantly alter superconducting properties, even when the coupling appears weak.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum electrodynamics in condensed matter, as it provides quantitative predictions for how environmental dissipation and coupling modify fundamental superconducting quantum properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates how coupling a short superconducting junction to an electromagnetic environment modifies the ground state energies and addition energies of its Andreev bound states.

**Main result.** The interaction corrections are derived, showing that the relative correction scale can be governed by $L G_Q \Delta$ rather than the naive $G_Q Z$, and these corrections can be significant even when the coupling strength $G_Q Z$ is small.

**Method.** The authors use a theoretical framework involving the calculation of junction admittance via the Keldysh technique and diagrammatic expansion (Feynman diagrams) to account for environmental coupling.

**Model / system.** The system is a short superconducting junction connecting two superconductors, whose energy levels (Andreev bound states) are perturbed by an external electromagnetic environment modeled either by an external impedance or the Mattis-Bardeen model.

**Key observables.** Interaction corrections to the even ground state energy ($\Delta E(e)$), the odd doublet addition energy ($\delta_{	ext{odd}}$), and the singlet addition energy ($\delta_{	ext{even}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting energy gap ($\Delta$), conductance quantum ($G_Q$), environmental impedance ($Z$), external inductance ($L$), and various frequency scales ($\omega_H, \omega_L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the junction is 'short' and employs approximations based on the relative strength of coupling ($G_Q Z \ll 1$), while also exploring non-perturbative regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, the impedance models for the environment, and the decomposition of the state-resolved junction admittance as a function of frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical system and environmental models, deriving general formulas for interaction corrections, calculating specific energy shifts ($\Delta E_e, \delta_{	ext{odd}}, \delta_{	ext{even}}$) using diagrammatic techniques, and analyzing the results in various physical regimes (small phases, near gap edges).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here we present a detailed theoretical investigation of the environment-induced interaction correction to the ground states and addition energies in a short superconducting junction. We derive general formulas for these corrections linking them to the frequency-dependent admittance of the junction and the environment impedance. We specify two environmental models: that of an external impedance and the Mattis-Bardeen model. In both cases we assume $G_Q Z \ll 1$, where $G_Q$ is the conductance quantum and $Z$ the typical environmental resistance.   While an expectation is that the typical scale of the relative correction is of the order $G_Q Z$, we have found that in many cases the dimensionless scale $L G_Q Δ$ provides a better estimation, where $Δ$ is the superconducting energy gap and $L$ an external inductance. We discuss the logarithmic renormalizations of the energies by low- and high-frequency phase fluctuations and single out a non-superconducting contribution related to the renormalization of transmission eigenvalues. We investigate in detail the peculiarities of the corrections at: i. small phases, where the interaction causes a current jump, ii. Andreev bound state energies close to the gap edge, iii. Andreev bound state energies close to Fermi level. In all these cases the correction may become comparable with the unperturbed energy, even for $G_Q Z \ll 1$, and we briefly sketch non-perturbative models relevant for the situations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that magnetoelectric phase transitions are governed by the condensation-like dynamics of an axion quasiparticle. By applying an extended Ginzburg-Landau framework analogous to BEC theory, the authors derive quantitative relationships linking the static axion angle, the transition temperature, and the material's intrinsic coupling strengths. This provides a novel, unified theoretical description for understanding ME effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between the critical dynamics of a condensed matter phase transition (ME transition) and the universal physics of BEC provides a powerful, unifying theoretical lens for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in solids.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a macroscopic theory explaining the dynamical origin of magnetoelectric (ME) phase transitions, which are experimentally observed but lack a comprehensive theoretical framework.

**Main result.** The ME phase transition can be successfully modeled by treating the axion quasiparticle (AQ) field as undergoing a Bose-Einstein Condensation (BEC)-like process, yielding scaling laws between the static AQ angle and the transition temperature.

**Method.** The authors employ an extended Ginzburg-Landau (GL) theory, linearizing the equations of motion around the critical temperature and analyzing the resulting dynamics in the frequency domain.

**Model / system.** The system involves coupled dynamics of magnetization (M), polarization (P), and the axion angle ($	heta$) in ME materials, modeled using a free energy functional and incorporating concepts from Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya Interaction (DMI).

**Key observables.** Static AQ angle ($	heta_0$), AQ frequency ($\omega_a$), and the ME phase transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phenomenological coefficients ($\lambda_M, \lambda_	heta$), damping coefficients ($\eta_M, \eta_P$), and the DMI strength ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on phenomenological damping terms inserted by hand, the assumption of spatially homogeneous solutions, and treating the AQ dynamics as a weakly interacting Bose gas.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relationship between the AQ angle and temperature, showing power-law scaling ($	heta_0 \propto T_c^{3/4}$) that fits experimental data, and depict the BEC phase diagram.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the ME coupling, formulating the dynamics via GL theory, deriving the BEC analogy for the AQ field, and finally deriving quantitative relationships between $	heta_0$, $\omega_a$, and $T_c$ by solving the linearized equations of motion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetoelectric phase transitions have been experimentally studied, but no macroscopic theory has been proposed to explain their dynamical origin. In this work, we assume that the axion quasiparticle with frequency undergoes a condensation like process. We show that these magnetoelectric phase transitions can be described within a Ginzbur Landau framework by introducing a coupled dynamic parameter, the axion angle, which is proportional to the magnetoelectric coeffcient. We derive relations between the static axion angle, the axion frequency, and the phase transition temperature for different magnetoelectric materials, respectively, and compare these calculations with existing experimental results. We also connect the artificially designed Dzyaloshinskii Moriya interaction with the axion condensate like process, so that the relation between the static axion angle and the experimentally measured frequency shift can be obtained.

</details>

### [Chemical pressure and vacancies in crystals under nonhydrostatic stress](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02435v1)

**Summary.** This work develops a thermodynamic model for crystalline solids that accounts for the coupling between mechanical stress and the concentration of vacancies via a chemically induced pressure. By deriving governing equations from non-equilibrium thermodynamics, the authors propose an alternative to standard theories, showing how chemical potential gradients act as internal body forces under external stress.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical condensed matter, the rigorous application of non-equilibrium thermodynamics and the coupling of multiple degrees of freedom (mechanical strain and chemical composition) mirrors complex open quantum systems where energy and particle exchange are coupled.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the coupling between mechanical stress and chemical equilibrium (composition/vacancy concentration) in crystalline solids under nonhydrostatic stress.

**Main result.** The proposed chemical pressure model generates an internal density-dependent stress that modifies the standard Larché-Cahn (LC) thermomechanical response, showing qualitative agreement with LC theory for surface tractions.

**Method.** The theory is derived using non-equilibrium continuum thermodynamic methods, formulating governing equations based on free energy density and chemical potential gradients.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-component crystal with a fixed number of lattice sites, modeled as a compressible lattice gas. The coupling mechanism is based on letting the site binding energy vary with spatial density, generating a chemical pressure.

**Key observables.** Chemical pressure, vacancy number ($N_v$), stress tensor (Cauchy/Piola), and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonhydrostatic stress (surface tractions, gravitation), temperature ($T$), and lattice site density ($s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to equilibrium properties, and the model assumes a single-component crystal with no interstitial atoms.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first defining the free energy density incorporating elastic and binding energy terms. It then derives coupled balance equations for force and chemical potential, showing how the chemical pressure gradient acts as both a drift force and an internal body force.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inhomogeneous stress is a driving force for diffusion of vacancies in crystals. The other way around a non-uniform distribution of vacancies induces stress. The accepted theory of composition-stress coupling in crystals is Larché-Cahn (LC) theory. Composition is defined in terms of the population of lattice sites with a limit of one-particle per site. Coupling is modelled by adding a compositional strain term to the elastic strain in the constitutive relation for stress. An alternative mechanism, proposed here, is letting the site binding energy vary with spatial (deformed) density. This generates a chemical pressure adding to the elastic stress. The governing equations for this model are derived using non-equilibrium continuum thermodynamic methods. The gradient of the chemical pressure has a dual function acting both as the drift force for migration and as an effective internal one body force in the Cauchy equation for the elastic stress. The practical evaluation presented in the paper is restricted to equilibrium properties. We examine the effect of externally applied non-hydrostatic stress, either in the form of surface tractions or a one-body force density (gravitation). The model system is a one component crystal with a fixed number of lattice sites. The number of particles can be variable but is always smaller than the number of lattice sites. The linear elastic response is modelled by the standard Lamé stress tensor. The results for systems deformed by surface tractions are in qualitative agreement with LC theory allowing for differences in the expression for the elastic moduli. Deviations are more serious for the crystal deformed by gravitation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an in silico study on achieving magnonic lasing in constricted magnetic waveguides by modeling analogue black-hole physics. Using advanced micromagnetic simulations, the authors show that incorporating interfacial Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction significantly reduces the operational current density. The work successfully demonstrates the coherent emission of spin waves, establishing design principles for advanced spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of analogue black holes and wave lasing in magnetic systems provides a rich platform for studying non-linear wave dynamics and energy extraction, which has parallels in open quantum systems and cavity QED concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to design and understand magnonic lasing phenomena by modeling analogue black-hole physics in constricted ferromagnetic waveguides.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the incorporation of interfacial Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (iDMI) significantly lowers the critical current density required for operation, and they successfully model spin-wave lasing from thermally excited magnons.

**Method.** The research employs in silico micromagnetic simulations, utilizing a two-dimensional Poisson solver to obtain realistic current-density profiles for the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a thin ferromagnetic waveguide with bow-tie constrictions, where magnetization dynamics are governed by the LLG equation modified to include Spin-Transfer-Torque (STT). The system exhibits magnonic black-hole analogues due to the Doppler shift induced by spin-polarized currents.

**Key observables.** Resonant spin-wave amplification, spin-wave lasing emission frequency, and the critical current density ($J_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interfacial Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (iDMI) strength, applied current density ($J$), and geometric parameters of the constriction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis moves beyond idealized step-like current distributions by using realistic current profiles from a Poisson solver, and it models lasing by including stochastic thermal fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the waveguide, the dependence of critical current on external fields, the spin-wave dispersion relations showing gap opening, and space-time diagrams confirming resonant amplification and lasing.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework (LLG equation, black-hole analogy) to simulating the basic resonance in constricted geometries, then refining the model by incorporating iDMI to reduce operating thresholds, and finally demonstrating coherent spin-wave lasing using thermal excitation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Analogue black-hole systems have been proposed in various physical platforms, including magnetic materials, offering rich physics and promising applications such as wave lasing. However, their practical realization and characterization remain largely unexplored. Here, we present an in silico study of magnonic black-hole phenomena in constricted ferromagnetic waveguides driven by spin-polarized currents. Using micromagnetic simulations with a two-dimensional Poisson solver to obtain realistic current-density profiles, we demonstrate the formation of a double-hole cavity bounded by analogue event horizons, enabling resonant spin-wave amplification. We characterize the resonances as a function of magnetic and geometric parameters and identify the corresponding spin-wave modes. We further show that gradual tapers enhance transmission by suppressing spin-wave reflections, while interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction can mimic the current-induced Doppler shift, substantially reducing the critical current density required for applications and, consequently, Joule heating. Finally, we demonstrate a spin-wave laser in which thermally excited spin waves undergo selective amplification and coherent emission at well-defined resonance frequencies. These results provide design principles for magnonic analogues of gravitational systems and point toward their potential for advanced spintronic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models the phase synchronization of fish schools separated by a gap, treating the interaction as mediated solely by vision. By coupling two stochastic bistable oscillators, the authors quantitatively reproduce how synchronization strength decays with the physical separation distance. This establishes a connection between collective biological behavior and the physics of coupled stochastic oscillators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical framework of coupled stochastic oscillators and the analysis of non-equilibrium steady states, particularly the decay laws derived for the coupling parameter $\lambda(d)$, provide a rich analogy for understanding information transfer in complex, non-equilibrium physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how sensory coupling mediates phase coherence between two spatially separated, confined active systems, focusing on information transfer across a physical boundary.

**Main result.** The phase synchronization strength decays systematically with the gap distance, following a stretched exponential law, confirming that visual coupling is distance-dependent.

**Method.** The authors combine experimental measurements of fish school orientation with theoretical modeling using coupled stochastic bistable oscillators.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two fish schools separated by a gap, modeled by two coupled stochastic bistable oscillators. The dynamics are analyzed using coupled differential equations for the orientation angles.

**Key observables.** Phase difference distribution $P(\varphi)$, synchronization order parameter $R$, and Mutual Information $I(	heta_r; 	heta_l)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength $J$ (quantifying visual information transfer), bistability strength $K$, and the gap distance $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the solid partitions eliminate hydrodynamic coupling, isolating vision as the primary interaction channel, and that the dynamics can be reduced to coupled noisy bistable oscillators.

**Figures summary.** Figures show time series of orientations, phase-difference distributions $P(\varphi)$ varying with gap distance $d$, and plots demonstrating the distance dependence of synchronization measures ($R$ and $I$) and the coupling parameter $\lambda(d)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical setup and the coupled oscillator model, derives the stationary distribution for the phase difference, and then systematically analyzes how this distribution, and related measures like $R$ and $I$, change as a function of the separation distance $d$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how sensory coupling mediates phase coherence between spatially separated, confined active systems. Using two fish schools separated by a water-filled transparent gap, we isolate vision as the primary interaction channel. Under boundary confinement, each school's center-of-mass heading exhibits noisy bistable dynamics, spontaneously switching between two antiparallel directions along the partition wall. Turning events in one school bias the orientation of the other, driving distance-dependent phase synchronization. We model this behavior using two coupled stochastic bistable oscillators whose exact solution quantitatively reproduces the empirical results. Our work establishes a precise connection between nonequilibrium active systems and coupled stochastic oscillators, offering insight into boundary-spanning information transfer in living collectives.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how breaking the interface-orientation symmetry in a grating structure, achieved by oblique metal deposition, causes asymmetric optical transmission. By combining advanced full-wave simulations with sample-reversal measurements, the authors quantify a substrate-favored transmission contrast. This demonstrates a robust method for engineering directional optical responses in metamaterials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete, experimentally verifiable platform for observing symmetry breaking in optical response, which has analogues in engineered quantum systems or anisotropic media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates and quantifies asymmetric optical transmission arising from broken interface-orientation symmetry (IOS) in grating metallizations.

**Main result.** The oblique deposition of metal creates a one-sided, louver-like structure that leads to measurable, substrate-favored directional transmission ratios, which are successfully predicted by full-wave Maxwell calculations.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements using sample-reversal techniques with theoretical predictions derived from full-wave Maxwell simulations (FDTD).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a diffraction grating metallized with silver (Ag) via oblique-angle deposition, creating a self-shadowed, one-sided profile. The analysis uses principles of reciprocity and diffraction theory.

**Key observables.** Directional transmittance ratio ($T_{	ext{back}}/T_{	ext{front}}$), polarization-dependent contrast, and broadband substrate-favored transmission.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deposition angle ($\alpha$), grating period ($d$), and wavelength ($\lambda$), with optimal asymmetry observed near $45^\circ$ deposition angle.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes ideal ballistic growth and that the observed asymmetry is entirely due to the broken IOS, while the zeroth-order specular reflection remains reciprocal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the one-sided Ag deposition profile, measured substrate-favored ratios for various periods, and comparisons between computed Maxwell results and scalar wave-packet models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical basis using reciprocity, details the fabrication via oblique deposition, presents the full-wave simulation framework, and validates the predictions with quantitative sample-reversal measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reciprocal optical structures can transmit finite-angle illumination asymmetrically when the two illumination directions couple differently to the available diffraction channels. We realize this in a single overlay-free step: oblique metallization of a conventional diffraction grating, where ballistic self-shadowing grows a one-sided, louver-like silver profile. Electron microscopy and Ag mapping show the one-sided silver redistribution on oblique-coated films and its absence on a normal-incidence control. Full-wave Maxwell calculations on the ballistic-growth geometry predict broadband substrate-favored transmission, carried by the transverse-magnetic (TM) polarization in the diffraction regime (contrast up to 2.1) and polarization-independent and spectrally flat (1.5-1.9) deep in the geometric regime; the rejected power is redirected predominantly into specular reflection, and the deposition angle selects the magnitude and sign of the effect (optimum near $45^\circ$). Calibrated three-color sample-reversal measurements yield dark-corrected substrate-favored ratios that persist in both output-analyzer projections on a $45^\circ$-coated 1-$μ$m-period film (up to 1.8) and lie within the computed geometric-regime band on a 30-$μ$m-period film (1.5-1.8, with reference-normalized absolute transmittances).

</details>

(d)–(f) present the real-space local topological marker (LTM) C with respect to ky at the critical ESP strengths of (a)–(c), respectively. The three curves correspond to three different locations: the left boundary, center, and right boundary. (g)–(i) illustrate the real-space variation of the...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'topological lithography,' a method to create robust topological states by applying controllable external scalar potentials to existing systems. By modifying the effective mass term, the approach bypasses the need for complex material fabrication, allowing for programmable and reconfigurable topological patterns. This universality is demonstrated across mechanical, electronic, and circuit platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using external fields to engineer topological properties in real space, rather than relying on material composition or symmetry breaking, offers a highly tunable paradigm for designing quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional topological insulators require distinct bulk symmetries, limiting flexibility; the goal is to induce tunable topological states anywhere (edge, bulk, remote) using only external fields.

**Main result.** An external scalar potential field can induce programmable topological states by modifying the Jackiw-Rebbi mechanism, enabling 'topological lithography' across mechanical, electronic, and acoustic platforms.

**Method.** The approach uses an External Scalar Potential (ESP) field to modify the effective mass term in the Hamiltonian, inducing a local topological marker (LTM) characterizing the transition.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled on a hexagonal lattice, analyzed via tight-binding Hamiltonians, and experimentally validated using mechanical vibrational systems and LC oscillatory circuits.

**Key observables.** Local Topological Marker (LTM), band structure evolution, and in-gap mode transmission ratios.

**Important parameters / regimes.** ESP strength ($\gamma$), potential shape (quadratic, Gaussian), and lattice geometry parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to map the physics onto effective 1D chains or lattice models, and that the external field control is highly precise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between traditional and ESP-induced edge states, the evolution of band structures with ESP strength, and experimental setups showing LTM mapping and lithographic patterning.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the limitations of conventional TIs to introducing the ESP mechanism, detailing the theoretical framework (JR mechanism, LTM), validating it across multiple physical platforms (mechanical, electronic, LC circuits), and concluding with demonstrations of programmable topological lithography.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological insulators (TIs), recognized for their robust boundary states and unconventional phase transitions, have emerged as one of the most impactful discoveries in recent decades, attracting considerable interest across diverse fields. However, conventional TIs require topological contrasts between adjacent bulk regions, typically achieved by distinct symmetries, which limits their flexibility and broader applicability. In this work, we introduce a "lithography" approach to inducing topological states by applying external fields in time-reversal-symmetric systems. These states transcend the conventional bulk topology design paradigm and offer exceptional tunability: they can exist at geometric edges, within the bulk, or even be induced remotely. Because the external field is highly controllable, the induced states are programmable, reconfigurable, and can be easily tailored into desirable patterns. Theoretically, we demonstrate that the external field modifies the Jackiw-Rebbi mechanism, inducing a real-space topological transition characterized by a local topological marker (LTM). We further establish an extended valley bulk-edge correspondence, which explains both the conventional scenario and our findings. Our results, validated across mechanical, electronic, and acoustic platforms, highlight the broad applicability of this approach to various systems. This work not only advances the theory of topology but also enhances the diversity, tunability, and practical implementation of topological states and materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel optical technique to determine the spatial rank of higher-wave magnetic order in altermagnets. By measuring the absorption spectrum using finite photon momentum, the authors show that a joint Fourier projection isolates the magnetic harmonic's rank via a characteristic power-law dependence on momentum. This method provides a robust diagnostic tool for characterizing complex magnetic textures in materials like MnTe.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique of using momentum-resolved spectroscopy to extract higher-order spatial harmonics is a powerful, non-linear optical diagnostic tool applicable to complex magnetic order parameters, bridging condensed matter theory with advanced quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is optically identifying the spatial rank of higher-wave altermagnetism because local electric-dipole response cannot uniquely resolve distinct continuum harmonics.

**Main result.** By utilizing finite photon momentum and a joint Fourier projection, the spatial rank ($\ell$) can be selectively isolated, yielding a power law scaling of $|T_\ell| \propto q^{\ell-2}$.

**Method.** The authors derive a rank-matching rule and employ a joint Fourier projection in polarization and momentum angle, which is verified by a Ward-consistent finite-q microscopic calculation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on altermagnetic materials exhibiting higher-wave order (d-, g-, or i-wave), using MnTe as a testbed. The analysis involves calculating the joint Fourier projection of the spin-resolved absorption rate.

**Key observables.** The joint Fourier projection $T_\ell(q, \omega)$, which exhibits a power law scaling $|T_\ell| \propto q^{\ell-2}$ corresponding to the rank $\ell$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The photon momentum $q$ is crucial for achieving rank selectivity; the scaling exponent is $\ell-2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the finite photon momentum supplies the necessary missing spatial rank information, and the joint projection successfully filters out lower-order crystalline aliases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the rank-matching principle via joint angular tomography ($d: q^0, g: q^2, i: q^4$) and show that the parent $g$-wave component remains strongly encoded in MnTe despite strong SOC effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical necessity of finite photon momentum for rank-selective tomography, derives the projection formula, and then applies this method to first-principles calculations on MnTe to confirm the persistence of the higher-wave magnetic texture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Identifying the spatial rank of higher-wave altermagnetic order optically is challenging because local electric-dipole response does not uniquely resolve distinct continuum harmonics. We show that finite photon momentum turns one-photon spin-resolved absorption into a rank-selective tomography. For the planar $|m|=\ell$ sector of an even-parity $\ell$-wave component, a joint Fourier projection in polarization and momentum angle isolates $\mathcal T_\ell\proptoη_\ell q^{\ell-2}$, yielding the hierarchy $d:q^0$, $g:q^2$, and $i:q^4$; phase changes track rotations of the selected magnetic harmonic. A Ward-consistent finite-$q$ microscopic calculation reproduces these powers without imposing them. Although discrete crystal symmetry can generate lower-order local aliases, they are orthogonal to the selected momentum harmonic and cannot contaminate it below $q^{\ell-2}$. In MnTe, whose nonrelativistic parent order is three-dimensional $g$ wave while spin--orbit coupling lowers the exact relativistic spin-momentum-locking symmetry, first-principles calculations show that more than $99.9\%$ of the Fourier power of the Néel-projected $A$-region spin-energy contrast remains in the parent $g$-wave-derived $m=3$ harmonic. Structured near fields place the required momentum window within experimental reach.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the anomalous paramagnetic superfluid response in altermagnetic superconductors. Using quasiclassical Green's functions, the authors show that the inherent anisotropy of the altermagnetic state causes a measurable paramagnetic Meissner effect. This effect is highly sensitive to external fields and impurity levels, providing a new diagnostic tool for gapless superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of unconventional superconducting responses, particularly those involving magnetic order (altermagnetism), provides rich theoretical ground for understanding how broken symmetries influence macroscopic quantum phenomena like the Meissner effect, which is relevant to advanced quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence and characteristics of an anomalous (paramagnetic) superfluid response in superconducting materials that possess altermagnetic order.

**Main result.** The altermagnetic state leads to anisotropic gapless superconductivity, manifesting a paramagnetic Meissner effect strongest when the altermagnetic and Zeeman fields are parallel and in clean samples.

**Method.** The analysis employs quasiclassical theory using the Eilenberger equation framework to calculate the linear response current density via the Meißner kernel.

**Model / system.** The system is an altermagnetic superconductor subjected to an out-of-plane Zeeman field. The theoretical framework incorporates anisotropic exchange fields, Zeeman coupling, and impurity scattering into the Green's function formalism.

**Key observables.** Paramagnetic Meißner kernel components ($K_{ij}$), momentum-resolved Density of States (DOS), and the superconducting free energy ($\Delta\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strength of the altermagnetic field, the Zeeman field magnitude ($h$), and the impurity scattering rate ($\Gamma_{imp}$) relative to the superconducting gap ($\Delta_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes weak altermagnetic exchange fields compared to the Fermi energy, allowing for a nearly spherical Fermi surface approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin-split Fermi surfaces, momentum-resolved DOS showing gap closing, and the Meißner kernel components ($K_{xx}, K_{yy}$) as functions of applied fields and impurity concentrations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the theoretical framework (quasiclassical Green's functions) to calculating the superfluid response (Meißner kernel), deriving the key results regarding anisotropy and impurity effects, and concluding with the physical implications of the paramagnetic response.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report on the emergence of the anomalous (paramagnetic) superfluid response in altermagnetic superconductors at arbitrary impurity concentrations. Due to anisotropic gapless superconductivity, altermagnetic superconductors with an out-of-plane Zeeman field display an anisotropic paramagnetic Meissner effect. The effect is strongest for parallel altermagnetic and Zeeman exchange field vectors and in the clean sample. The presence of nonmagnetic impurities leads to isotropisation and, consequently, weakens the effect; however, the paramagnetic response sustains intermediate amounts of impurities in the system. As demonstrated in recent experiments, microwave superfluid stiffness measurements can serve as a sensitive probe of gapless superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the direct experimental observation of flat electronic bands in twisted bilayer graphene grown via CVD, overcoming a major hurdle in scaling up MATBG research. By using nano-ARPES, the authors confirmed that CVD-grown samples exhibit electronic properties comparable to exfoliated samples. This validates CVD as a viable platform for studying strongly correlated physics in moiré materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The realization of correlated electronic states in a scalable, solid-state platform like CVD graphene is crucial for studying strongly correlated electron physics outside of highly controlled, small-sample environments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to determine if the fragile flat-band and correlated electronic states characteristic of magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG) could be realized using scalable, large-area growth methods like Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD).

**Main result.** The study successfully observed electronic bands in CVD-grown TBG that are comparable to those measured in exfoliated samples, confirming the potential for scalable moiré materials hosting flat bands.

**Method.** The research employed room-temperature nano-Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (nano-ARPES) on CVD-assembled TBG domains, supplemented by STM and advanced data fitting techniques.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Twisted Bilayer Graphene (TBG) formed by stacking CVD-grown graphene layers near the magic angle. The electronic structure is analyzed using models derived from the moiré superlattice potential.

**Key observables.** Flat-band dispersion, gap opening near the Fermi level, Moiré lattice parameter (12.9 nm), and the relaxation length of the near-MA configuration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($\sim 1.11^\circ$), doping shift ($> 100$ meV), and the domain size compatible with transport experiments.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed p-doping shift is attributed to synchrotron-radiation-induced photodoping, and the analysis relies on fitting EDCs with Lorentzian/Voigt lineshapes.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM imaging of the moiré superlattice, ARPES-revealed electronic dispersion with flat bands, and position-resolved EDC fitting used to map the electronic configuration across the domain.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the experimental feasibility of CVD-grown MATBG, through quantitative ARPES measurements confirming flat bands, to detailed analysis mapping domain homogeneity and electronic structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Advances in chemical vapor deposition (CVD) growth have driven graphene crystal quality to unprecedented levels, yet it is still unknown whether this route can realize the fragile flat-band and correlated states of the magic-angle (MA) twisted bilayer graphene (TBG). Here, we report on the experimental observation by room-temperature nano-angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (nano-ARPES) of flat bands in a TBG sample close to the MA, assembled via a grow-and-stack protocol based on low-pressure CVD of graphene on copper. Our study indicates electronic bands fully comparable to those measured in exfoliation-based samples and determines the size of the largest near-MA domain to be compatible with electronic transport experiments, motivating further experiments on flat band physics in CVD-graphene.

</details>

The discrete spectrum produces coherent dephasing and finite-size recurrences. For ∆0 = 1 meV, the time unit is ℏ/∆0 ≃0.66 ps. The spectral response is strongly nonmonotonic in d. At d = 5.75ξ and 7.5ξ, most of the packet weight lies in a near- zero state, and C(t) varies slowly. At d = 7ξ and 8.25ξ,</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the spectral reorganization in a superconducting disk containing a vortex by studying the coupling between the vortex core and the boundary. Using BdG calculations, the authors show that while the localized wave packet retains high norm, the resulting spectral properties and parity memory are highly sensitive to the vortex-boundary separation. This highlights that simple measures of wave function overlap are insufficient predictors of topological memory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of topological superconductivity, Majorana fermions, and spectral dynamics in confined geometries is highly relevant to understanding topological quantum computation and superconducting quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the coupling between a vortex core and the boundary reorganizes the low-energy electronic spectrum of a finite 2D p_x+ip_y superconductor, and what this implies for the coherence and memory of Majorana wave packets.

**Main result.** A large retained norm of the reference packet does not guarantee spectral concentration or persistent parity memory, as the spectral measure and parity dynamics vary significantly with the vortex-boundary separation.

**Method.** Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) calculations are employed, supplemented by analyzing regional coherence measures and parity correlators.

**Model / system.** The system is a finite 2D p_x+ip_y superconducting disk containing a vortex, modeled using the grand-canonical mean-field Hamiltonian and solved via BdG equations.

**Key observables.** Nonmonotonic core-edge reorganization of eigenstates, retained norm of the reference packet, spectral measure (concentration/fragmentation), and signed parity correlator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vortex-boundary separation (d), superconducting gap ratio (Δ_0/E_F = 0.36), and disk radius (R=30ξ).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a finite-disk approximation and an idealized reference preparation method for the vortex core packet.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate vortex geometry and image construction; other figures show the spectral measure and parity correlator varying nonmonotonically with the vortex-boundary distance d.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian to calculating spectral reorganization via BdG, and finally analyzing the implications for parity memory using time-domain correlators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how vortex--edge coupling reorganizes the low-energy sector of a finite two-dimensional \(p_x+ip_y\) superconducting disk as a function of the vortex--boundary separation \(d\) and examine what this reorganization implies for the parity memory associated with a prescribed vortex-core Majorana wave packet, a resource relevant to Majorana-based quantum operations. Bogoliubov--de Gennes calculations reveal nonmonotonic core--edge reorganization of the lowest positive-energy finite-disk eigenstate, with particularly rapid variation near \(d\simeq7ξ\), where \(ξ\) is the coherence length. To separate this eigenstate reorganization from the spectral representation of a prescribed state, we rigidly translate a centered-vortex core-reference packet to each fixed vortex position, restrict it to the target disk, and project it, without intermediate normalization, onto the particle-hole-complete low-energy subspace. For \(Δ_0/E_F=0.36\) and disk radius \(R=30ξ\), the resulting retained norm exceeds \(0.98\) at all six sampled separations, \(4.25\leq d/ξ\leq8.25\), while, depending on \(d\), the spectral measure is concentrated near zero energy, fragmented over several low-energy levels, or dominated by finite-energy weight. Correspondingly, the signed parity correlator displays slow temporal variation, rapid coherent dephasing, or sign-changing oscillations, with possible finite-size recurrences at later times. Thus a large retained norm does not by itself imply spectral concentration or persistent parity memory.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates room-temperature control over moiré excitons in $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{WSe}_2$ heterobilayers by applying local mechanical strain via an AFM tip. By coupling strain engineering with PL spectroscopy and advanced theoretical modeling, the authors show they can not only activate these excitons at ambient temperatures but also tune their emission wavelength into the crucial second telecom window. This establishes local strain as a powerful tool for realizing practical quantum excitonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use mechanical strain as a non-electrical, on-demand knob to tune quantum optical properties (like emission wavelength) at ambient conditions is highly relevant for developing practical quantum light sources and excitonic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is overcoming the limitation of studying moiré excitons at cryogenic temperatures to enable practical, room-temperature quantum control.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that local strain engineering using an AFM tip can activate, control, and tune the emission wavelength of moiré excitons in $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{WSe}_2$ heterobilayers to the second telecom window at room temperature.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (PL spectroscopy under controlled local strain and power variation) with theoretical modeling (DFT and kinetic rate equations).

**Model / system.** The system is the moiré superlattice formed by stacking $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{WSe}_2$ heterobilayers. The physics involves quantized moiré exciton states whose properties are tuned by external mechanical strain.

**Key observables.** Photoluminescence (PL) spectra, moiré exciton resonance energies ($	ext{X}1-	ext{X}6$), spectral linewidth, and the effective emission energy as a function of applied strain.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature (300 K), local strain magnitude (tensile/compressive), excitation power ($P$), and the resulting shift towards the telecom window ($\sim 1.3 \mu	ext{m}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical model assumes the blueshift is due to repulsive dipole–dipole interactions, and the kinetic model assumes excitation populates the highest energy level followed by non-radiative relaxation.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the PL spectrum evolving dramatically under increasing strain (multiple resonances emerging), the dependence of peak amplitudes on excitation power, and quantitative plots mapping the effective emission energy against applied strain (showing both initial blueshift and subsequent redshift).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (moiré excitons), details the experimental control mechanism (local strain via AFM), analyzes the spectral signatures (power dependence and strain tuning), and supports these findings with a comprehensive theoretical model capturing the energy shifts and linewidth narrowing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré superlattices in heterobilayers of atomically thin transition metal dichalcogenides provide a versatile platform for exploring quantum many-body physics as they can trap excitons, leading to the formation of quantized moiré exciton states. However, such moiré excitons have been predominantly studied at cryogenic temperatures, which severely limits their practical applications. Here, we demonstrate room-temperature activation and control of moiré excitons in MoS$_2$/WSe$_2$ heterobilayers using local strain engineering. Applying mechanical strain with a modified atomic force microscopy tip, we observe a series of resonances attributed to interlayer exciton states confined in the moiré potential. Power-dependent photoluminescence measurements elucidate the population dynamics of the moiré exciton states, while controlled local strain enables continuous tuning of their emission wavelength to the center of the second telecom window. We provide a theoretical model that captures all experimentally observed features, including the unconventional spectral shape of the moiré exciton emission. Our findings establish local tip-induced strain as a powerful tool for the on-demand manipulation of moiré excitons, paving the way for room-temperature quantum excitonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the stacking order of h-BN layers can be reversibly switched by applying external torque, a process termed 'twistronics.' By combining AFM experiments and atomistic simulations, the authors quantify the mechanical asymmetry between the stable AA and AB stacking states. This finding paves a pathway for designing reconfigurable nano-electromechanical devices based on controlled interlayer coupling.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on mechanics, the study of phase transitions (stacking changes) driven by external fields (torque) and the resulting polarization states offers analogies to controlling quantum states in engineered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of controllably switching the interlayer stacking configuration in hexagonal boron nitride (h-BN) using external mechanical torque.

**Main result.** The stacking can be reproducibly switched between AA and AB modes, exhibiting a measurable asymmetry ratio (approx. 0.7) and showing that the peak torque scales linearly with contact area, indicating dominant edge elasticity.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements using an AFM setup to apply controlled torque with atomistic molecular dynamics (MD) simulations (LAMMPS) to model the atomic interactions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the contact interface between h-BN layers. The modeling involves simulating the torsional loading on these homojunctions, comparing flexible atomic reconstructions against more rigid theoretical models.

**Key observables.** Rotational torque (T) vs. angle ($	heta$), peak torque values, asymmetry ratio ($	ext{T}_{	ext{AB}} / 	ext{T}_{	ext{AA}'}$), and the scaling of torque with contact area ($	ext{T} \propto 	ext{A}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the stacking configurations (AA, AB), the applied external torque, and the contact area (A).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The linear scaling of torque with area suggests that interfacial elasticity is the dominant mechanism governing the twisting process, overriding predictions from purely rigid models.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mechanical setup, force-angle profiles showing torque peaks at specific angles ($60^\circ, 120^\circ$), and comparisons between experimental torque-angle profiles and MD simulation results, particularly regarding area scaling and yielding mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental measurement of torque switching in h-BN, then using atomistic simulations to reproduce and explain the observed asymmetry and the linear scaling of torque, contrasting ductile vs. brittle yielding mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twistronics of layered materials has emerged as a highly active field due to its profound implications for quantum electronics and materials engineering. However, the controllable manipulation of interlayer stacking remains a significant experimental challenge. Here, the homogeneous contact between hexagonal boron nitride layers is shown to be reproducibly switched between two distinct stable stacking configurations via an externally applied torque. Combining experiments and computational modelling, we identify the stacking order of these stable states as the commensurate AA and AB modes. These two states are associated with different rotational torque maxima, exhibiting an asymmetry ratio of 0.7, and distinct dynamics as a function of the twist angle. Moreover, the peak torque values scale linearly with contact area, highlighting the dominant role of edge elasticity in the twisting process. Given that the AA and AB stacking modes correspond to different out-of-plane electric polarization states, our findings offer a pathway for reconfigurable nano- and micro-electromechanical devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses the Stochastic Self-Consistent Harmonic Approximation (SSCHA) to model the structural and electronic properties of the superhydride YScH8 under high pressure. The authors demonstrate that neglecting quantum and anharmonic lattice effects leads to significant overestimations of the superconducting critical temperature (Tc). A full treatment is necessary to accurately predict the material's behavior, matching theory to experiment.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state superconductivity, the rigorous application of free-energy minimization and quantum fluctuations (SSCHA) to determine equilibrium structure and vibrational properties shares methodological parallels with advanced quantum many-body treatments in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to accurately predict the superconducting critical temperature (Tc) in high-pressure ternary superhydrides by accounting for the effects of quantum and anharmonic ionic motion.

**Main result.** Incorporating quantum and anharmonic effects significantly renormalizes the crystal structure and lowers the dynamical stability threshold pressure, leading to a predicted Tc of 113 K at 190 GPa, which matches experimental observations.

**Method.** The Stochastic Self-Consistent Harmonic Approximation (SSCHA) is employed to minimize the free energy, providing a quantum-mechanically consistent treatment of lattice vibrations and structural relaxation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the P4/mmm-YScH8 superhydride under extreme high pressures. The methodology involves calculating the free energy and deriving phonon frequencies from the free-energy Hessian, contrasting this with classical harmonic approximations.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature (Tc), dynamical stability threshold pressure, phonon density of states, and Eliashberg spectral function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (up to 190 GPa), electron-phonon coupling constant (lambda), and Coulomb pseudopotential (mu*).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SSCHA method is used in its static limit, which is assumed to provide accurate phonon frequencies and EPC parameters for the system.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare harmonic vs. anharmonic pressures and structural parameters, and show the decomposition of the Eliashberg spectral function into contributions from different atomic sites (H1, H2, H3, Y, Sc).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results from classical harmonic approximations against those derived from the SSCHA formalism. It first establishes the structural renormalization and pressure shift, then uses the resulting quantum structure to calculate superconducting parameters, concluding that anharmonicity is essential for quantitative agreement with experiment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent synthesis of P4/mmm-YScH$_{8}$ at 191.7 GPa with a superconducting critical temperature Tc of 113 K has attracted considerable interest in the study of ternary superhydrides. Here we systematically evaluate the effects of quantum and anharmonic motion of ions in P4/mmm-YScH$_{8}$ using the stochastic self-consistent harmonic approximation (SSCHA). We find that these effects renormalize the crystal structure and lower the dynamical stability threshold pressure from $\sim$140 GPa to $\sim$84 GPa, a reduction of about 40%. Furthermore, neglecting these effects causes a significant overestimation of the $T_{c}$, for instance by $\sim$20 K at 190 GPa. Our prediction of $T_{c}$ at 190 GPa is 113 K ($μ^{*}$= 0.13), close to the experimental observation. Analysis of the phonon density of states and Eliashberg spectral function further reveals site-dependent contributions to superconductivity. These results establish that a consistent treatment of nuclear quantum motion and anharmonicity is essential for accurately predicting superconductivity in compressed ternary hydrides.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theory to predict the third-order nonlinear thermal Hall effect in altermagnets. By analyzing symmetry constraints, the authors show that this nonlinear response is a unique signature of altermagnetism, depending critically on the orientation of the material's Néel vector. This provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool for identifying and characterizing these novel magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of Berry curvature and nonlinear transport coefficients connects to advanced topics in topological materials and non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, which are relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the symmetry-enforced third-order nonlinear thermal Hall effect (THE) in altermagnets.

**Main result.** A dominant third-order nonlinear THE is predicted for altermagnets with out-of-plane Néel order, providing a unique diagnostic tool to confirm altermagnetism and determine the Néel vector orientation.

**Method.** The authors developed a theoretical framework using Boltzmann transport theory and semiclassical wavepacket theory to calculate the transverse energy current up to the third order in the temperature gradient.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on altermagnets, which are novel collinear antiferromagnets. Calculations are performed for both electronic contributions (using materials like KV2Se2O) and magnon contributions (using insulators like MnF2).

**Key observables.** The third-order nonlinear thermal Hall coefficient ($\kappa_{3ba}$), which exhibits a characteristic $\pi$-periodic dependence on the temperature gradient direction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Néel vector orientation (out-of-plane vs. in-plane) is critical, as it dictates whether the third-order or linear THE dominates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Relaxation Time Approximation (RTA) for both electronic and magnon transport calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Berry curvature patterns for different altermagnetic types and show the calculated dependence of the third-order thermal Hall conductivity on the temperature gradient angle $\theta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first develops the general theoretical formalism for the nonlinear THE, then applies this theory to analyze specific symmetry conditions, and finally demonstrates the results using concrete examples from both metallic and insulating altermagnets.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermal Hall effect (THE) is a powerful probe of material properties, even in insulators. Here we investigate the thermal response of altermagnets by developing a theory on Berry curvature driven nonlinear THE from both electrons and magnons. We identify symmetry conditions under which the third-order THE dominates while linear and second-order contributions vanish. Notably, d-wave and i-wave altermagnets with out-of-plane Neel order satisfy these conditions, whereas in-plane order yields a dominant linear THE. Using KV2Se2O (a d-wave altermagnetic metal) and MnF2 (an altermagnetic insulator) as concrete examples, we show nonzero third-order nonlinear THE from electrons and magnons, respectively, both exhibiting a pi-periodic dependence on the direction of temperature gradient. These symmetry-guaranteed Berry curvature phenomena provide a diagnostic for altermagnetism in candidate materials and enable determination of the Neel vector orientation.

</details>
