**Summary.** This research reports the successful fabrication and characterization of a diatomic Kagome lattice using self-assembled organic molecules on a metal surface. By combining STM measurements with topological tight-binding calculations, the authors provided direct evidence for the existence of topologically protected zigzag edge states, establishing a novel supramolecular platform for quantum material research.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates how complex, synthetic supramolecular chemistry can be used to engineer and observe fundamental topological electronic states, providing a platform for studying quantum materials beyond traditional crystal growth methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was the direct observation and theoretical confirmation of zigzag edge states in a supramolecular diatomic Kagome lattice.

**Main result.** The authors provided direct evidence for these topological edge states using STM spectroscopy, confirming predictions from tight-binding calculations based on the lattice's topology.

**Method.** The study combines experimental characterization using Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) with theoretical modeling via Tight-Binding (TB) calculations and topological analysis using the Zak phase.

**Model / system.** The system is a diatomic Kagome lattice formed by the self-assembly of a triptycene derivative with phenazine moieties (Trip-Phz) on a Pb(111) surface. This structure is an extension of the honeycomb and Kagome lattices.

**Key observables.** STM topography, dI/dV spectra (showing distinct peaks), and spatial mapping of electronic states localized at the zigzag edges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice parameter of $1.92 \pm 0.04$ nm; coupling constants $t_0 = -0.141$ eV and $t_1 = -0.096$ eV; topological invariant $	ext{Z}(k_y) = \pi \pmod{2\pi}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The edge state existence is confirmed by the bulk-edge correspondence linked to the quantization of the Zak phase, despite the lattice lacking chiral symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM topographs of the lattice, dI/dV spectra confirming molecular bands and edge state signatures, and theoretical plots illustrating the Zak phase calculation confirming topological nontriviality.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system (Trip-Phz on Pb(111)), presents STM observations confirming the lattice structure and edge termination, and then uses TB calculations and Zak phase analysis to provide the theoretical underpinning and direct evidence for the topological edge states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lattice geometry plays a fundamental role in the behavior of Bloch electrons in a crystal. The diatomic Kagome lattice, an extension of the honeycomb and Kagome lattices, is predicted to give rise to emergent and topological phenomena, but its experimental investigation has been limited thus far. Here, we fabricate a diatomic Kagome lattice through self-assembly of a triptycene derivative with phenazine moieties (Trip-Phz)---a $\mathrm{C_3}$-symmetric, non-planar $π$-conjugated molecule. Our scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) observations show that Trip-Phz forms a highly ordered diatomic Kagome lattice terminated by zigzag-type edges on the Pb(111) surface. Combined STM measurements and tight-binding calculations provide direct evidence for the existence of the edge states that correspond to those of graphene. These states are topological edge states dictated by the quantization of the Zak phase and the bulk-edge correspondence.This work reveals an ideal platform for exploring quantum materials with unique lattice geometries using supramolecular technology.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work investigates the magnon Hanle effect in antiferromagnetic alpha-Fe2O3 using electrical spin injection and detection. The key finding is the observation of a nonreciprocal magnon spin signal, which changes when the roles of the injector and detector are swapped. This demonstrates the potential of antiferromagnetic magnonics for advanced spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of nonreciprocity in magnonics, linked to an antisymmetric pseudofield, provides a platform for realizing electronics-inspired phenomena in magnetic materials, relevant for understanding spin-orbit coupling effects in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to observe and characterize the nonreciprocal magnon Hanle effect in an antiferromagnetic insulator.

**Main result.** The researchers successfully demonstrated a nonreciprocal magnon spin signal in alpha-Fe2O3, where reversing the roles of the spin injector and detector alters the measured signal.

**Method.** The experiment utilizes a non-local, two-terminal geometry to measure magnon spin currents via electrical injection and detection using Pt electrodes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is alpha-Fe2O3 thin films coupled with Pt electrodes. The phenomenon studied is the magnon Hanle effect, which involves the precession of the magnon pseudospin.

**Key observables.** The magnon spin signal ($R_{	ext{magn}} = V_{	ext{det}}/I_{	ext{inj}}$), its dependence on the external magnetic field, and the difference in signal when the injector/detector roles are interchanged.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The external magnetic field ($\mathbf{H}$), particularly the compensation field (6 T) and the in-plane rotation angle ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The nonreciprocity is attributed to the presence of an antisymmetric magnonic pseudofield, which is a key theoretical deduction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) sketches the sample setup and angle dependence. Figure 1(b) shows the field dependence confirming the compensation field. Figure 1(c) compares forward and backward propagation signals, highlighting the nonreciprocity.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, measurements of the Hanle effect, and then focuses on demonstrating and analyzing the nonreciprocal nature of the signal as a function of magnetic field and angle.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The precession of the magnon pseudospin about the equilibrium pseudofield, the latter capturing the nature of magnonic eigenexcitations in an antiferromagnet, gives rise to the magnon Hanle effect. Its realization via electrically injected and detected spin transport in an antiferromagnetic insulator demonstrates its high potential for devices and as a convenient probe for magnon eigenmodes and the underlying spin interactions in the antiferromagnet. Here, we observe a non-reciprocity in the Hanle signal measured in alpha-Fe2O3 using two spatially separated Pt electrodes as spin injector and detector. Interchanging their roles alters the detected magnon spin signal. The recorded difference depends on the applied magnetic field and reverses sign when the signal passes its nominal maximum at the so-called compensation field. Our findings unlock the high potential of antiferromagnetic magnonics towards the realization of electronics-inspired phenomena.

</details>

**Authors:** Yashpal Singh, Juan E. Peralta, Koblar A. Jackson, Mark R. Pederson

**Summary.** This paper introduces a 'Wannier-first' approach to DFT, enabling the simulation of extended chiral and helical materials by constructing localized Wannier-like functions directly in real space. This method efficiently handles complex symmetries, such as screw rotations, without the computational cost of large supercells. It provides a robust, practical framework for studying materials with nontrivial periodic symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state DFT, the core numerical technique—constructing localized basis sets and solving physics problems in a real-space, symmetry-adapted manner—is highly relevant for simulating open quantum systems or molecular dynamics where localized representations are preferred over Bloch wavefunctions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient electronic structure method for extended chiral systems that can accurately incorporate complex symmetries like screw rotations without resorting to prohibitively large supercells.

**Main result.** The Wannier-first approach successfully calculates total energies and band structures for various chiral and layered materials, showing excellent agreement with reference periodic calculations.

**Method.** It uses a real-space, Wannier-like function construction within a finite Wannier domain, avoiding the explicit calculation of Bloch states during the self-consistent field (SCF) cycle.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to extended periodic systems, including chiral chains ($	ext{-C}\equiv	ext{C}	ext{-}$, $	ext{-Li-F-}$), graphene, and layered graphite structures, allowing for the natural inclusion of combined translation-rotation (screw) symmetries.

**Key observables.** Total energies, band structures, band gaps ($E_g$), and energy differences ($\delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the Wannier Domain (WD) and the convergence threshold ($\epsilon = 10^{-5}$ Hartree).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The contribution of Wannier-like functions outside the finite Wannier Domain is negligible for charge density calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the Wannier domain and localized functions; Figure 2 shows the iterative flowchart for SCF convergence; Table I compares calculated band gaps and energy differences across various systems and twist angles.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the formalism, building upon previous work, by defining the Wannier-like function optimization ($\hat{\Pi}$ operator), outlining the SCF procedure using the localized basis, and finally applying the method to benchmark systems to validate its capability with complex symmetries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a real-space formulation of DFT for extended systems in which localized Wannier-like functions are constructed directly from localized Gaussian basis functions without explicitly computing canonical Bloch-like states during the self-consistent cycle. Building on the formalism of Pederson and Lin [Phys. Rev. B \textbf{35}, 2273 (1987)], a variational set of Wannier-like functions is generated self-consistently within a finite Wannier domain and used to construct the charge density, electrostatic potential, and per cell total energy of the extended system. The occupied space can be determined entirely from the Wannier-like functions. Electronic band structures can be recovered in a post-processing step by solving the full Hamiltonian in a Bloch-like basis constructed from Gaussian orbitals. A key feature of the method is that it can incorporate combined translation--rotation, or screw, symmetries, enabling efficient simulations of chiral and helical systems with finite twist angles at essentially the same computational cost as systems described by pure translational symmetry. The approach is validated through calculations on linear and twisted $\mathrm{{-}C{\equiv}C{-}}$ and $\mathrm{{-}Li{-}F{-}}$ chains, as well as graphene, where total energies and band structures show excellent agreement with reference periodic calculations. To illustrate the ability of the method to treat three-dimensional systems, it is further applied to AA graphite, in which carbon atoms in adjacent graphene layers are aligned directly above one another, as well as helically stacked AA graphite structures. The Wannier-first framework provides a practical route for treating extended systems with nontrivial translational, rotational, and screw symmetries, and provides a natural foundation for the implementation of orbital-dependent functionals such as the Perdew--Zunger self-interaction correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that spin-pair defects in hBN can function as highly robust quantum sensors, maintaining their spin resonance even when the host material undergoes a structural phase transition to wBN under extreme pressure. By combining optical and magnetic measurements in a DAC, the authors show that high pressure is a versatile tool for both sensing and engineering quantum emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clear experimental demonstration of using extreme conditions (high pressure) not just as a challenge, but as a controllable knob to engineer and stabilize quantum emitters, which is highly relevant for solid-state quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum defects embedded in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) behave and evolve when the material undergoes structural phase transitions induced by extreme high pressure.

**Main result.** Spin-pair defects maintain robust, pressure-independent spin resonance and optical emission up to 20 GPa, while the host material undergoes an irreversible structural change from hBN to wBN, demonstrating high pressure as a tool for defect engineering.

**Method.** Experiments were conducted *in-situ* using a diamond anvil cell (DAC), employing optical photoluminescence (PL) and optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) spectroscopy across varying pressures.

**Model / system.** The platform is optically active spin-pair defects (SPs) within hBN. The system is subjected to compression up to 22 GPa, causing a structural phase transition from hexagonal to wurtzite boron nitride (wBN).

**Key observables.** PL intensity, ODMR contrast, spin resonance frequency, and structural phase identification (via Raman/TEM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (up to 22 GPa), temperature (implied ambient/cryogenic), and defect type (SP vs. V-B).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The spin-pair defect mechanism is assumed to be generic and robust, allowing the magnetic sensing function to be decoupled from the lattice structure changes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the loss of V-B PL and the persistence/enhancement of SP PL across the hBN-to-wBN phase transition, alongside ODMR measurements confirming SP robustness.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the pressure response of different defects (SP vs. V-B), demonstrates the structural phase transition using multiple techniques, and quantifies the robustness of the SP sensing mechanism across the entire pressure range.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extreme pressures can transform materials and their properties, but probing these in-situ is made challenging by the small sample volumes and access requirements demanded by diamond anvil cells. Quantum defects offer a route to local measurements under such conditions, yet their sensing performance can be dictated by pressure-induced changes in their own host material. On the other hand, pressure may also be harnessed as a tool to engineer and stabilize new quantum defects with emergent functionalities. Here, we demonstrate both aspects within a unified platform based on optically active spin-pair defects in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN). As robust quantum sensors under pressure, these spin-1/2 systems retain pressure-independent spin resonances up to 20 GPa while maintaining or even enhancing their optical emission, in stark contrast to the spin-1 boron-vacancy centre in the same material. Simultaneously, we show that compression acts as a means of quantum defect engineering: the starting hBN undergoes an irreversible transformation into wurtzite boron nitride (wBN), during which the defect landscape is reconfigured. Spin-pair sensors are seen to persist across this structural transition, however, depending on the starting material we also observe new, highly fluorescent defects in the wBN phase. These results establish spin-pair defects in boron nitride as pressure-resilient quantum sensors while highlighting high pressure itself as a versatile pathway for creating and tuning quantum emitters.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates magnon excitations in the altermagnetic metal $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$ using RPA calculations on a derived tight-binding Hamiltonian. The authors demonstrate that altermagnetism dictates both the magnon dispersion and its anisotropic decay, while SOC opens a significant gap, making the material promising for spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of magnon damping due to coupling to the particle-hole continuum, combined with the prediction of directionally selective transport, provides a rich platform for understanding spin dynamics in correlated magnetic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin excitations (magnons) in the metallic altermagnetic compound $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$, focusing on how altermagnetic symmetry and spin-orbit coupling control the magnon dispersion and decay.

**Main result.** The calculations reveal characteristic degeneracies and directional splittings in the magnon spectra, and the inclusion of SOC opens a sizable anisotropy gap ($\approx 6 	ext{ meV}$) at the $\Gamma$-point, suggesting potential room-temperature magnetic stability.

**Method.** The research employs a fermionic Hamiltonian derived from *ab initio* calculations, computing transverse spin susceptibilities within the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) to determine the magnon dispersion and linewidths.

**Model / system.** The system is the metallic, quasi-two-dimensional altermagnetic vanadium oxyselenide, $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$. The model uses a multi-orbital tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating both hopping terms and spin-orbit coupling ($H = H_0 + H_{	ext{SOC}}$).

**Key observables.** Magnon dispersion relation, magnon linewidths/lifetimes, angular dependence of magnon energies, and the $\Gamma$-point anisotropy gap ($\approx 6 	ext{ meV}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** SOC parameters ($\lambda_V = 22 	ext{ meV}$, $\lambda_{	ext{Se}} = 206 	ext{ meV}$), and the characteristic energy scales in the GHz-to-THz range.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the C-type magnetic configuration and that the low-energy electronic states are well-described by the tight-binding model derived from DFT.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin-resolved electronic bands showing altermagnetic splitting, magnon dispersion along high-symmetry paths (M–$\Gamma$-X–Y–$\Gamma$), and spectral density maps highlighting anisotropic damping.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the electronic structure via DFT/PAW to constructing an effective tight-binding Hamiltonian. It then applies RPA to calculate the magnon spectrum, analyzing the effects of altermagnetism, SOC, and finite lifetime broadening.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the spin excitation in altermagnetic vanadium oxyselenides, focusing on the metallic quasi-two-dimensional compound $\text{KV}_2\text{Se}_2\text{O}$. Using a fermionic Hamiltonian derived from ab initio calculations, we compute the transverse spin susceptibilities within the random phase approximation and extract the magnon dispersion relation. We show that the altermagnetic symmetry leads to characteristic degeneracies and directional splittings in both the electronic bands and the magnon spectra along high-symmetry paths of the Brillouin zone. The metallic $\text{KV}_2\text{Se}_2\text{O}$ exhibits finite magnon linewidths arising from the coupling to the particle-hole continuum. Furthermore, by fixing the magnitude of the wave vector and varying its in-plane direction, we uncover a pronounced angular dependence of the magnon energies and lifetimes, with complementary damping behavior between the two magnon branches. Upon including spin-orbit coupling, the system exhibits an out-of-plane easy c-axis anisotropy, which opens a magnon gap of approximately 6 meV at the $Γ$-point, possibly rendering the magnetic order stable at room temperature. Our results demonstrate that altermagnetism controls not only the dispersion but also the anisotropic decay of spin excitations, highlighting altermagnetic metals as promising platforms for directionally selective magnon transport.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the Spin Hall Magnetoresistance (SMR) effect is a powerful, non-invasive tool for characterizing the spin structure of antiferromagnetic insulators. By measuring resistivity changes under rotating magnetic fields, the authors show that the SMR amplitude scales predictably with the sublattice magnetization, allowing for the study of AF spintronics in materials like $\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$. This provides a robust method for reading out magnetic order in systems previously difficult to probe.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of electrical transport to map out complex, non-collinear magnetic order parameters is relevant to understanding quantum states in engineered materials, bridging classical magnetism with transport physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a simple electrical transport method to probe the spin structure of antiferromagnetic (AF) materials, which lack a net macroscopic magnetization.

**Main result.** The Spin Hall Magnetoresistance (SMR) effect successfully reads out the spin configuration in AF insulators, showing that the SMR amplitude scales with the sum of absolute sublattice magnetizations.

**Method.** Angle-dependent magnetoresistance (ADMR) measurements are performed by measuring the longitudinal and transverse resistivities of a Pt top electrode as an external magnetic field is rotated in-plane.

**Model / system.** The study uses bilayer heterostructures combining easy-plane antiferromagnetic insulators (like $\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ and NiO) with a Pt heavy-metal electrode. The analysis is modeled using two magnetic sublattices ($M_A$ and $M_B$) and comparing results to known ferrimagnetic systems.

**Key observables.** Characteristic sinusoidal modulations in longitudinal and transverse resistivities ($\rho_{long}$ and $\rho_{trans}$), and the SMR amplitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The SMR amplitude scales with the product of the volume density ($n$) of magnetic ions and their spin state ($S$), i.e., $nS$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the SMR effect is sensitive to the spin configuration of *all* independent magnetic sublattices, not just the net magnetization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows in-plane ADMR for $\alpha$-Fe$_2$O$_3$/Pt, NiO/Pt, and Y$_3$Fe$_5$O$_{12}$/Pt bilayers. Figure 2 illustrates the scaling behavior of the maximum SMR.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (probing AF spin structure) by introducing SMR as a tool. It details the measurements on various bilayers, develops a comprehensive model based on two sublattices, and presents results showing the universal scaling of the SMR amplitude with $nS$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Antiferromagnetic materials promise improved performance for spintronic applications, as they are robust against external magnetic field perturbations and allow for faster magnetization dynamics compared to ferromagnets. The direct observation of the antiferromagnetic state, however, is challenging due to the absence of a macroscopic magnetization. We show that the spin Hall magnetoresistance (SMR) is a versatile tool to probe the antiferromagnetic spin structure via simple electrical transport experiments by investigating the easy-plane antiferromagnetic insulators alpha-Fe2O3 (hematite) and NiO in bilayer heterostructures with a Pt heavy-metal top electrode. While rotating an external magnetic field in three orthogonal planes, we record the longitudinal and the transverse resistivities of Pt and observe characteristic resistivity modulations consistent with the SMR effect. We analyze both their amplitude and phase and compare the data to the results from a prototypical collinear ferrimagnetic Y3Fe5O12/Pt bilayer. The observed magnetic field dependence is explained in a comprehensive model, based on two magnetic sublattices and taking into account magnetic field-induced modifications of the domain structure. Our results show that the SMR effect allows to readout the spin configuration and to investigate magnetoelastic effects in antiferromagnetic multi-domain materials. We demonstrate that the SMR amplitude scales with the sum of the absolute sublattice magnetizations in ferrimagnetic and antiferromagnetic materials. In alpha-Fe2O3/Pt bilayers, we find an unexpectedly large SMR amplitude of 2.5E-3, twice as high as for prototype Y3Fe5O12/Pt bilayers, making the system particularly interesting for room-temperature antiferromagnetic spintronic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that magnetic octupole (MO) degrees of freedom can emerge robustly in spin-orbit-entangled multiorbital systems. It establishes the MO Rashba-Edelstein effect, showing that an applied electric field can generate a non-equilibrium MO polarization. This MO response can be dominant and provides a novel, electrically controllable mechanism for manipulating magnetic order in antiferromagnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on multipolar responses (octupoles) and their electrical generation via the Edelstein effect is highly relevant to understanding emergent magnetic order and spin-orbit torque mechanisms in modern spintronics and topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence and electrical generation of magnetic octupole (MO) degrees of freedom in electronic systems, particularly in the context of Rashba-Edelstein effects.

**Main result.** Spin-orbit-entangled multiorbital states can host MO degrees of freedom, leading to an MO Rashba-Edelstein effect that can dominate over conventional spin responses, providing an interface-based route for generating non-equilibrium MO polarization.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using minimal models for $p$-orbitals on a 2D lattice, calculating non-equilibrium polarization via linear response theory (e.g., $\delta\langle D_y angle = \chi_D E_x$).

**Model / system.** The study uses spin-orbit-entangled multiorbital states, modeled by $p_x, p_y, p_z$ orbitals on a 2D triangular or square lattice, incorporating SOC and interface-induced splitting.

**Key observables.** MO Rashba texture, MO Rashba-Edelstein polarization ($\delta\langle O_y angle$), Néel MO torque ($T_{MO}$), and sublattice-resolved susceptibilities ($\chi^D_{A,B}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pure-MO limit, $d$-wave Antiferromagnet (AM) structure, and the relative strength of MO vs. spin responses.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often employs the constant-relaxation-time intraband approximation for non-equilibrium responses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the emergence of MO textures, the pure-MO limit, and the calculation of current-induced torques on the Néel vector, showing MO accumulation dominating spin accumulation in certain bands.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from general concepts of magnetic multipoles in spin-orbit-entangled systems to specific models (triangular/square lattice) demonstrating the MO Rashba texture, and finally applying this to calculate a measurable torque in $d$-wave antiferromagnets.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic multipoles have attracted growing interest as order parameters and dynamical degrees of freedom in unconventional magnets, yet it remains unclear how broadly they can emerge as active electronic degrees of freedom. Here, we show that spin-orbit-entangled multiorbital states can host magnetic octupole (MO) degrees of freedom. In Rashba systems, this gives rise to an MO Rashba texture accompanying the J-Rashba texture. Remarkably, spin-orbital entanglement can realize a pure-MO limit in which the spin and orbital-angular-momentum textures vanish while the MO texture remains finite. An applied electric field converts this texture into a nonequilibrium MO polarization through an MO Rashba-Edelstein effect. Around the pure-MO regime, the MO Edelstein response dominates over the conventional spin response, showing that multipolar responses need not be small corrections to dipolar spin physics. Our results establish an interface-based route for electrically generating nonequilibrium multipolar magnetic polarization in spin-orbit-coupled systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates heat transfer in a dense gas by comparing the standard Enskog equation with a modified version that includes a new factor. Numerical simulations show that while the modification has little effect on bulk properties like temperature, it causes a measurable difference in the stress tensor, especially under high confinement. This highlights the importance of subtle theoretical modifications in accurately modeling non-equilibrium transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical gas dynamics, the rigorous comparison of kinetic equations and the establishment of theorems (like H theorem) are fundamental techniques applicable to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics problems in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares the heat transfer in a dense gas using two variants of the Enskog equation: the original and a modified version incorporating a new factor.

**Main result.** The modification has a limited influence on temperature, density, and heat flow, but a visible difference appears in the stress tensor, particularly in highly confined gas regimes.

**Method.** The authors performed a numerical study of the time-dependent heat transfer problem by solving the kinetic equation using finite-difference and spectral methods.

**Model / system.** The system is a dense gas confined between two parallel plates, modeled using the Enskog equation framework for hard spheres.

**Key observables.** Temperature (T), density (rho), heat flow vector (q_1), and the stress tensor (p_ij).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Confinement ratio (sigma/L), Knudsen number (Kn), and the ratio of boundary temperatures (T_+/T_-).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes hard spheres, uses the Carnahan–Starling EoS, and considers one-dimensional behavior in the transverse directions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the time evolution of T and q_1 agreeing between models, while p_11 shows divergence, especially in confined cases. Other figures quantify this difference's dependence on Kn and confinement.

**Paper structure.** The paper sets up the heat transfer problem, compares the OEE and EESM formulations, numerically solves the resulting kinetic equations, and analyzes the resulting macroscopic quantities, focusing on the differences in the stress tensor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Heat transfer in a dense gas between two parallel plates is studied for assessing the quantitative difference of two variants of the Enskog equation, i.e., the original Enskog equation and the Enskog equation with a modified Enskog factor recently proposed in Phys. Rev. E \textbf{111}, 065108 (2025). The main advantage of the latter is that the H theorem, which is lacking in the former, has been established. The influence of the modification is assessed by comparing the macroscopic quantities in details. It is found that the influence of this modification is quite limited in the density, temperature, and heat flow vector. However, the influence on the stress tensor is visible in some severe setting of parameters.

</details>

**Authors:** Sofia Zinzani, Gilberto Nardi, Giacomo Becatti, Davide Alimonti, Letícia F. Basso, Kevin Rossi, Francesca Baletto

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'pySNOW', a comprehensive Python software suite for computational materials science, specifically targeting the analysis of nanoparticles and nanoalloys. It allows researchers to extract detailed structural, chemical, and catalytic information from atomistic simulation trajectories. This tool aims to streamline the process of understanding and rationally designing novel nanomaterials in silico.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter structure, the development of a highly integrated, general-purpose simulation analysis toolkit like pySNOW is valuable for any field requiring complex data processing from atomistic simulations, including those in quantum many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing robust computational tools to characterize the complex structure-property relationships in nanoworld materials like nanoparticles and nanoalloys.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'pySNOW', a comprehensive, modular Python suite designed to perform detailed morphological, chemical, and catalytic analysis on atomistic simulation data.

**Method.** The suite processes standard XYZ format input files to calculate numerous structural descriptors, connect to various simulation engines (MD, MC), and model catalytic processes like the Oxygen Reduction Reaction (ORR).

**Model / system.** The focus is on metallic nanoparticles and nanoalloys, analyzed using atomistic simulations. The tool supports analyzing structures derived from classical or ab initio molecular dynamics simulations.

**Key observables.** Coordination numbers (CNs), CNA patterns, PDDF, RDF, layer-by-layer density, shape descriptors (inertia/gyration tensors), Steinhardt parameters, SAXS spectra, and electrochemical activities (current/mass).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is applicable across various conditions, including those relevant to nanoparticle coalescence and catalytic reactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary requirement is the availability of simple, human-readable XYZ coordinate or trajectory input files.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the complete pySNOW workflow, showing the pipeline from reading input data to performing various analyses and modifications.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the modular architecture of pySNOW, covering input/output handling, structural descriptor calculation, advanced analysis modules for catalysis, and utility functions, while comparing its scope against existing tools like MDTraj and pySCAL.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In computational materials science, numerical simulations are indispensable tools for revealing atomic-scale processes. For building blocks of the nanoworld, such as nanoparticles and nanoalloys, atomistic simulations provide detailed insight into their behaviour under diverse conditions. These simulations enable the study of formation and growth mechanisms, transport phenomena, chemophysical stability, and chemical reactions, including catalytic processes. To unravel the complex structure--property relationships that characterise nanoobjects, it is crucial to develop robust and insightful representations of their atomistic structure at global and local scales. Such descriptions enhance our understanding of nanoparticle behaviour and play a key role in guiding their rational design in silico for targeted applications.

</details>

(b) After the torus is cut open, the two bases |u0⟩on τ and |u1⟩on Pτ are glued along the boundaries by these transition functions. </figure>

(a) A single winding with both endpoints pinned at θ = 0. (b) A single winding with both endpoints pinned at θ = π. (c) The ref- erence spectral flow with the two endpoints pinned at θ = 0 and θ = π, respectively. (d) A single winding relative to the reference spectral flow in (c).</figcaption>

The spectral flow over the half-zone ex- hibits a single crossing at θ = π, yielding η = 1. (b) Bulk spectrum at the phase transition for t = 1.75 and (m1, m2, m3, m4) = (0.25, 0.25, 0.2, 0.2). (c) The spectrum at t = 1 on a slab geometry with open boundary condi- tions along the y direction. (d) Spectrum of a finite square at t = 1, with symmetry-preserving boundary perturbations (m1, m2, m3, m4) = (0.25, 0.25, 0.2, −0.2). The four in-gap states are localized at corners, as illustrated by the DOS in- set.</figcaption>

</div>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that the K-theoretic classification of topological phases is insufficient for systems with P and T symmetries. The authors introduce a new topological invariant, $\eta$, derived from analyzing the symmetry-constrained path space. This invariant reveals distinct topological phases that are invisible to standard K-theory diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical extension to topological classification beyond standard K-theory, which is crucial for understanding novel topological phases in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard K-theoretic framework for classifying topological phases is shown to be incomplete, even for simple spinless systems possessing both P (inversion) and T (time-reversal) symmetries.

**Main result.** The authors identify a new topological invariant ($\eta$) that cannot be determined from the parity spectra at inversion-invariant momenta, revealing crystalline topological phases absent from K-theory.

**Method.** The classification is achieved via a first-principles analysis of the topological classes by analyzing the 'symmetric clutching data' and utilizing Wilson-loop spectral flow methods.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on spinless systems with P and T symmetries, using the Bloch Hamiltonian $H(\mathbf{k})$ and examining its behavior at inversion-invariant momenta ($\Lambda_i$).

**Key observables.** The new $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant ($\eta$), the Stiefel–Whitney classes, and the path-connected components $\pi_0(C_n)$ of the path space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parity spectrum of occupied states at inversion-invariant momenta, and the structure of the fundamental group $\pi_1(O(N))$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a continuous orthonormal basis of occupied states on the fundamental domain $	au$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the division of the Brillouin torus into an inversion fundamental domain ($	au$) and show schematic Wilson-loop spectral flows for the $\eta$ invariant.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework by analyzing path spaces $C_{S_n\Gamma, F}$ to determine the fundamental group $\pi_0(C_n)$, and then applies this to physical systems to derive the new invariant $\eta$ that supplements the K-theoretic classification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The K-theoretic framework provides a complete topological classification of the tenfold symmetry classes and has been generalized to incorporate crystalline symmetries. Here, we show that this classification is incomplete even in the elementary case of spinless systems possessing both P and T symmetries. We obtain the complete classification through a first-principles analysis of the topological classes of P- and T-symmetric bands, namely, by classifying the corresponding symmetric clutching data. We identify a topological invariant that is invisible from the K-theoretic perspective when the occupied states at each inversion-invariant momentum have uniformly positive or uniformly negative parity. In special cases, such as when all inversion-invariant momenta have uniformly positive parity, this invariant can be interpreted as the second Stiefel--Whitney class or the Euler number defined over an inversion fundamental domain, namely, half of the Brillouin zone. Our work not only reveals a new topological invariant that cannot be determined from the parity spectra at inversion-invariant momenta under P and T symmetries, but also demonstrates the existence of crystalline topological phases that are absent from the K-theoretic classification.

</details>

### [From Goldene to Noblene: exhaustive enumeration of the ordered Au-Ag-Cu monolayer alloys](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02709v1)

**Authors:** Marcelo Lopes Pereira Junior  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02709v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This work exhaustively maps the ordered phase space of Au-Ag-Cu monolayers, demonstrating that local atomic arrangement, not just stoichiometry, controls the material's energy. They identify a unique, stable phase called Noblene, which is predicted to be synthesizable because it shares the lattice structure of known gold monolayers. The study provides a detailed methodology for predicting novel 2D quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter materials science, the rigorous application of DFT to map complex, highly ordered, low-dimensional phases provides a powerful template for studying emergent order in quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To exhaustively map the ordered Au-Ag-Cu monolayer alloys and determine that atomic arrangement, rather than simple elemental proportion, dictates the mixing energy in these 2D systems.

**Main result.** The equimolar ordering, named Noblene, is identified as the only dynamically stable and metallic structure where every atom is surrounded exclusively by unlike species, suggesting a plausible synthesis route from Goldene.

**Method.** The study employs Density Functional Theory (DFT) combined with an exhaustive enumeration of derivative superstructures and a pair model fit to calculate mixing energies and elastic constants.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Au-Ag-Cu ternary alloy realized as a two-dimensional monolayer on a close-packed hexagonal lattice. The analysis focuses on the interplay between local atomic ordering and overall chemical composition.

**Key observables.** Mixing energy (meV/atom), Poisson's ratio, elastic constants ($C_{11}, C_{22}, C_{12}, C_{66}$), and electronic structure (PDOS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The competition between Au-Cu contacts (binding) and Ag-Cu contacts (non-binding) governs the energetics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to two dimensions (in-plane) and uses DFT calculations based on the PBE functional.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four reference monolayers (Au, Ag, Cu, and Noblene) on the close-packed lattice, and show the mixing energy landscape for various supercell stoichiometries.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically enumerates superstructures, calculates their energetics via DFT, establishes that local ordering dominates composition, and finally characterizes the unique, stable Noblene phase using electronic and elastic properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional metals became a laboratory reality with the isolation of Goldene, a gold sheet one atom thick released by chemical exfoliation, which raises the question of what alloying can achieve on the same close-packed lattice. Here we combine an exhaustive enumeration of derivative superstructures with density functional theory to map the ordered Au-Ag-Cu monolayer alloys, relaxing every symmetry-inequivalent arrangement up to four atoms per cell and extending the set with larger cells selected by a fitted model. We find that the arrangement of the atoms, and not their proportion, controls the mixing energy, since the spread among the orderings of a single composition is several times the step between neighboring compositions, one composition spans more than 50~meV/atom between two arrangements of the same three atoms, and at two compositions the arrangement decides the sign. The energetics is carried by a competition between Au-Cu contacts, which bind, and Ag-Cu contacts, which do not, and bulk enthalpies computed under the same protocol show that this chemistry is not uniformly rescaled in two dimensions, since Au-Cu orders more strongly in the monolayer than in the crystal while the other two weaken. A pair model completed by an elastic size-mismatch term reproduces the computed energies to a few meV/atom and returns a composition landscape whose convex hull no balanced ternary composition reaches. One equimolar ordering, which we name Noblene, is the only structure in which every atom is surrounded exclusively by unlike species, and it is dynamically stable and metallic, and carries a Poisson's ratio above one half, higher than that of any of the three pure monolayers. Since Noblene sits on the lattice that Goldene already realizes, the route that produced Goldene is a plausible starting point for its synthesis.

</details>
